Concentrating Local Solutions of the Two-Spinor Seiberg-Witten Equations on 3-Manifolds
Gregory J. Parker

Abstract
Given a compact 3-manifold Y Y and a ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -harmonic spinor ( 𝒵 0 , A 0 , Φ 0 ) (\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0}) with singular set 𝒵 0 \mathcal{Z}_{0} , this article constructs a family of local solutions to the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations parameterized by ε ∈ ( 0 , ε 0 ) \varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}) on tubular neighborhoods of 𝒵 0 \mathcal{Z}_{0} . These solutions concentrate in the sense that the L 2 L^{2} -norm of the curvature near 𝒵 0 \mathcal{Z}_{0} diverges as ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , and after renormalization they converge locally to the original ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -harmonic spinor. In a sequel to this article, these model solutions are used in a gluing construction showing that any ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -harmonic spinor satisfying some mild assumptions arises as the limit of a family of two-spinor Seiberg-Witten solutions on Y Y .



1
Introduction

Equations of generalized Seiberg-Witten type are conjectured to have deep connections to the geometry and topology of manifolds. Examples include the Vafa-Witten equations
[
38
,
30
,
29
]
and the Kapustin-Witten equations
[
15
,
41
,
40
]
, which are predicted to connect 3 and 4-dimensional topology to other areas. Another example is the ADHM Seiberg-Witten equations, which are expected to play a key role in Donaldson-Segal’s program to construct invariants of manifolds with special holonomy in dimensions 6,7, and 8
[
6
,
3
,
10
]
. In contrast to the standard Seiberg-Witten (SW) equations, the moduli spaces of solutions to generalized Seiberg-Witten equations may not be compact, and the lack of well-understood compactifications is one of the main barriers in the study of these equations.

Pioneering work of Taubes
[
31
,
33
,
36
,
37
,
35
,
34
]
, Haydys-Walpuski
[
13
]
, and Walpuski-Zhang
[
39
]
has shown that sequences of solutions to generalized SW equations on a manifold
Y
can diverge, but after renormalization must converge to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors
or more general types of
Fueter sections
—solutions of a different, in general non-linear PDE on
Y-\mathcal{Z}
where
\mathcal{Z}
is a codimension 2 singular set. On a compact Riemannian 3-manifold
(Y,g_{0})
, the first of these is defined for our purposes as follows. Given an embedded submanifold
\mathcal{Z}\subset Y
of dimension 1 and a spinor bundle
S_{0}\to Y
, fix a real line bundle
\ell\to Y-\mathcal{Z}
equipped with its unique flat connection
A_{0}
with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
consists of a triple
(\mathcal{Z},A_{0},\Phi)
where
\Phi\in\Gamma(S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell)
is a spinor satisfying

\not{D}_{A_{0}}\Phi=0\ \ \text{ on }\ \ Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}\hskip 42.67912pt\text{and}\hskip 42.67912pt\int_{Y-\mathcal{Z}}|\nabla_{A_{0}}\Phi|^{2}<\infty,

(1.1)

where
\not{D}_{A_{0}}
denotes the Dirac operator twisted by the connection
A_{0}
on
\ell
. Said more simply, a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor is a harmonic spinor on the open manifold
Y-\mathcal{Z}
in a spin structure which does not necessarily extend over
\mathcal{Z}
and whose covariant derivative is
L^{2}
.

The above convergence results suggest that the moduli spaces of solutions to a specific generalized Seiberg-Witten equation should admit natural compactifications obtained by including
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors or, more generally, the type of Fueter section arising for that equation as boundary strata. Constructing these compactifications requires addressing the converse to the convergence question:

Question 1.1
.

which
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors or Fueter sections arise as the limit of a sequence of Seiberg-Witten solutions
?

In terms of PDE, this question is a gluing problem, requiring the patching together of solutions of the two different equations. When the singular set
\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
is empty, this gluing problem was solved in a general setting by Doan-Walpuski
[
4
]
, though in this case the “gluing” is rather trivial because no model solutions are required. Indeed, they instead refer to this case as a “deformation” problem. In the situation that
\mathcal{Z}\neq\emptyset
, which is a stable condition under perturbations
[
5
,
28
,
25
]
, the gluing problem requires model solutions near the singular set.

The present work provides two crucial steps towards solving the gluing problem in the case of the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations on a closed 3-manfiold. In this case, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors that arise are as defined in (
1.1
). Given a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0})
satisfying some mild assumptions, the first step accomplished in this article is to construct a 1-parameter family of model solutions to the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations in a neighborhood of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
which converge locally to
(A_{0},\Phi_{0})
. The second step is to analyze the linearized equations at this family of model solutions whose limiting linearization at
(A_{0},\Phi_{0})
is a degenerate elliptic operator whose symbol vanishes along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.
In a sequel to this article
[
26
]
, these model solutions are used to solve the gluing problem and answer Question
1.1
in this setting.

1.1
Main Results

To state the main results, let us first describe the set-up briefly. Additional details are given in Section
2
. Let
(Y,g_{0})
denote a closed, oriented Riemannian 3-manifold, and let
S\to Y
be the spinor bundle associated to a
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure. Furthermore, let
E\to Y
be a rank 2 complex vector bundle with structure group
SU(2)
bundle endowed with a fixed connection
B_{0}
. The
two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations
are the following system of equations for a pair
(\Psi,A)\in\Gamma(S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E)\times\mathcal{A}_{U(1)}
of an
E
-valued spinor and a
U(1)
-connection lifted from
\text{det}(S)
:

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(1.2)

\displaystyle\star F_{A}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(\Psi,\Psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(1.3)

where
\not{D}_{A}
is the Dirac operator on
S\otimes E
twisted by
A
and the fixed connection
B_{0}
on
E
,
F_{A}
is the curvature of
A
, and
\mu
a point-wise quadratic map. The equations are invariant under
U(1)
-gauge transformations.

As mentioned above, there may be sequences
(\Psi_{i},A_{i})
of solutions to (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) that have no convergent sub-sequences modulo gauge. For such sequences, the
L^{2}
norm
\|\Psi_{i}\|_{L^{2}}\to\infty
must diverge. To highlight the role of the
L^{2}
norm for such sequences, one can renormalize the spinor by setting

\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}}\hskip 85.35826pt\Phi:=\varepsilon\Psi

and instead consider the equations (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) for the pair
(\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon},A)
. The results of Haydys-Walpuski in
[
13
]
show that if the sequence
(\Psi_{i},A_{i})
has no subsequences for which
\|\Psi_{i}\|
remains bounded, then the renormalized sequence
(\Phi_{i},A_{i})
converges subsequentially to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor as
\varepsilon\to 0
modulo gauge transformations on the complement of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}
(see Theorem
2.3
in Section
2.1
for a precise statement). At present, it is not known that the singular set of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor arising in this way necessarily has more regularity than being a closed, rectifiable subset of Hausdorff codimension 2. We do not attempt to address these regularity issues here.

Reversing the convergence statement to address the gluing question, let
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0})
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor on
(Y,g_{0})
with respect to a perturbation induced by
B_{0}
. We assume that it satisfies the following.

Assumption 1
.

(Smoothness) the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq Y
is a smooth, embedded link, and
A_{0}
does not extend smoothly across any component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

Assumption 2
.

(Non-degeneracy)
the spinor
\Phi_{0}
has non-vanishing leading-order, i.e. there is a constant
c_{1}
such that

|\Phi_{0}|\geq c_{1}\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{1/2}.

Assumption 3
.

(Isolated)

\Phi_{0}
is the unique
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor with respect to
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},g_{0},B_{0})
up to scaling.

The main result is the following construction of model solutions:

Theorem 1.2
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0})
as in (
1.1
) satisfying Assumptions
1
–
3
and an orientation on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, there exists a
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure
S
on
Y
such that the following hold.

(i)

S
extends
S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell
to
Y
in the sense that
S|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\simeq S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell
, and is determined by a complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}\to Y
such that

S=S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{L}\hskip 42.67912pt\text{ and }\hskip 42.67912ptc_{1}(\det S)=-\text{PD}[\mathcal{Z}_{0}].

Additionally,
\Phi_{0}
is naturally a section of a rank 4 real subbundle of
(S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E)|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
.

(ii)

For this
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure
S
, there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there exist model solutions
(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon})
on the tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
of radius
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{1/2}
satisfying the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A_{\varepsilon}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(1.4)

\displaystyle\star F_{A_{\varepsilon}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mu(\Phi_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(1.5)

for spinors in
S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E
.

(iii)

\tfrac{\Phi_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}
extends via a cut-off function to a smooth section of
S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E
on the closed manifold
Y
that is equal to
\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}
away from
N_{\lambda/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and has
L^{2}
norm
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}+O(\varepsilon^{-1/4})
on
Y
.

The second main result shows that these model solutions approach
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. This follows from applying the main results of
[
24
]
to the model solutions constructed in Theorem
1.2
.

Corollary 1.3
.

The model solutions
(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon})
converge to
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
in the following sense. Fix a family of compact subsets
K_{\varepsilon}\Subset N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
such that
\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z}_{0})\geq c_{1}\varepsilon^{2/3}
for a positive constants
c_{1}
. Then there are constants
C,c
independent of
\varepsilon
such that the un-renormalized difference

(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})=\left(\frac{\Phi_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon}\right)-\left(\frac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon},A_{0}\right)

(1.6)

obeys the following properties.

(i)

There is a half-dimensional subbundle
S^{\text{Im}}\subseteq S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E
such that the components of the spinor in
S^{\text{Im}}
and the connection decay to
(\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon},A_{0})
exponentially on
K_{\varepsilon}
. That is,

\|(\varphi^{\text{Im}}_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})\|_{C^{0}(K_{\varepsilon})}\ \leq\ \frac{C}{|\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z})|^{3/2}\varepsilon}\ \text{Exp}\left(-\frac{c}{\varepsilon}\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z})^{3/2}\right).

(ii)

The remaining spinor components decay to
(\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon},A_{0})
like
\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{-\nu}
for any
0<\nu<\frac{1}{4}
; i.e. there is an
\varepsilon
-independent constant
\gamma^{\prime}<<1
such that

\|\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\nu}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1,2}(K_{\varepsilon})}\ \leq\ C_{\nu}\,\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma^{\prime}}.

Note that
\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is a function on
Y
, whereas
\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z})=\inf_{y\in K_{\varepsilon}}\text{dist}(y,\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon
. In particular, if
\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z}_{0})\geq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma_{1}}
for some
\gamma_{1}>0
, then
(\varphi^{\text{Im}}_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})
decays to 0 faster than any polynomial on
K_{\varepsilon}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The third and final main result is about the linearization of the Seiberg-Witten equations at the model solutions. Since in the eventual gluing construction these model solutions are pasted onto the manifold using a cut-off function to form global approximate solutions on
Y
, the statement of the theorem is given for these. Let
\chi(r)
denote a cut-off function supported on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
equal to 1 for radii
r\leq\lambda/2
. The
Approximate solutions
are defined as

\left(\frac{\Phi^{\text{\text{App}}}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon},A^{\text{\text{App}}}_{\varepsilon}\right):=\left(\frac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon},A_{0}\right)+\chi(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon}),

(1.7)

where
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})
is the un-renormalized difference defined in
1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
denote the “extended gauge-fixed” Seiberg-Witten equations at these approximate solutions, which are defined precisely in Section
2
. This operator is viewed as a first-order boundary value problem on a tubular neighborhood
N_{2\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
(with twice the radius of the one in Theorem
1.2
) by introducing a Hilbert space
H
and a projection

\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}:L^{1,2}(N_{2\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\to H

(1.8)

so that
\ker(\Pi^{\mathcal{L}})
is the subspace of sections satisfying certain boundary and orthogonality conditions. The precise definitions of
H
and
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
are given in Section
7
. The statement also references certain weighted norms
\|-\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}
and
\|-\|_{L^{2}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}
defined in Section
5
; these are equivalent (not uniformly) to the standard norms on
L^{1,2}(N_{2\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))
and
L^{2}(N_{2\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))
respectively.

Theorem 1.4
.

Subject to the boundary and orthogonality conditions defined by
1.8
, the extended gauge-fixed linearization of the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations at the approximate solutions
1.7

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}:\ker(\Pi^{\mathcal{L}})\subseteq L^{1,2}(N_{2\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\longrightarrow L^{2}(N_{2\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

(1.9)

is Fredholm of Index 0. Additionally, there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is invertible, and there are positive constants
C,\gamma^{\text{in}}\!<\!<1
independent of
\varepsilon
such that the bound

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq\frac{C_{\nu}}{\varepsilon^{1/12+\gamma^{\text{in}}}}\ \|\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

(1.10)

holds.

Remark 1.5
.

The model solutions constructed in part (ii) of Theorem
1.2
will sometimes be referred to as “fiducial solutions”. This terminology is taken from
[
19
]
where fiducial solutions of a similar nature were found for Hitchin’s equations (see Section
1.2
and Section
4
for further discussion). The model solutions of Theorem
1.2
actually solve the
extended
Seiberg-Witten equations as defined in Section 2.4. That is, they include an auxiliary 0-form component
a_{0}
.

Remark 1.6
.

Assumptions
2
–
3
can be shown to be open conditions (see
[
14
]
), though we do not prove such a statement here. It is conjectured that Assumption
1
also holds generically within the set of
(g_{0},B_{0})
that admit
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors. The genericity of the embedding condition is the subject of ongoing work by other authors
[
12
]
. This and other questions on the regularity of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
involve significant detours into geometric measure theory (see
[
34
,
43
,
11
]
) and are beyond the scope of the present article, thus we choose to impose Assumption
1
. This assumption could readily be weakened (e.g.
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is an
L^{k,2}
-embedding for
k=3
), but the required analysis would distract the main goals of the present article.

Remark 1.7
.

Notice that the size of the tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
on which the model solutions exist shrinks as
\varepsilon^{1/2}
. This is perhaps displeasing, but is unavoidable. The choice of a power of
\varepsilon
plays a crucial role in the proofs of Theorems
1.2
and
1.4
; in particular it has a nuanced role in the definition of
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
and the proof of the bound
1.10
. Additionally, the lack of a uniform bound in Theorem
1.4
is not merely a shortcoming of the methods: explicit counterexamples to
1.10
without the power of
\varepsilon
are constructed during the proof.

1.2
Motivation for Approach

This section briefly motivates and summarizes the approach taken to the proofs of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.4
.

1.2.1. Degenerating Linearizations

The gluing problem for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors does not fit into the standard framework used in many other gluing problems. These differences are due to the existence of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, near which the equations degenerate and standard elliptic theory breaks down. Indeed, under the assumption that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\emptyset
, Doan-Walpuski
[
4
]
solved the gluing problem in great generality using standard elliptic theory. Unfortunately, none of their approach extends to the case that
\mathcal{Z}\neq\emptyset
.

To be more precise, the standard elliptic theory breaks down in the following way. The linearized Seiberg-Witten equations

\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi_{0},A_{0})}:L^{1,2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0})\longrightarrow L^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0})

(1.11)

at a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor with
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\neq\emptyset
are a degenerate elliptic system whose symbol vanishes along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Operators with this type of degeneracy are known as
elliptic “edge” operators
, and are well-studied in microlocal analysis
[
27
,
20
,
21
,
8
]
. For the edge operator
\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi_{0},A_{0})}
, there is no natural choice of function spaces on which it is Fredholm; in particular, (
1.11
) has an infinite-dimensional cokernel. For any family of model solutions
(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon})
, the resulting family of linearized equations

\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon})}\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi_{0},A_{0})}

(1.12)

is converging to this limiting operator with inifinite-dimensional cokernel (in no precise sense, as the function spaces change in the limit). As a result, one
cannot
expect the linearizations to be uniformly invertible in any reasonable sense.

The consequences of this are two-fold. In the present article, this manifests in the difficulty of proving Theorem
1.4
, where the subspace limiting to the infinite-dimensional cokernel ruins any naive approach. The proof unavoidably requires delicate analysis of the degenerating family (
1.12
), which is carried out in Sections
6
-
7
. The second consequence is for the eventual gluing: even with Theorem
1.4
in hand, the gluing problem still appears at first to have an infinite-dimensional obstruction coming from the cokernel of
1.11
. This is addressed in
[
26
]
by considering deformations of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, which requires the study of the infinite-dimensional family of operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{Z})
parameterized by nearby singular sets
\mathcal{Z}
, though no more is said about this issue here.

1.2.2. Relation to Limiting Configurations

The gluing problem for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors is effectively a generalization of the gluing problem that arises at the boundary of the moduli space of solutions to Hitchin’s equations on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, and this observation guides parts of our approach.

The boundary objects in the Hitchin moduli space, known as
limiting configurations
, are singular Higgs fields whose singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{\Sigma}\subset\Sigma
is a finite collection of points. Given a limiting configuration
\Phi_{0}
and a singular point
z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\Sigma}
, one makes the ansatz that there are local model solutions which differ from
\Phi_{0}
by a complex-gauge transformation
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
which depends only on the distance
r
from
z
. That is, are locally of the form

\Phi_{\varepsilon}=e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}\cdot\Phi_{0}.

(1.13)

This leads to an
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of ODEs for
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
that can be solved to yield model solutions. These are then spliced onto
\Sigma
and corrected to true solutions using methods that exploit the
holomorphic structure of Hitchin’s equations to circumvent the problem of the degenerating linearization (see
[
19
,
7
]
for details).

The relation of this case with the gluing problem for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors is, essentially, that it is a dimensional reduction. More precisely, the gluing problem in the case of Hitchin’s equations is the dimensional reduction of the gluing problem for the closely related (though more difficult) Kapustin-Witten equations. For a 3-manifold
Y=S^{1}\times\Sigma
, the limiting configurations at the boundary of the Hitchin moduli space on
\Sigma
can be lifted to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic 1-forms (which are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors for the Dirac-type operator
(d+d^{\star})
) that are invariant in the
S^{1}
direction. The singular set is lifted to
\mathcal{Z}=S^{1}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\Sigma}
. Up to some minor differences between the equations, the three-dimensional gluing problem for the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations can be viewed as a generalizing of the construction for Hitchin’s equation to the non-
S^{1}
-invariant case.

Unfortunately, for the case of a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-harmonic spinor on a general 3-manifold
Y
, the lack of a holomorphic structure on
Y
means virtually none of the techniques used for Hitchin’s equations are applicable. First, there is no analogue of the holomorphic structure which can be exploited to circumvent the problem of the degenerating family of linearized operators, and this problem must be confronted. Even disregarding this issue with the linearization, there are several critical issues in extending the 2-dimensional approach to find 3-dimensional model solutions. For one, the holomorphic structure on
\Sigma
allows one to choose local coordinates putting
\Phi_{0}
in a standard form, thus the 2-dimensional model solution is unique up to coordinate change. In contrast, in three-dimensions the local form of
\Phi_{0}
lies in an infinite-dimensional space of possibilities. Secondly, one cannot make an effective simplifying ansatz akin to
1.13
. In addition to having to upgrade the ODE for
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
to a PDE for
h_{\varepsilon}(r,t)
depending also on
t
the tangential coordinate
\mathcal{Z}
, the lack of a holomorphic structure, means the number of equations also increases. In combination, these features mean there is no analogue of the ansatz
1.13
that will lead to a system of PDEs near
\mathcal{Z}
that is meaningfully simpler than the full Seiberg-Witten equations.

Despite these differences, our approach still relies heavily on a very close analogue of the two-dimensional model ODE solutions, as we now explain.

1.2.3. Our Approach

Given the above, one must abandon the hope of finding explicit model solutions and instead turn to abstract methods. One reliable abstract method is the Implicit Function Theorem (IFT), and in fact, as explained momentarily, any other method would be redundant. Our use of the IFT here relies on the following observation: although the solution to the local PDE near the singular set cannot be found explicitly, its leading order term must be given by the
t
-parameterized family of 2-dimensional model solutions on the normal planes. These are not solutions, and in fact the error from being a solution does not approach 0 in
L^{2}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Yet, surprisingly, it is sufficiently small that with the correctly weighted function spaces the IFT can correct these to true model solutions. Of course, applying the IFT requires analyzing the linearization at these, which has the same shortcoming as described above: this family of linearizations degenerates to an operator which is not Fredholm.

Thus our approach produces model solutions in two steps. The first is to first introduce a
t
-parameterized family of 2-dimensional model solutions which smoothes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor to a “de-singularized” pair
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
. After this, an application of the IFT corrects them to the desired 3-dimensional model solutions, proving Theorem
1.2
. With these model solutions in hand, one forms approximate solutions on the closed manifold by introducing a cut-off function, and the global gluing argument proceeds from there. Schematically, the steps of the gluing are

(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
(\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{\text{mod}},A_{\varepsilon}^{\text{mod}})
(\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{\text{app}},A_{\varepsilon}^{\text{app}})
(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon}).
de-sing.
correct
cutoff
\begin{matrix}\text{gluing}\\
\text{iteration}\end{matrix}

where the first two steps are accomplished in the present work, and the last two relegated to the sequel
[
26
]
as explained in the introduction.

A key advantage of this approach is that it proves Theorems
1.2
and
1.4
simultaneously. The proofs both rely on the study of the degenerating family of linearizations at the de-singularized pair
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
. This study extends across Sections
4
–
7
and culminates in Theorem
7.1
, which describes the invertibility of this family of operators. Theorems
1.2
and
1.4
then follow immediately from Theorem
7.1
. While other approaches to Theorem 1.2 (such as finding more explicit local solutions) might be possible, any such approach would be redundant, since Theorem
7.1
is needed anyway to establish Theorem
1.4
.

1.3
Outline

Section 2 introduces background material and provides an overlay of technical statements on what was said in the introduction. Section 2.1 gives the precise statement of the convergence theorem of Haydys-Walpuski. Sections 2.2 give relevant linear algebra constructions, and Section 2.3 gives a more precise definition of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors which arises from a version of the Haydys correspondence. Section 2.4 states the Weitzenböck formula for the linearized equations, which is used later.

Section 3 covers some basic properties of the singular Dirac operator
1.1
. Section 3.1 covers its semi-Fredholm properties, and Section 3.2 establishes local forms for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors which are of key importance. Relying on these local forms, Section 3.3 is devoted to the topological question of how to reconstruct the
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure in part (i) of Theorem
1.2
.

Section 4 constructs the de-singularized configurations and estimates their failure to be true solutions. Section 4.1 reviews the dimensionally reduced problem, which is essentially identical to the corresponding problem for Hitchin’s equations found in
[
19
]
. Section 4.2 extends these to the parameterized ODE case, and Section 4.3 contains the error calculation.

Section 5 begins the analysis of the linearized equations at the de-singularized configurations. Section 5.1 defines the relevant function spaces. Section 5.2 defines a model operator given by the situation where metric near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is Euclidean. In Section 5.3 it is shown that the
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of model operators on the planes normal to
\mathcal{Z}
are all re-scaling of a single
\varepsilon
-invariant operator
\mathcal{N}
at the invariant scale
O(\varepsilon^{2/3})
.

Section 6 begins the bulk of the technical analysis by studying the scale-invariant normal operator
\mathcal{N}
. This operator can be understood via complex geometry, and viewing it via this lens makes certain properties manifest. In this section, it is found that
\mathcal{N}
naturally has a two (real) dimensional kernel that cannot be perturbed away, despite the fact that the Seiberg-Witten equations on a compact 3-manifold are index 0. This kernel is the first manifestation of the infinite-dimensional cokernel that arises as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Section 6.1 and Section 6.2 provide background and review the relevant standard Fredholm theory. Sections 6.3-6.5 study the normal operator
\mathcal{N}
in its holomorphic guise, and Section 6.6 provides details on the aforementioned two-dimensional kernel.

Section 7 generalizes the results of the previous section to the 3-dimensional case. This follows essentially from integration by parts and the observation that all the tangential derivatives along
\mathcal{Z}
are comparatively mild. In this section, the kernel of the normal operator which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}
is upgraded to a high-dimensional subspace which approaches
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z};\mathbb{C})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
to become the infinite-dimensional cokernel in the limit. To make the integration by parts work involves setting up the quite intricate collection of boundary and projection conditions
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
(cf. Theorem
1.4
) for the linearized operator, which accounts for this section’s length despite the simplicity of the underlying idea. Section 7.1 reviews some standard results about APS boundary conditions for Dirac operators. Section 7.2 discusses the high-dimensional subspace approaching the limiting cokernel, and Section 7.3 sets up the boundary conditions accounting for this. Sections 7.4 and 7.5 then carry out the integration by parts argument, which by that point becomes rather involved. Section 7.6 generalizes to the case of an arbitrary metric near
\mathcal{Z}
.

Section 8 concludes the proofs of Theorems
1.2
and
1.4
, which after the analysis of the linearization in Sections 5-7 are essentially immediate. The Appendices cover some calculations that would disrupt the flow of the rest of the article.

2
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-Harmonic Spinors and Compactness

Let
(Y,g_{0})
be a closed, oriented Riemannian 3-manifold. Choose a
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
Y
, and let
S\to Y
be the associated spinor bundle. We denote Clifford multiplication by
\gamma:\Omega^{1}(Y)\to\text{End}(S)
. Since every 3-manifold is spin, we may alternatively specify a
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure by choosing a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
with spinor bundle
S_{0}
and taking
S=S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{L}
where
\mathcal{L}
is a complex line bundle. In this second description, the
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure obtained depends on the choice of
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
.

The two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations are an extension of the standard Seiberg-Witten equations (
[
22
,
16
]
) that instead consider spinors valued in two (possibly twisted) copies of
S
. Let
E\to Y
denote an
SU(2)
-bundle equipped with a fixed smooth background connection
B_{0}
. Define

S_{E}:=S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E,

and denote by
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle
the real inner-product on sections of
S_{E}
arising from the Hermitian inner-products on
S
and
E
. Pairs
(\Psi,A)\in\Gamma(S_{E})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{L}}
consisting of a spinor in
S_{E}
and a
U(1)
connection on
\mathcal{L}
are called
configurations
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
Two-Spinor Seiberg-Witten Equations
for configurations
(\Psi,A)
are

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(2.1)

\displaystyle\star F_{A}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(\Psi,\Psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(2.2)

where
\not{D}_{A}
is the Dirac operator on
S_{E}
formed using the Spin connection on
S_{0}
, the background connection
B_{0}
on
E
, and the connection
A
on
\mathcal{L}
, and
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu
is a pointwise quadratic
moment map
. These equations are invariant under the action of the
gauge group

\mathcal{G}=\text{Maps}(Y;U(1))
.

The moment map
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu:S_{E}\to\Omega^{1}(i\mathbb{R})
is given in a local orthonormal coframe
e^{j}
by

\frac{1}{2}\mu(\Psi,\Psi)=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{i}{2}\langle\gamma(ie^{j})\Psi,\Psi\rangle e^{j}.

In a local trivialization
E|_{U}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2}\times U
, we may write
\Psi=(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
as a pair of spinors in
S
in which case
\mu(\Psi,\Psi)=\mu_{\circ}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{1})+\mu_{\circ}(\Psi_{2},\Psi_{2})
, where
\mu_{\circ}
is the moment map in the standard Seiberg-Witten equations.

2.1
Compactness Theorem

It is a well-known fact that the moduli space of solutions to the standard Seiberg-Witten equations modulo the action of the gauge group
\mathcal{G}
is compact (
[
22
]
, Chapter 5). The proof of this relies on the pointwise equality

\langle\gamma(\mu(\Psi,\Psi))\Psi,\Psi\rangle=\tfrac{1}{4}|\Psi|^{4},

(2.3)

which via the Weitzenböck formula for
\not{D}_{A}
leads to an
a priori
bound

\|\Psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\int_{Y}|s|\ dV

for the spinor component of solutions, where
s
is the scalar curvature of
g
. Starting with this, the proof of compactness is a standard application of elliptic theory (
[
22
]
, Sections 5.2-5.3).

For the case of the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations (
2.1
)-(
2.2
), there are non-zero spinors for which
\mu(\Psi,\Psi)=0
, thus no bound akin to (
2.3
) can hold. The consequence is that for the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations

\text{\it There may be sequences of solutions $(\Psi_{i},A_{i})$ such that }\|\Psi_{i}\|_{L^{2}}\to\infty.

Note that this
L^{2}
-norm is a gauge-invariant quantity. To understand the behavior of such sequences of solutions, one considers renormalizing by dividing by the
L^{2}
-norm. Equivalently, we “blow-up” the space of configurations by adding the sphere are infinity in
L^{2}(Y;S_{E})
.

Thus consider re-normalized spinors to replace configurations
(\Psi,A)
with
blown-up configurations

(\Phi,A,\varepsilon)
by setting

\Phi=\varepsilon\Psi\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ where }\hskip 28.45274pt\varepsilon=\frac{1}{\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}}.

Definition 2.2
.

The
blown-up Seiberg-Witten Equations
for a blown-up configuration
(\Phi,A,\varepsilon)\in\Gamma(S_{E})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\infty)
are

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A}\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(2.4)

\displaystyle\star\varepsilon^{2}F_{A}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(\Phi,\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

(2.6)

As before, these equations are invariant under the action
\mathcal{G}=\text{Maps}(Y;U(1))
. Solutions with
\varepsilon\neq 0
are solutions of the original equations (
2.1
)-(
2.2
) where
\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}
.

The upcoming theorem, due to Haydys-Walpuski
[
13
]
and building on the work of Taubes in
[
31
]
, describes the limiting behavior of sequences of solutions for which the
L^{2}
-norm diverges. Additional regularity results were proved by Taubes
[
34
]
, and Zhang
[
43
]
; a more general approach to the original result was later given by Walpuski-Zhang in
[
39
]
. The precise statement of the theorem is rather intricate, and it merits preliminary explanation.

One would naively expect that a sequence of solutions
(\Phi_{i},A_{i},\varepsilon_{i})
with
\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
would converge to a solution of (
2.4
)-(
2.6
) with
\varepsilon=0
, i.e. a pair
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
solving

\not{D}_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}=0\ \ \ \ \ \text{ such that }\ \ \ \ \ \Phi_{0}\in\mu^{-1}(0)\ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \|\Phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}}=1.

(2.7)

A version of this statement is true, but there are several caveats.

The first caveat arises from the fact that
\mu^{-1}(0)
is not fiberwise a manifold; instead, it is singular at the point
0\in\mu^{-1}(0)
; it is therefore unclear what it means for
\Phi_{0}
to solve the equation
\not{D}_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}=0
at the singular locus
|\Phi|^{-1}(0)
. The second difficulty is describing the limiting process for the connection, since it no longer appears in the limiting
\varepsilon=0
equations (
2.7
). It turns out that the connection converges to a well-defined limit away from a second singular locus around which the energy density
|F_{A}|^{2}
concentrates and becomes unbounded. The coupling of the equations dictates, however, that this concentration may only occur where the spinor hits the singular point of
\mu^{-1}(0)
and
these two singular loci therefore coincide
. Consequently, the statement of the convergence theorem makes reference to a singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
which plays the dual role of

1.

The set of
y\in Y
for which the limiting spinor hits the singularity, i.e. has
\Phi_{0}(y)=0\in\mu^{-1}(0)
.

2.

The set of
y\in Y
away from which
|F_{A_{i}}|^{2}
remains bounded.

The following theorem makes these ideas precise. The statement given here combines the result of Haydys-Walpuski, Taubes, and Zhang referenced above.

Theorem 2.3
.

(Haydys-Walpuski
[
13
]
, Taubes
[
34
]
, Zhang
[
43
]
)
Let
(\Phi_{i},A_{i},\varepsilon_{i})\in\Gamma(S_{E})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})\times(0,\infty)
denote a sequence of solutions to the blown-up Seiberg-Witten equations

\not{D}_{A_{i}}\Phi_{i}=0\hskip 28.45274pt\star\varepsilon_{i}^{2}F_{A_{i}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(\Phi_{i},\Phi_{i})=0\hskip 28.45274pt\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{2}}=1

with respect to a sequence of converging metrics
g_{i}\to g_{0}
on
Y
and connections
B_{i}\to B_{0}
on
E
. Then, either

(i)

If
\limsup\varepsilon_{i}>0
, then
(\Phi_{i},A_{i},\varepsilon_{i})
converges subsequentially modulo gauge to a solution with
\varepsilon>0
.

OR

(ii)

If
\limsup\varepsilon_{i}=0
, there exists a triple
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0},A_{0})
where

•

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq Y
is a closed rectifiable subset of Haudorff codimension at least 2.

•

\Phi_{0}
is a spinor on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
such that
|\Phi_{0}|
extends as a continuous function to
Y
with
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=|\Phi_{0}|^{-1}(0)
.

•

A_{0}
is a flat connection on
\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,

such that
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
satisfies the
\varepsilon=0
version of the blown-up Seiberg-Witten equations
2.7
on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with respect to the metric
g_{0}
and the connection
B_{0}
on
E
. Furthermore, there is an
\alpha>0
such that and after passing to a subsequence and up to gauge transformations defined on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
,

\Phi_{i}\overset{L^{2,2}_{loc}}{\longrightarrow}\Phi_{0}\hskip 28.45274ptA_{i}\overset{L^{1,2}_{loc}}{\longrightarrow}A_{0}\hskip 28.45274pt|\Phi_{i}|\overset{C^{0,\alpha}}{\longrightarrow}|\Phi_{0}|

(2.8)

where local convergence means on compact subsets of
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

∎

As we will see in the next two subsections, the data of case (ii) is equivalent to that of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor. The main result of
[
24
]
shows that the convergence (
2.8
) in this Theorem is
C^{\infty}_{loc}
on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

Remark 2.4
.

Although
\Phi_{0}
is a section of a bundle of dimension
>2
, solutions of the equations (
2.7
) are topologically constrained and do not behave generically. The stability of a singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
of Hausdorff codimension 2 follows from the main results of
[
5
,
25
,
28
]
.

2.2
The Hyperkähler Quotient

This section explicitly identifies
\mu^{-1}(0)
in fibers of
S_{E}
and gives important linear algebra constructions (see also
[
5
]
, Appendix A).

Consider the vector space

V=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H}

equipped with its real inner product as a model for the fibers of
S_{E}
. It carries a pointwise action of
U(1)
via the first factor, and a Clifford multiplication
\gamma:\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\text{End}(V)
given by

\gamma(dt)=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix}\otimes Id\hskip 28.45274pt\gamma(dx)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes Id\hskip 28.45274pt\gamma(dy)=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes Id.

where
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is given coordinates
(t,x,y)
. A pointwise spinor
\Phi\in V
may be written in the form

\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\beta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\ +\ \begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{2}\\
\beta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

(2.9)

In this form the pointwise moment map is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu(\Phi,\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}(|\beta_{1}|^{2}+|\beta_{2}|^{2}-|\alpha_{1}|^{2}-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})\ dt\ \

(2.10)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\text{Re}(-\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta_{1}-\overline{\alpha}_{2}\beta_{2})\ dx

(2.11)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\text{Im}(-\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta_{1}-\overline{\alpha}_{2}\beta_{2})\ dy.

(2.12)

Notice that the sign convention here differs from many authors since we have written the Seiberg-Witten equations as
\star F_{A}+\mu=0
rather than
\star F_{A}=\mu
. It is easy to check the under the identification
(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{*}=\text{Im}\mathbb{H}
given by
dt\mapsto I,dx\mapsto J,dy\mapsto K
, the map
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu
is indeed the hyperkähler moment map associated to the
U(1)
action, justifying the name.

We can identify
V\simeq\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})
so that
2.9
is written as the matrix

\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}\\
\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(2.13)

Lemma 2.5
.

Under the above isomorphism,

\mu^{-1}(0)\ \simeq\ \text{Cone}(U(2)).

In particular, it is a smooth 5-dimensional manifold away from
0\in V
.

Proof.

In terms of the matrix (
2.13
), the second and third moment map equations (
2.11
)-(
2.12
) show that the columns are orthogonal in the Hermitian metric, and the first equation (
2.10
) requires that the rows have the same norm. Thus the matrix is a possibly 0 multiple of a unitary matrix.
∎

Next, we establish the form of the hyperkähler quotient orbifold
\mu^{-1}(0)/U(1)
. To do this, we construct slices for the
U(1)
action. We will show, in fact, that there is a global slice for the action up to a stabilizer of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
. To begin, each factor of
V=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H}
carries a complex anti-linear involution, denoted by
J:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
j:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
respectively, such that
J^{2}=j^{2}=-1
. Explicitly, these are

J\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\beta}\\
\overline{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 85.35826ptj(q):=qj.

(2.14)

Together these give rise to a real structure
\tau:V\to V
satisfying
\tau^{2}=Id
given by

\tau:=J\otimes j.

We denote by
V^{\text{Re}}
and
V^{\text{Im}}
the
+1
and
-1
eigenspaces of
\tau
respectively. So that

\displaystyle V^{\text{Re}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\psi\otimes 1+J\psi\otimes j\ |\ \psi\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\}

(2.15)

\displaystyle V^{\text{Im}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\psi\otimes 1-J\psi\otimes j\ |\ \psi\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\}.

(2.16)

Lemma 2.6
.

The subspace
V^{\text{Re}}
provides a global slice for the
U(1)
action up to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-stabilizer. That is,

V^{\text{Re}}\subset\mu^{-1}(0)

and each
U(1)
-orbit intersects
V^{\text{Re}}
in two point which differ by multiplication by
-1
. Consequently, the hyperkähler quotient is given by

\mu^{-1}(0)/U(1)\simeq\mathbb{H}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Proof.

A pointwise spinor
\Phi\in V^{\text{Re}}
has the form

\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\beta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\beta}_{1}\\
\overline{\alpha}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

(2.17)

which automatically satisfies (
2.10
)-(
2.12
). Moreover, if
e^{i\phi}\Phi\in V^{\text{Re}}
is another element in the same
U(1)
orbit in
V^{\text{Re}}
it must also be of the form (
2.17
). Since

e^{i\phi}\Phi=e^{i\phi}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\beta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+e^{i\phi}\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\beta}_{1}\\
\overline{\alpha}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\phi}\alpha_{1}\\
e^{i\phi}\beta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+e^{2i\phi}\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{e^{i\phi}\beta_{1}}\\
\overline{e^{i\phi}\alpha_{1}}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

this implies
e^{2i\phi}=1
hence
e^{i\varphi}=\pm 1
.
∎

To complete this section we note one more simple lemma, which is central to many arguments in this paper. Let

V=V^{\text{Re}}\oplus V^{\text{Im}}

(2.18)

denote the decomposition from (
2.15
)-(
2.16
). The linearization of
\mu
at
\Phi
is given by its polarization, which we denote
\mu(-,\Phi)
. We may extend this to a map
\mu(-,\Phi):V\to(\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{1})(i\mathbb{R})
by redefining

\mu(\Psi,\Phi)\Rightarrow(-i\langle i\Psi,\Phi\rangle\ ,\ \mu(\Psi,\Phi))

(2.19)

where the previous definition now constitutes the 1-form component. Notice polarizing cancels the factor of
\tfrac{1}{2}
. Similarly, we extend Clifford multiplication to
(\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{1})(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by scalar multiplication in the first factor.

Lemma 2.7
.

The following statements hold:

(A)

Clifford multiplication by real and purely imaginary forms

\gamma:\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R})\to\text{End}(V)\hskip 56.9055pt\gamma:\Lambda(i\mathbb{R})\oplus\Lambda^{1}(i\mathbb{R})\to\text{End}(V)

respectively preserve and reverse the splitting (
2.18
).

(B)

If
\Phi\in V^{\text{Re}}
is non-zero, then

V^{\text{Re}}=\{\gamma(b)\Phi\ |\ b\in\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R})\}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptV^{\text{Im}}=\{\gamma(a)\Phi\ |\ a\in\Lambda^{0}(i\mathbb{R})\oplus\Lambda^{1}(i\mathbb{R})\}

(C)

If
\Phi\in V^{\text{Re}}
then

\ker(\mu(-,\Phi))=V^{\text{Im}}

and the reverse for
\Phi\in V^{\text{Im}}
.

Proof.

For (A), simply note that Clifford multiplication by real forms commutes with
J
, hence with
\tau
and preserves the splitting. For purely-imaginary forms, it anti-commutes by the anti-linearity of
J
. This implies (B) since
\gamma(e^{j})\Phi
is orthogonal to
\gamma(e^{k})\Phi
in the real inner product for
j\neq k
ranging over
j,k=0,1,2,3
. (C) follows in turn from (B) since
\mu(-,\Phi)
and
\gamma(-)\Phi
are adjoints.
∎

2.3
The Haydys Gauge

In the
\varepsilon=0
limit of
the blown-up Seiberg-Witten equations (
2.4
)-(
2.6
) the variables are
\Phi_{0}
and
A_{0}
, but
A_{0}
no longer satisfies an elliptic equation. There is a special choice of gauge, however, that effectively eliminates
A_{0}
as a variable. This allows the limiting
\varepsilon=0
equation to be reinterpreted as an equation for only
\Phi_{0}
that is elliptic on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
(though not uniformly so). This gauge is a key part of the Haydys correspondence (
[
9
,
5
]
) in the case that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\emptyset
.

Since the limiting connection
A_{0}
in Theorem
2.3
is flat with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, it follows that

(\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}})^{2}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{C}}

(2.20)

is trivial. Indeed, it carries a flat connection whose holonomy is trivial, this being the one induced by
A_{0}
. It follows also that
\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
admits a reduction of structure group to a real line bundle. More precisely,

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
admits a flat connection
A_{0}
with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then

(A)

The first chern class
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
is 2-torsion.

(B)

There exists a real line bundle
\ell\to Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
such that

\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\simeq\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}.

(C)

The set of gauge equivalence classes of connections
A_{0}
on
\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
is a torsor on the kernel of the integral Bockstein homomorphism

\beta:H^{1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

The short exact sequence
\mathbb{Z}\overset{\times 2}{\to}\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
induces the long exact sequence

\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\overset{\beta}{\longrightarrow}H^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z})\overset{\times 2}{\longrightarrow}H^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\ldots

and
2.20
shows that
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
is in the kernel of
\times 2
, which is (A). Exactness implies
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
the image of a class in
H^{1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. For (B)-(C), note that flat connections with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
up to gauge (on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
) are in one-to-one correspondence with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-valued representations in
Hom(\pi_{1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq H^{1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, thus with real line bundles
\ell
via
w_{1}(\ell)=\text{hol}_{A_{0}}
under this isomorphism.
The complex line bundle whose first chern class is
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}})=\beta(w_{1}(\ell))
is simply
\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}=\ell\otimes\mathbb{C}
(this is the
\check{\text{C}}\text{ech}
description of
\beta
). (B) therefore holds by exactness, and the set of flat
A_{0}
with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-holonomy on a given isomorphism class of complex line bundle is in one-to-one correspondence with the fiber of
\beta
over its first chern class, which gives (C).

∎

Given the above lemma, we may fix an isomorphism

\sigma:\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\ \simeq\ \ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}},

(2.21)

where
\ell
is the real line bundle specified by the holonomy representation of
A_{0}
. Such a choice is only determined up to gauge transformations on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, as composing with a gauge transformation (now thought of as acting on the
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
factor) gives another such choice. A choice of
\sigma
gives a reduction of structure group of the the spinor bundle
S=S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})
from
\text{Spin}^{c}
to
SU(2)
. The auxiliary bundle
E
has structure group
SU(2)
by definition, hence there are global versions of the maps

J:S|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\to S|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\hskip 56.9055ptj:E\to E

(2.22)

from the previous subsection (which depend on the choice of
\sigma
). They are given in local trivializations by the same expressions
2.14
which commute with the action
SU(2)
. It follows that there is a global splitting

S_{E}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}=S^{\text{Re}}\oplus S^{\text{Im}}

(2.23)

which is determined by
\sigma
. Thus this splitting is specified up to gauge transformations on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and the gauge equivalence classes are given by the gauge equivalence classes of
A_{0}
as in Lemma
2.8
.

The following lemma gives the special gauge choice advertised at the beginning of the section.

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0})
is a triple as in Theorem
2.3
. There exists a choice of a gauge
u\in\mathcal{G}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
such that the following equivalent conditions holds:

(1)

After replacing the isomorphism
\sigma
by
u\circ\sigma
,

\sigma:\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\ \simeq\ \ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

sends

A_{0}\ \ \mapsto\nabla^{\text{flat}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\text{d}

(2.24)

where
\nabla^{\text{flat}}
is the unique flat connection with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
\ell
.

(2)

In the splitting
2.23
determined by the new
\sigma
, one has

\Phi_{0}\in\Gamma(S^{\text{Re}}).

This choice of gauge is referred to as the
Haydys gauge
. It is unique up to the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{G}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
.

Proof.

We construct
u
so that
(2)
holds and show this implies
(1)
. Let
\{U_{\alpha}\}
be a finite open cover of
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
obtained by restricting a finite open cover on the compact
Y
to the subspace topology. We may assume that on each
U_{\alpha}
there is a trivialization

g_{\alpha}\times f_{\alpha}:(S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{L}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E)|_{U_{\alpha}}\simeq U_{\alpha}\times(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H})

with transition functions

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}:U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle SU(2)\times\{1\}\times SU(2))

\displaystyle f_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\{1\}\times U(1)\times\{1\}

for
S_{0}\otimes E
and
\mathcal{L}
respectively. We may assume that
g_{\alpha\beta}
are induced by trivializations that extend to
Y
.

Let
\nabla
denote the connection induced by the spin connection and
B
and the product connection
d
on the middle factor. Thus on
U_{\alpha}
each we may write

\nabla_{A_{0}}=\nabla+\gamma(ia_{\alpha})

for a connection form
a_{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{\alpha};\mathbb{R})
. Locally, in each trivialization there is a real structure given by

\tau_{\alpha}:=J\otimes 1\otimes j

where
J,j
are as in
2.14
.
\tau_{\alpha}
do not
a priori
give rise to a global real structure
\tau
as the transition functions
g_{\alpha\beta}f_{\alpha\beta}
do not respect the
SU(2)
structure.

By Lemma
2.6
, we may choose local gauge transformations
u_{\alpha}
on each
U_{\alpha}
unique up to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
factor such that

u_{\alpha}\Phi_{0}|_{U_{\alpha}}\in\text{Re}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H})

(2.25)

since
\Phi_{0}\in\mu^{-1}{(0)}
In the new trivializations
u_{\alpha}f_{\alpha}:\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\to U_{\alpha}\times\mathbb{C}
, the transition functions

f_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}=u_{\alpha}^{-1}u_{\beta}f_{\alpha\beta}

preserve the condition (
2.25
) thus by Lemma
2.6
, we must have that
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}.
Let
\ell
be the real line bundle determined by
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}:U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Since
f_{\alpha\beta}
and
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}
differ by a
\check{\text{C}}\text{ech}
coboundary, we obtain an isomorphsim
\sigma^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\simeq\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}
, and the gauge transformations
u_{\alpha}
patch to form a global gauge transformation
\sigma^{\prime}\circ u=\sigma
. This yields
(2)
. Moreover, in this gauge
J,j
are respected by the transition functions, hence
\tau_{\alpha}=\tau|_{U_{\alpha}}
agrees with the global structure defined by (
2.22
) using the trivialization
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Now we show that in this trivialization
\sigma^{\prime}
, item
(1)
holds. In the local trivialization on each
U_{\alpha}
write
\nabla
to be connection formed from
\nabla^{\text{Spin}}
on
S_{0}
and
B_{0}
on
E
. We claim that in this trivialization,
u_{\alpha}A_{0}=\nabla
, i.e.
u_{\alpha}\cdot(\text{d}+ia_{\alpha})=\text{d}
is the product connection on the
\mathbb{C}
factor. To see this, write the Dirac equation

\not{D}_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}\gamma(e^{j})\nabla_{j}+\gamma(iu_{\alpha}\cdot a_{\alpha})\right)u_{\alpha}\Phi_{0}=0

and
\nabla,\gamma
preserve
\text{Re}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H})
, while
\gamma(ia_{\alpha})
exchanges it with
\text{Im}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H})\otimes\mathbb{C}
by part (A of Lemma
2.7
and the fact that
\nabla
is an
SU(2)\times SU(2)
connection hence respects
J,j
. It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{3}\gamma(e^{j})\nabla_{j}(u_{\alpha}\Phi_{0})

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\text{Re}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H})\otimes\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\gamma(iu_{\alpha}\cdot a_{\alpha})u_{\alpha}\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\text{Im}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H})\otimes\mathbb{C}

must individually vanish, implying
a_{\alpha}=0
since
\Phi_{0}(y)\neq 0
for
y\in Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Thus in the trivializations
f_{\alpha}^{\prime}
,
A_{0}
is the product connection, so globally it patches to the connection
\nabla^{\text{flat}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\text{d}
on
\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}
in the trivialization
\sigma^{\prime}
. This shows
(1)
. In fact, since there is always a unique gauge transformation up to constants so that
(1)
holds, the two statements are equivalent up to constant gauge transformations.

∎

From now on, we fix the association
2.21
to be one of the two determined by the Haydys gauge defined by the previous lemma. This choice subsequently fixes the splitting
2.23
.

Lemma 2.10
.

The splitting
S_{E}=S^{\text{Re}}\oplus S^{\text{Im}}
determined by the Haydys gauge satisfies the following.

(A)

The conclusions of Lemma
2.7
hold globally.

(B)

The splitting is parallel with respect to
\nabla_{A_{0}}
. In particular, the Dirac operator splits as

\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\text{Re}}:\Gamma(S^{\text{Re}})\to\Gamma(S^{\text{Re}})\hskip 85.35826pt\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\text{Im}}:\Gamma(S^{\text{Im}})\to\Gamma(S^{\text{Im}})

Proof.

(A) is immediate from the pointwise version Lemma
2.7
. For (B), note that in the Haydys gauge of Lemma
2.9
, the connection formed from
A_{0}
and the spin connection respects the
SU(2)
structure, hence commutes with
J
. The connection
B_{0}
on
E
is an
SU(2)
connection hence automatically commutes with
j
, thus
\nabla_{A_{0}}
commutes with
\tau=J\otimes j
.
∎

With the above preparation, we may give a more precise definition of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors which refines Definition
1.1
in the introduction.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq Y
be a smooth, embedded link. Fix a real line bundle
\ell\to Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and set
\mathcal{L}_{0}:=\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}
. Denote by
A_{0}
the connection (
2.24
) formed from
\nabla^{\text{flat}}
on
\ell
and the product connection as in (
2.24
). An (unoriented)
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic Spinor
is a triple
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0})
where
\Phi_{0}\in\Gamma(S^{\text{Re}})
satisfies

(i)

\|\Phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}}=1

(ii)

\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\text{Re}}\Phi_{0}=0\hskip 28.45274pt\text{on }\hskip 8.5359ptY-\mathcal{Z}_{0}.

(iii)

|\Phi_{0}|
extends continuously to
Y
with
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=|\Phi_{0}|^{-1}(0)
.

Such triples are considered up to the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
. When
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is equipped with an orientation, the parenthetical descriptor is removed.

Notice that, although we have reached it in a circuitous way, this definition makes no reference to the Seiberg-Witten equations. The bundle
S=S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{L}_{0}\simeq S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell
is simply the spinor bundle associated to another spin structure on
Y-\mathcal{Z}
, which need not extend over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Conversely, given a spinor bundle
S_{1}\to Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
we form
S^{\text{Re}}\subset S_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E
as before. In fact, it is straightforward to show that since an
SU(2)
bundle
E
on a 3-manifold is necessarily trivial, that
S^{\text{Re}}\simeq S_{1}
and the only effect of introducing
E
is a perturbation to the spin Dirac operator of
S_{1}
arising from
B_{0}
. We also remark that the upcoming Proposition
3.7
in Section 3 implies that the continuous extension in item (iii) is equivalent to the integrability condition in
1.1
.

Another key point is that Definition
2.11
makes no reference to a complex line bundle
\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}=\mathcal{L}_{0}
: the information about the isomorphism class of
\mathcal{L}
is lost in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. There are many choices of extensions
\mathcal{L}\to Y
whose restriction to
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, and before beginning any analysis of the gluing question one must first answer the topological question of
which

\text{Spin}^{c}
structure the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor should be glued into. This is addressed in Section
3
in the setting where Assumptions
1
-
3
hold.

Remark 2.12
.

Definition
2.11
makes sense if
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is simply a closed, rectifiable subset of Hausdorff codimension 2. The extension of the definition of an unoriented
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor is trivial; the oriented case requires some geometric measure theory arguments (see
[
11
]
). Note that other authors generally do not assume that the definition includes an orientation of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. The results of
[
11
]
, however, show that when a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor arises as a limit of solutions to the Seiberg-Witten equations it carries a preferred orientation.

2.4
The Weitzenböck Formula

This section derives the Weitzenböck formula for the gauge-fixed Seiberg-Witten equations with two spinors linearized at a possibly singular configuration. This formula is the two-spinor version of the one appearing in
[
32
]
Equation 5.21.

In dimension 3, it is standard (
[
16
]
) to supplement the equations (
2.1
)-(
2.2
) by an auxiliary 0-form field
a_{0}\in\Omega^{0}(i\mathbb{R})
; this extends them to an elliptic system modulo gauge. Extend Clifford multiplication to a map
\gamma:(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1})\to\text{End}(S)
denoted by the same letter. The
extended (Two-Spinor) Seiberg-Witten Equations
for a configuration
(\Psi,A,a_{0})\in\Gamma(S_{E})\times\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})\times\Omega^{0}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
are

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A}\Psi+\gamma(a_{0})\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(2.26)

\displaystyle\star F_{A}-da_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(\Psi,\Psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(2.27)

This system is again invariant under the action of the gauge group
\mathcal{G}
(which acts trivially on
a_{0}
). For irreducible configurations (
\Psi
not identically 0), integration by parts shows that
a_{0}=0
, thus irreducible solutions of (
2.26
)-(
2.27
) are the same as irreducible solutions of the original equations (
2.1
)-(
2.2
). For the purposes of the eventual gluing result, it suffices to only consider irreducible solutions. The
extended blown-up Seiberg-Witten equations
are defined analogously with the addition of the auxiliary
0
-form
a_{0}
and the term
\varepsilon^{2}da_{0}
in the second equation.

Let
(\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon},A)\in C^{\infty}(Y;S_{E})\times(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{L})\otimes\Omega^{0}(i\mathbb{R}))
denote a smooth configuration with
\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}=1
. Here, we have condensed the notation by replacing
A
with
A+a_{0}
. Differentiating a 1-parameter family of nearby configurations
(\frac{\Phi}{\varepsilon},A)+s(\varphi,a)
shows that the linearization of the equations at
(\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon},A)
acting on the variation
(\varphi,a)
is given by

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}SW(\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon}+s\varphi,A+sA)=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A}\varphi+\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon}\vskip 4.26773pt\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi)}{\varepsilon}+(\star d-d)a\end{pmatrix}.

Supplementing the pair
(\Phi,A)
with an auxiliary
0
-form
a_{0}\in\Omega^{0}(i\mathbb{R})
and the gauge-fixing condition

-d^{\star}a-{i\text{Re}\langle i\varphi,\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon}\rangle}=0

extends the linearization to the elliptic system

\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi,A,\varepsilon)}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A}&\gamma(\_)\tfrac{\Phi}{\varepsilon}\vskip 4.26773pt\\
\tfrac{\mu(\_,\Phi)}{\varepsilon}&\mathbb{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\vskip 4.26773pt\\
a\end{pmatrix}\hskip 56.9055pt\text{where}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{d}a=\begin{pmatrix}0&-d^{\star}\\
-d&\star d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}\\
a_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

and
a=(a_{0},a_{1})\in(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1})(i\mathbb{R})
. The moment map is extended as in (
2.19
) to
\mu=(-i\langle i\varphi,\Phi\rangle,\mu^{1})
with
\mu^{1}
being the 1-form components. The reader is cautioned that because of the singular nature of the connection
A_{0}
, the linearization at a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
is not a bounded operator on
L^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0})
.

The Weitzenböck formula is given below. Notice that in this formula something rather miraculous has occurred:
a priori
one would expect the Weitzenböck formula to contain first order terms in
(\varphi,a)
. The fact that these terms cancel is a special property of the Seiberg-Witten equations.

Proposition 2.13
.

(Weitzenbock Formula)
Let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq Y
be a closed subset and
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
a configuration smooth on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Then on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
the operator
\mathcal{L}_{({\Phi_{0}},A_{0},\varepsilon)}
satisfies

\mathcal{L}^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi_{0},A_{0},\varepsilon)}(\varphi,a)=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A_{0}}\not{D}_{A_{0}}\varphi\vskip 2.84544pt\\
\mathbb{d}\mathbb{d}a\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(\mu(\varphi,\Phi_{0}))\Phi_{0}\vskip 2.84544pt\\
\mu(\gamma(a)\Phi_{0},\Phi_{0})\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,a)

where the latter is the off-diagonal zeroth-order term

\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,a)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma(\epsilon\_)\not{D}_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}-2\_\cdot\nabla_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}\\
\epsilon\mu(\_,\not{D}_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0})+2i\langle i\_,\nabla_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}\rangle&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
a\cdot\nabla\Phi_{0}
denotes the contraction of 1-form indices,
2i\langle i\varphi,\nabla\Phi_{0}\rangle
is the contraction of spinor components (yielding a 1-form), and
\epsilon
acts by
(-1)^{k}
on
k
-forms.

Proof.

The operator
\mathcal{L}
is formally self-adjoint. Expanding the expression and abbreviating
\not{D}=\not{D}_{A_{0}}
and
\nabla=\nabla_{A_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi_{0},A_{0},\varepsilon)}(\varphi,a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}&\mathcal{\gamma}(\_)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\_,\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}&\mathbb{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}&\mathcal{\gamma}(\_)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\_,\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}&\mathbb{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}\not{D}\varphi+\tfrac{\gamma(\mu(\varphi,\Phi_{0}))\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\vskip 2.84544pt\\
\mathbb{d}\mathbb{d}+\tfrac{\mu((\gamma(a)\Phi_{0}),\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}(\gamma(a){\Phi_{0}})+\gamma(\mathbb{d}a){\Phi_{0}}\vskip 2.84544pt\\
\mathbb{d}\mu(\varphi,\Phi_{0})+{\mu(\not{D}\varphi,\Phi_{0})}\end{pmatrix}

Next, we use the following identities, which are proved in
[
24
]
:

\displaystyle\not{D}(\gamma(a)\Phi_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\gamma(\mathbb{d}a)\Phi_{0}-\gamma((-1)^{\deg}a)\not{D}\Phi_{0}-2a\cdot\nabla\Phi_{0}

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{d}\mu(\varphi,\Phi_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mu((\not{D}\varphi),\Phi_{0})+(-1)^{\deg}\mu(\varphi,(\not{D}\Phi_{0}))+2i\langle i\varphi\ ,\ \nabla\Phi_{0}\rangle.

(2.29)

Substituting these yields the formula.
∎

In the case that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is empty, i.e. if the configuration
(\Phi,A)
is smooth, integration by parts and the above yields immediately yields the
the following
L^{2}
version of the Weitzenböck formula will be used in later sections. This formula does not apply to the linearization at the singular configuration
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
.

Corollary 2.14
.

If
(\Phi,A)
is a smooth configuration, then
\mathcal{L}_{({\Phi_{0}},A_{0},\varepsilon)}
is formally self-adjoint and

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi,A,\varepsilon)}(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}=\|\not{D}_{A}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\|\mathbb{d}a\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\gamma(a)\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\mu(\varphi,\Phi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle(\varphi,a)\ ,\ \mathfrak{B}(\varphi,a)\rangle

where the inner product is in
L^{2}
and
\mathfrak{B}
is as above. An equivalent statement holds in the case that
(Y,\partial Y)
is a manifold with boundary, up to the addition of a boundary term.

Proof.

The formal self-adjointness follows from the fact that
\mu,\gamma
are fiberwise adjoints. This also implies

\langle b,\mu(\gamma(b)\Phi,\Phi))\rangle=\langle\gamma(b)\Phi,\gamma(b)\Phi\rangle=|b|^{2}|\Phi_{0}|^{2}

for all
b
and

\langle\varphi,\gamma(\mu(\varphi,\Phi)\Phi)\rangle=\langle\mu(\varphi,\Phi),\mu(\varphi,\Phi)\rangle=|\mu(\varphi,\Phi)|^{2}

for all
\Phi
. The expression for the
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
terms follows.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

Notice that this cancellation of the first-order terms does
not
hold for the
\varepsilon
-version of the blown-up Seiberg equations unless
\varepsilon=1
. The unequal renormalization of the spinor and connection components in the blown-up equations disrupts the cancellation. It is for this reason that we prefer to work with the un-renormalized equation wherever possible.

3
The Singular Dirac Operator

Locally near a component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, the Dirac operator
\not{D}_{A_{0}}
takes the form

\not{D}_{A_{0}}=\not{D}\ +\ O\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)

(3.1)

where
\not{D}
is the Dirac operator on
S_{E}
formed using a smooth background connection that extends over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and
r
denotes the distance to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. In particular, the zeroth order term is unbounded on
L^{2}
. An equivalent viewpoint is to consider
r\not{D}_{A_{0}}=r\not{D}+O(1)
in which case the zeroth order term is bounded, but the symbol degenerates along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Elliptic operators of this type are known as
elliptic edge operators
, and have been studied extensively in microlocal analysis dating back to the 1980s. Authoritative sources on similar operators include
[
27
,
20
,
21
,
8
]
and the references therein.

This section gives some necessary results about the singular Dirac operator. The results are stated here without proof, and the reader is referred to
[
12
,
27
,
20
]
for proofs, as well as
[
25
]
which provides more detailed analysis in this particular case. The final subsection uses these to address the topological problem of reconstructing the
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure explained at the end of Section
2
. It is instructive in understanding the operator (
3.1
) to first consider the following example.

Example 3.1
.

Consider
Y=S^{1}\times D^{2}
with coordinates
(t,x,y)
and take
\mathcal{Z}=S^{1}\times\{0\}
. Consider the trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to Y
of rank 2, and let
\ell\to Y-\mathcal{Z}
be the real line bundle that restricts to the mobius bundle on
\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
equipped with its unique flat connection
A_{0}
. The spinor bundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell
is globally trivial (its determinant is trivial and
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
has no 2-torsion here). In fact,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle e^{i\theta/2}\psi

provides an explicit trivialization. Indeed,
e^{i\theta/2}
provides a nowhere-vanishing section of each factor
\underline{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\ell
with the proper monodromy condition. In this trivialization, we may write

\nabla_{A_{0}}=\text{d}+\frac{i}{2}d\theta=\text{d}+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)\hskip 71.13188pt\not{D}_{A_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}&2\partial\\
2\overline{\partial}&-i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{4}\gamma\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)

where
(r,\theta)
are polar coordinates on the
\mathbb{R}^{2}
factor and
z=x+iy
and
z=x-iy
complex ones. After decomposing a spinor in Fourier series

\psi=e^{ik\theta}e^{i\ell t}\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{+}_{k\ell}(r)\\
\psi^{-}_{k\ell}(r)\end{pmatrix}

the Dirac operator becomes a decoupled family of ODEs which may be solved using Bessel functions (see
[
12
,
28
]
). One finds that

\psi_{\ell}^{\text{Euc}}=e^{-i\theta/2}\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}e^{-|\ell|r}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\\
\tfrac{\text{sgn}(\ell)}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.2)

are an
L^{2}
-orthonormalized set of solutions parameterized by
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\neq 0}
.

This example is the local model of the infinite-dimensional cokernel alluded to in the introduction (section
1.2
). Notice that this phenomenon is not an artifact of the non-compactness of
Y
as
r\to\infty
— these solutions concentrate exponentially near
\mathcal{Z}
for large
|\ell|
.

The family of solutions (
3.2
) display the two following key properties:

(1)

\psi_{\ell}^{\text{Euc}}\in L^{2}
but
\nabla_{A_{0}}\psi_{\ell}^{\text{Euc}}\notin L^{2}
, thus these are
not

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors as defined in (
1.1
).

(2)

\psi_{\ell}^{\text{Euc}}
do not extend smoothly across
r=0
; instead they have asymptotic expansions with half-integer powers of
r
.

In this setting there are no
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors because all solutions whose derivative is
L^{2}
along
\mathcal{Z}
are not
L^{2}
as
r\to\infty
. These are the key properties which generalize to the case of a general 3-manifold.

3.1
(Semi)-Fredholm Theory

Returning to the setting of a general closed 3-manifold
(Y,g_{0})
, let
r:Y\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
denote a weight function equal to
\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})
in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and bounded away from it. Consider the weighted Sobolev spaces defined by:

\displaystyle rH^{1}_{e}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};S^{\text{Re}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\varphi\ \ \Big|\ \ \int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\nabla_{A_{0}}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\ dV<\infty\right\}

\displaystyle L^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};S^{\text{Re}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\psi\ \ \Big|\ \ \int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\psi|^{2}\ dV<\infty\right\}.

Here, the subscript
e
stands for “edge”.

The next proposition follows from the general theory of
[
27
]
. It is also proved using elementary methods in Section 2 of
[
25
]
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The operator

\not{D}_{A_{0}}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};S^{\text{Re}})\longrightarrow L^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};S^{\text{Re}}).

is (left) Semi-Fredholm, i.e.

•

\ker(\not{D}_{A_{0}})
is finite-dimensional, and

•

\text{Range}(\not{D}_{A_{0}})
is closed.

∎

Notice that elements
\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}_{A_{0}})
satisfy the integrability condition of Definition
1.1
, thus this space constitutes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors (integrability
|\nabla_{A_{0}}\Phi|^{2}
implies the weighted
L^{2}
-term is finite as well which gives the reverse inclusion). Assumption
3
imposes the requirement that this finite-dimensional space is 1-dimensional and spanned by
\Phi_{0}
.

The next proposition is not explicitly needed, but it is at the heart of the analysis for the question of gluing
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors (recall
1.2
). It states that the cokernel of the singular Dirac operator
\not{D}_{A_{0}}
is a small perturbation of the case of Example
3.1
; in particular, it is infinite-dimensional and concentrates strongly near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. This proposition is proved in Section 4 of
[
25
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

There is a bounded linear isomorphism

\bigoplus_{\pi_{0}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})\ \oplus\ \ker(\not{D}_{A_{0}}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\ \overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\ \text{Coker}(\not{D}_{A_{0}})

where the direct sum is over components of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. It is given by the inclusion on
\ker(\not{D}_{A_{0}}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
, and on the summand corresponding to a component
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
, by the linear extension of

e^{i\ell t}\ \mapsto\ \psi_{j,\ell}^{\text{Re}}\ +\ \xi_{j,\ell}

where

•

\psi_{j,\ell}^{\text{Re}}
is given in a local trivialization of
S_{E}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\underline{\mathbb{H}}}
extending across
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
by

\psi_{j,\ell}^{\text{Re}}=\pi^{\text{Re}}\left(\chi\psi^{\text{Euc}}_{\ell}\otimes 1\right)

where
\psi_{\ell}^{\text{Euc}}
is given by Equation (
3.2
),
\pi^{\text{Re}}
is the projection to
S^{\text{Re}}
and
\chi
is a cut-off function supported on a neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
.

•

\xi_{\ell}
is a perturbation satisfying
\|\xi_{j,\ell}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\tfrac{C}{|\ell|}
.

∎

3.2
Local Forms

Because of the effective degeneracy of the symbol, standard elliptic regularity fails for operators of the form (
3.1
). The proper replacement of elliptic regularity is, as suggested by the form of (
3.2
), the existence of asymptotic expansions near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
that generalize Taylor expansions by allowing non-integral powers. Before writing these local expansions, let us choose local coordinates and express the Dirac operator using these coordinates and an appropriate trivialization.

We endow a tubular neighborhood diffeomorphic to a solid torus
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
of a component
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with local coordinates as follows. Let
\gamma:S^{1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}
denote an arclength parameterization of the component
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
whose length is denoted
|\mathcal{Z}_{j}|
. When
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is oriented, it is assumed that
\gamma
is chosen respecting its orientation. Next, choose a global orthonormal frame
\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
of the pullback
\gamma^{*}N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
of the normal bundle to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. We require that
\{\dot{\gamma},n_{1},n_{2}\}
is an oriented frame with respect to the orientation on
Y
.

Definition 3.4
.

A system of
geodesic normal coordinates
for
r_{0}<r_{\text{inj}}
where
r_{\text{inj}}
is the injectivity radius of
Y
is the diffeomorphism
S^{1}\times D_{r_{0}}\simeq N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
for a chosen component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
defined by

(t,x,y)\mapsto\text{Exp}_{\gamma(t)}(xn_{1}+yn_{2}).

Here
t
is the coordinate on the
S^{1}
factor, which has radius normalized so that
t\in[0,|\mathcal{Z}_{j}|)
. In these coordinates the Riemannian metric
g
can be written

g=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}+\ [2x\mathfrak{m}_{x}(t)+2y\mathfrak{m}_{y}(t)]dt^{2}\ +\ [\mu(t)y]dtdx\ +\ [-\mu(t)x]dtdy\ +\ O(r^{2})

where
\mu(t),\mathfrak{m}_{x}(t),\mathfrak{m}_{y}(t)
are defined by

\displaystyle\mu(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}n_{x},n_{y}\rangle=-\langle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}n_{y},n_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},n_{\alpha}\rangle

for
\alpha=x,y
. Given such a coordinate system,
(t,r,\theta)
are used to denote the corresponding cylindrical coordinates, and
(t,z,\overline{z})
the complex ones on the
D_{r_{0}}
factor.

We also have the following trivialization. Recall that as in Definition
2.11
, we denote
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

For each component
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, there exists a local trivialization

\sigma_{j}:(S_{0}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0}\otimes E)|_{N(\mathcal{Z}_{j})-\mathcal{Z}_{j}}\simeq(N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})-\mathcal{Z}_{j})\ \times\ (\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H}

in which

•

The connection
A_{0}
on the middle factor is given by

A_{0}\ =\ \text{d}+\frac{i}{2}d\theta+\epsilon_{j}\tfrac{i}{2}dt

where
\epsilon_{j}=0
or
1
depending on
\ell
and
S_{0}
.

•

The Dirac operator may be written

\not{D}_{A_{0}}=\not{D}+\frac{i}{2}\gamma(d\theta)+\mathfrak{d}_{1}+\mathfrak{d}_{0}

(3.3)

where
\not{D}
is the standard Euclidean Dirac operator, and
\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{0}
are respectively a first order and zeroth order term satisfying

|\mathfrak{d}_{1}\psi|\leq r|\nabla\psi|\hskip 85.35826pt|\mathfrak{d}_{2}\psi|\leq C|\psi|.

•

The anti-linear involution
J
defined in
2.22
is given by
e^{-i\epsilon_{j}t/2}e^{-i\theta}J_{0}
where
J_{0}
is given by the expression
2.14
. Consequently, a spinor
\Phi\in\Gamma(S^{\text{Re}})
takes the form

\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+e^{-i\theta}e^{-i\epsilon_{j}t}\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\beta}\\
\overline{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

(3.4)

Proof.

First, we trivialize the middle factor
\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}
. Fix a vector
s_{0}\in(\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}})_{(0,r_{0}/2,0)}
in the fiber above the point
(0,r_{0}/2,0)\in~N(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
. Parallel transport using
A_{0}
in the
+\theta
and
+t
directions defines a section
s
with monodromy
-1
around the meridian of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
and monodromy
\epsilon_{j}=0
or
1
around the longitude. The latter is determined by the line bundle
\ell
and by the choice of spin structure
S_{0}
. The section
e^{i\theta/2}e^{i\epsilon_{j}t/2}s
therefore defines a global nowhere-vanishing section of
\underline{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\ell
. Since
s
is parallel by construction, in the trivialization

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell

(3.5)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle e^{i\theta/2}e^{i\epsilon_{j}t/2}sf

(3.6)
