the connection becomes

{A_{0}}=\text{d}+\frac{i}{2}d\theta+\epsilon_{j}\tfrac{i}{2}dt.

The first bullet point follows.

The second bullet point, we extend the above trivialization to
\sigma_{j}
by choosing local trivializations of
S_{0}
and
E
that extend across
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
. We may additionally specify that in the trivialization of
S_{0}
, the two factors of
\underline{\mathbb{C}}
are the
\pm i
eigenspaces of
\gamma(e^{1})
where
\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}\}
is the orthonormal co-frame on
N(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
extending
dt,dx,dy
. In this trivialization, the connection on
S_{0}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0}\otimes E
is given by

\nabla_{A_{0}}=\text{d}+\frac{i}{2}d\theta+\text{b}_{0}+\Gamma+\epsilon_{j}\tfrac{i}{2}dt

where
\text{b}_{0}
and
\Gamma
are zeroth order terms arising from the connection
B_{0}
and the Christoffel symbols of
g_{0}
respectively. The second bullet point is then immediate from the first, where
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
arises from the
O(r)
failure of
dt,dx,dy
to be an orthonormal frame for
g_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
arises from a combination of
\text{b}_{0},\Gamma
and
\epsilon_{j}
.

For the third bullet point, recall that
J
is given by the local expression
2.14
in local trivialization respecting the
SU(2)
structure on
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}})
(which
\sigma_{j}
is not). In a system of local trivializations on a a contractible open sets
U_{\alpha}\subset N(\mathcal{Z}_{j})-\mathcal{Z}_{j}
respecting the
SU(2)
structure (i.e. one in which the transition functions on
\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}
are simply),
A_{0}
is given in each of these trivializations simply by
d
. These differ from the trivialization
3.6
by transition functions
e^{i\theta/2}e^{i\epsilon_{j}t/2}
, hence in the trivialization
\sigma_{j}
constructed using
3.6
,
J
is given by

J=(e^{-i\theta/2}e^{-i\epsilon_{j}t/2})J_{0}(e^{i\theta/2}e^{i\epsilon_{j}t/2})

and the third bullet point follows from the complex anti-linearity of
J
.
∎

Using these local coordinates and local trivialization, the Dirac operator
3.3
takes the local form
3.1
and the general regularity theory of
[
27
]
applies to give local asymptotic expansions. We consider the following type of asymptotic expansion.

Definition 3.6
.

A spinor
\psi\in L^{2}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{E})
is said to admit a
Polyhomogenous Expansion
with index set
\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2}
if

\psi\sim\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{m+n=k}r^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}c_{k,m,n}(t)z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d_{k,m,n}(t)e^{-i\theta}z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\end{pmatrix}

where
c_{k,m,n}(t),d_{k,m,n}(t)\in C^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathbb{H})
, and where
\sim
denotes convergence in the following sense: for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the partial sums

\psi_{N}=\sum_{k\leq N}\sum_{m+n=k}r^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}c_{k,m,n}(t)z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d_{k,m,n}(t)e^{-i\theta}z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\end{pmatrix}

satisfies the pointwise bounds

\displaystyle|\psi-\psi_{N}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N}r^{N+1}

(3.7)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{t}^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}_{A_{0}}(\psi-\psi_{N})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N,\alpha,\beta}r^{N+1-|\beta|}

(3.8)

for constants
C_{N,\alpha,\beta}
determined by the background data and choice of local coordinates and trivialization. Here,
\beta
is a multi-index of derivatives in the normal directions.

The appropriate version of elliptic regularity for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors, which follows from
[
27
]
, is the following.

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose that
\Phi_{0}\in rH^{1}_{e}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};S^{\text{Re}})
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor. Then
\Phi_{0}
admits a polyhomogenous expansion. Thus in the trivialization of Lemma
3.5
,
\Phi_{0}
has a local expression

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}c(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
\overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

(3.9)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\underset{m+n=k}{k\geq 1}}r^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}c_{m,n}(t)z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d_{m,n}(t)e^{-i\theta}z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+r^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}_{m,n}(t)z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\ \ \ \ \ \\
\overline{c}_{m,n}(t)e^{-i\theta}z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

(3.10)

where
c(t),d(t),c_{k,m,n}(t),d_{k,m,n}(t)\in C^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
. The terms on the second line of the expression will often be abbreviated by adding
O(r^{3/2})
to the leading order term, with the understanding that this notation refers to a collection of bounds as in (
3.7
)-(
3.8
).

Proof.

The existence of such an expansion is a consequence of the regularity theory in
[
27
,
12
]
. The relation between the two components follows from the form of
S^{\text{Re}}
Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

The smooth functions
c(t),d(t),c_{m,n}(t),d_{m,n}(t)
depend on the choice of frame
\{n_{x},n_{y}\}
made to define local coordinates in Definition
3.4
. More invariantly, these are sections of
(N\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\otimes(1/2+m-n)}
where
m=n=0
for
c(t),d(t)
.

3.3
Reconstructing
Spin^{c}
Structures

In this subsection, the above local forms are used to show that the
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure arising from the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{0}\to Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
can be extended to one on
Y
satisfying the conclusion of Theorem
1.2
. This section therefore finishes the topological portion of Theorems
1.2
-
1.4
.

Since
\Phi_{0}
behaves like
r^{1/2}
along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, it does not extend to the closed manifold as a smooth section. A version of
\Phi_{0}
squared does, however. For any section
\Phi
, let
\det\Phi\in\Gamma(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{L}_{0}^{2})
be the section defined as follows.
The identification used in (
2.13
) induces a bundle isomorphism
S_{E}\simeq\text{Hom}(E^{*},S)
so that
\Phi
can be regarded as a bundle map
E^{*}\to S
;
\det(\Phi)
is then a section of
\det(E^{*})^{-1}\otimes\det(S)=\mathcal{L}_{0}^{2}
. As in (
2.13
), there are local trivialization in which
\det(\Phi)
is given by the determinant of the matrix

\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}\\
\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.11)

Lemma 3.9
.

The following inequality holds pointwise for any
\Phi
:

\tfrac{1}{4}|\Phi|^{4}\leq|\mu(\Phi,\Phi)|^{2}+|\det\Phi|^{2}.

In particular,
\det(\Phi_{0})
vanishes nowhere on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Phi
has the above form, and write
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. Using the expressions for the moment map (
2.10
-
2.12
) denote the
dt
component by
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}
and the
dx+idy
component by
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
. Using the Hermitian inner product on
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\det(\Phi)
shows:

\displaystyle|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi,\Phi)|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(|\alpha_{1}|^{2}+|\alpha_{2}|^{2}-|\beta_{1}|^{2}+|\beta_{2}|^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\alpha|^{4}+|\beta|^{4}-2|\alpha|^{2}|\beta|^{2}

\displaystyle|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\Phi,\Phi)|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta_{1}+\overline{\alpha}_{2}\beta_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\alpha_{1}|^{2}|\beta_{1}|^{2}+|\alpha_{2}|^{2}|\beta_{2}|^{2}+2\text{Re}(\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}\overline{\beta}_{2})

\displaystyle|\det\Phi|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}-\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\alpha_{1}|^{2}|\beta_{2}|^{2}+|\alpha_{2}|^{2}|\beta_{1}|^{2}-2\text{Re}(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}\overline{\beta}_{1})

and the two real parts are negatives after conjugating. Adding these yields

|\Phi|^{4}=|\alpha|^{4}+|\beta|^{4}+2|\alpha|^{2}|\beta|^{2}\leq|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi,\Phi)|^{2}+4|\alpha|^{2}|\beta|^{2}\leq|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi,\Phi)|^{2}+4|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\Phi,\Phi)|^{2}+4|\det(\Phi)|^{2}.

The second statement follows directly from this inequality applied to
\Phi_{0}
; one has
\mu(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{0})=0
by definition of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor, hence
\det(\Phi_{0})
vanishing on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
would imply that
\Phi_{0}
vanished there as well, which is forbidden by Assumption
2
.
∎

Using
\det(\Phi_{0})
, we can now construct the
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure used in the gluing.

Proposition 3.10
.

There exists a unique
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure with spinor bundle
S=S_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{L}
where
\mathcal{L}\to Y
is a complex line bundle such that

(i)

\mathcal{L}|_{Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\simeq(\ell\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}})
.

(ii)

The trivialization of Lemma
3.5
extend to local trivialization of
\mathcal{L}
where the conclusion of Lemma
3.5
continue to hold.

(iii)

The determinant
\det(S)=\mathcal{L}^{2}
satisfies

c_{1}(\mathcal{L}^{2})=-\text{PD}[\mathcal{Z}_{0}].

(3.12)

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{L}
by extending the trivializations of Lemma
3.5
on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
across
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
for every component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Thus items (i) and (ii) hold automatically. Note, however, that while the expressions for the connection form
id\theta
and the involution
J=e^{-i\theta}J_{0}
they are not smooth across the origin.

Thus it suffices to show (iii). The local trivializations for
\mathcal{L}
as defined induce local trivializations
\mathcal{L}^{2}|_{N(\mathcal{Z}_{j})}\simeq N(\mathcal{Z}_{j})\times\mathbb{C}
of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
. In these, the local expression (
3.9
)-(
3.10
) show that locally

\det(\Phi_{0})=(|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2})\overline{z}\ +\ \overline{z}f(z,\overline{z}).

Assumption
2
implies that
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
, thus using Lemma
3.9
it follows that
\det(\Phi_{0})
is a smooth section of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
vanishing transversely along
|\det\Phi_{0}|^{-1}(0)=\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Since
\overline{z}
is orientation reversing, and the coordinates for the local expressions respect the orientation of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
by construction, (iii) follows.

∎

Remark 3.11
.

Notice that the orientation convention here is opposite that in
[
11
]
. As observed in
[
11
]
, the condition (
3.12
) places topological restrictions on the homology class represented by
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. We emphasize that these restrictions follow from the existence of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor and do not require an additional assumption.

Restatement of Assumptions

The above proposition completes any global topological statements required for the proofs of Theorems
1.2
-
1.4
. The remainder of the article is analytic in nature and works exclusively in the local coordinates and trivializations constructed using Lemma
3.5
and Proposition
3.10
. To summarize briefly, the starting point of the local analysis is the following local expressions for the pair
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}c(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
\ \overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j+O(r^{3/2}))

(3.13)

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}d\theta

(3.14)

and Assumption
2
in its pragmatic form is the statement that the quantity
K(t)=\tfrac{2}{3}(|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2})
is nowhere vanishing. Additionally, in a slight abuse of notation we have switched from letting
A_{0}
denote the connection to letting it denote the connection form in the local trivialization. Additionally, for notational simplicity we will assume that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
consists of a single component and that
\epsilon_{j}=0
and
B_{0}
is the product connection in the trivialization for this component. It is a trivial matter at the end of Section
8
to eliminate these restrictions.

4
De-Singularized Configurations

In this section we begin main portion of the analysis required for the proof of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.4
. As explained in the introduction, the construction requires several steps, the first of which is the “de-singularization” step

(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
de-sing.

of the limiting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor to an
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of nearby smooth configuration, which we undertake in the current section.

The de-singularized configurations are a family of solutions to an ODE parameterized by
(t,\varepsilon)\in S^{1}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0})
. More specifically, they are the
S^{1}
-parameterized family of the two-dimensional fiducial solutions on planes normal to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
for the leading order term of
\Phi_{0}
. These two-dimensional fiducial solutions are exact solutions in the Euclidean metric, and first appeared in the context of Hitchin’s Equations in
[
19
,
7
]
, though their existence may have been known to physicists before that. They are obtained from the limiting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor by applying a singular complex gauge transformation which solves a degenerate second order ODE. Section 4.1 gives the construction of these two-dimensional fiducial solutions, and follows
[
19
]
quite closely. Section 4.2 departs from the approach of
[
19
]
and from the holomorphic setting to introduce the parameterized version which yields the de-singularized configurations. Section 4.3 calculates the size of the error term by which these fail to be true solutions.

As explained at the end of the previous section, we now fix local coordinates on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and a trivialization of
S_{E}|_{N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}
in which the local expressions are given by (
3.13
-(
3.14
).

4.1
Dimensional Reducation

This subsection constructs fiducial solutions on the complex plane. Let
(\mathbb{C},g_{0})
denote the complex plane equipped with the flat Euclidean metric. The spinor bundle
S_{g_{0}}\simeq\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
is identified with the trivial
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-bundle, and
E\simeq\mathbb{C}\times{\mathbb{H}}
with the trivial quaternionic line bundle. Assume in this case that
B_{0}
is the product connection. We may write a configuration
(\Phi,A)\in\Gamma(S_{E})\times\mathcal{A}_{U(1)}
as

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\\
\beta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{2}\\
\beta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\left(adz-\overline{a}d\overline{z}\right)

where
\alpha,\beta
are
\mathbb{H}
-valued functions, and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},a
are complex-valued functions, and where we associate a connection form
A
with the connection
d+A
in the given trivialization. To convert from the complex coordinates to the real ones, we use the isomorphism

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}(i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\Omega^{1,0}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle i(a_{x}dx+a_{y}dy)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(a_{y}+ia_{x})dz

on
1
-forms. Under this association (see also Section 6.1 and Section 3 of
[
2
]
), the dimensionally-reduced blown-up Seiberg-Witten equations become

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(4.2)

\displaystyle F_{A}+\frac{\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(4.3)

where
(\mu_{\mathbb{R}},\mu_{\mathbb{C}})=\star_{3}\mu
under the isomorphism
\mathbb{R}\omega\oplus\Omega^{0,1}\simeq iT^{*}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{C})
so that, explicitly,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1,2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\beta_{j}\right)d\overline{z}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1,2}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}-|\beta_{i}|^{2}\right)idx\wedge dy.

Note also that we do not assume that
\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}=1
(in fact we won’t even assume it is in
L^{2}
). As we are looking for local solutions which will later be transferred to the closed manifold
Y
where the normalization is global, this is irrelevant for our immediate purposes.

The configuration space of pairs
(\Phi,A)\in\Gamma(C;S_{E}\oplus\Omega^{1}({i\mathbb{R}}))
carries an action of the complex gauge group

\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\{e^{h}\ |\ h:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}\}

by

e^{h}\cdot(\alpha,\beta,A)\mapsto(e^{h}\alpha,e^{-\overline{h}}\beta,A+\partial\overline{h}-\overline{\partial}h).

The first two Seiberg-Witten equations (
4.1
-
4.2
) are invariant under the action of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}
, while the third (
4.3
) is invariant only under the action of the real gauge group (
h\in i\mathbb{R}
).

Consider a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor which is equal to the leading order term in its asymptotic expansion, so that

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}cr^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
dr^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \ \\
\overline{c}r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)

where,
|c|^{2}+|d|^{2}>0
. Later we will take
c=c(t)
and
d=d(t)
for each fixed
t\in S^{1}
. We look for an
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of solutions satisfying the following ansatz:

Ansatz 4.1
.

Assume that there is an
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of solutions is complex gauge equivalent to
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
via a complex gauge transformation
h_{\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon
. Assume additionally that
h_{\varepsilon}=h_{\varepsilon}(r)
is rotationally invariant, so that

(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}}):=e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}\cdot(\Phi_{0},A_{0}).

We do not assume that
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
is smooth and allow the possibility that
h_{\varepsilon}(r)\to\infty
as
r\to 0
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
be configurations satisfying Ansatz
4.1
above. Then
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
satisfy the dimensionally reduced blown-up Seiberg-Witten equations (
4.1
-
4.3
) on
\mathbb{C}
if and only if the gauge transformations
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
satisfy the
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of ODEs

r^{2}\Delta_{r}h_{\varepsilon}(r)=\frac{9}{4}\frac{K^{2}r^{3}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\sinh(2h_{\varepsilon}(r))

(4.4)

where
\Delta_{r}
is the radial part of the Laplacian
\Delta_{r}=\tfrac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r})
and
K^{2}=\tfrac{2}{3}(|c|^{2}+|d|^{2})
.

Proof.

To begin, recall the polar coordinate expressions

\overline{\partial}_{z}=\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}(\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta})\hskip 28.45274pt\partial_{z}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-i\theta}(\partial_{r}-\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}).

(4.5)

The gauge-transformed configurations may be written as follows, where the second expression is the definition of the function
f_{\varepsilon}(r)
.

\displaystyle\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}cr^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \\
e^{-h_{\varepsilon}(r)}dr^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}\overline{d}r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \ \\
e^{-h_{\varepsilon}(r)}\overline{c}r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

\displaystyle A^{h_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{0}+\partial\overline{h}_{\varepsilon}(r)-\overline{\partial}h_{\varepsilon}(r):=f_{\varepsilon}(r)\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right).

We may now substitute these expressions into the Seiberg-Witten equations (
4.3
). By complex gauge invariance,
\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}
automatically satisfies the
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=0
equation. For the Dirac equation, the
\otimes 1
and
\otimes j
components behave identically so it suffices to calculate the first. This equation becomes

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&-2(\partial+(A^{h_{\varepsilon}})_{z})\\
2(\overline{\partial}+(A^{h_{\varepsilon}})_{\overline{z}})&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}ce^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}r^{1/2}\ \ \ \\
de^{-h_{\varepsilon}(r)}r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\ldots\otimes j.

Focusing on the first component, this becomes

\displaystyle-\left[e^{-i\theta}\left(\partial_{r}-\tfrac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}\right)+\frac{2f_{\varepsilon}(r)e^{-i\theta}}{r}\right]de^{-h_{\varepsilon}(r)}r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{i\theta}e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}}{2r^{1/2}}\Big(-2{f_{\varepsilon}(r)}+(r\partial_{r})h_{\varepsilon}(r)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)

and the first factor is non-zero, hence
f_{\varepsilon}(r)
must satisfy

f_{\varepsilon}(r)=\tfrac{1}{4}+\tfrac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}h_{\varepsilon}(r).

and the second component and the
\otimes j
term give the same equation.

For the third equation (
4.3
), we compute

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}-|\beta_{j}|^{2}\right)dx\wedge dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(r|c|^{2}e^{2h_{\varepsilon}(r)}-r|d|^{2}e^{-2h_{\varepsilon}(r)}+r|d|^{2}e^{2h_{\varepsilon}(r)}-r|c|^{2}e^{-2h_{\varepsilon}(r)})dx\wedge dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i9}{4}\frac{K^{2}r}{2}\sinh(2h_{\varepsilon}(r))dx\wedge dy

\displaystyle F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((\overline{\partial}f)\tfrac{d\overline{z}\wedge dz}{z}-(\partial f)\tfrac{dz\wedge d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\tfrac{1}{z}\partial_{r}f-\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-i\theta}\tfrac{1}{\overline{z}}\partial_{r}f\right)dz\wedge d\overline{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\tfrac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f\right)dz\wedge d\overline{z}=\left(\tfrac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f\right)2idx\wedge dy.

Combining these we obtain the system of ODEs

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}h_{\varepsilon}(r)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\tfrac{2}{r}\partial_{r}f_{\varepsilon}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9}{4}\frac{K^{2}r}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\sinh(2h_{\varepsilon}(r))

(4.7)

and substitution the first into the second then multiplying by
r^{2}
yields the proposition.
∎

Up to a constant factor, Equation (
4.4
) is the same equation obtained for the corresponding situation using Hitchin’s equations. It is of Painlevé type and it is solved in
[
19
]
, Section 3 via the following substitution. It turns out that all the
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of solutions are all re-scalings of a single invariant solution.

Let
\tau=\frac{K}{\varepsilon}r^{3/2}
and
h_{\varepsilon}(r)=G(\tau)
so that

r\partial_{r}=\frac{3}{2}\tau\partial_{\tau}

and Equation (
4.4
) becomes

(\tau\partial_{\tau})^{2}G=\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}\sinh(2G).

(4.8)

This equation admits a distinguished solution which is defined by the two conditions that
h
has an asymptote at
\tau=0
and decays to
0
as
\tau\to\infty
. The next below proposition collects the essential properties of this solution, which we do not prove and instead refer the reader to
[
19
]
(see Equation (25) and the accompanying discussion).

First, we change variables once more. It turns out that it is quite confusing to use the variable
\tau
which depends non-linearly on
r
. We will instead opt for a linear scaling by replacing
\tau
by
\rho=\tau^{2/3}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Define the
\varepsilon
-invariant length
by

\rho:=\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}r.

Then define
H(\rho):=G(\rho^{3/2})
so that the
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of solutions are given by

h_{\varepsilon}(r)=H(\rho).

The essential properties of the solution are now expressed in terms of
H(\rho)
. For the proof of these properties, see Lemma 3.3 of
[
19
]
(performing the above substitution for
\rho
into their results).

Proposition 4.4
.

There exists a unique
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of solutions
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
to (
4.4
) such that
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
has an asymptote at
r=0
and decays to
0
as
r\to\infty
. This family
h_{\varepsilon}(r)=H(\rho)
are all dilations of a single
\varepsilon
-independent function such that
H(\rho)=G(\tau)
solves (
4.8
). It satisfies the following properties

1.

H(\rho)
is strictly positive and monotonically decreasing.

2.

H(\rho)
decays faster than exponentially as
\rho\to\infty
. More specifically, there are constant
C,c,\rho_{0}
such that for
\rho\geq\rho_{0}

H(\rho)\leq C\text{Exp}(-c\rho^{3/2})

and similarly for the derivatives of
H(\rho)
.

3.

At
\rho=0
there is an asymptotic expansion of the form

H(\rho)=-\log\left(\rho^{1/2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}\rho^{2j}\right)

(4.9)

in which
a_{0}\neq 0
. In particular, up to leading order
H(\rho)=\frac{}{}\log(\rho^{-1/2})
so that

e^{H(\rho)}=\rho^{-1/2}+O(1).

4.

The function
f_{\varepsilon}(r)=\tfrac{1}{4}+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho\partial_{\rho}H(\rho)
vanishes to second order at
r=0
, and increases monotonically to its limiting value of
\tfrac{1}{4}
. Additionally,
|\tfrac{f_{\varepsilon}(r)}{r}|\leq C\varepsilon^{-2/3}
for all
r
, and the difference of
f_{\varepsilon}(r)
and
f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(r)
from their limiting values decreases exponentially, i.e.

\displaystyle|f_{\varepsilon}(r)-\tfrac{1}{4}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\rho\text{Exp}(-c\rho^{3/2})

\displaystyle|f_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\rho\text{Exp}(-c\rho^{3/2})

and similarly for the higher derivatives.

Proof.

The first statement is immediate from the re-scaling above. The first through third items follow immediately from Equation (27) in
[
19
]
. The first two statement of the fourth bullet point follow from parts (a)-(c) of Lemma 3.3 in
[
19
]
. The exponential bound on
f^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(r)
follows from that on
H(\rho)
by the equation
\tfrac{2}{r}\partial_{r}f_{\varepsilon}(r)=\tfrac{9}{4}\tfrac{K^{2}r}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\sinh(2h_{\varepsilon}(r))
, and the one on
f^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(r)
by the fundamental theorem of calculus.

∎

The properties of the function
h_{\varepsilon}(r),f_{\varepsilon}(r)
above translate into similar properties of the two-dimensional fiducial solutions
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
. We state these momentarily for the 3-dimensional case. The radial profiles of the two-dimensional fiducial solutions are plotted below with those of the limiting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor:

Figure 1:
The radial profiles of the de-singularized configurations and limiting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor.

4.2
De-Singularization on
Y

This subsection introduces the de-singularized configurations on the closed manifold
Y
, which are a
t
-parameterized of the 2-dimensional fiducial solutions of the previous subsection on each plane normal to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Here, we work in local coordinates on a tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
of radius
\lambda
possibly depending on
\varepsilon
.

Returning to the case of full generality when
\Phi_{0}
may have higher order terms, write

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}c(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \ \\
\overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\ +\ O(r^{3/2})

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}d\theta=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)

as before, and let

K^{2}(t):=\frac{2}{3}(|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}).

Assumption
2
requires that
K(t)
is bounded below by a constant greater than 0 depending only on
\Phi_{0}
.

Definition 4.5
.

Define the
t
-dependent
\varepsilon
-invariant length
by

\rho_{t}:=\left(\frac{K(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}r,

and the
de-singularized configurations
by

(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}}):=e^{\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)h_{\varepsilon}(r,t)}\cdot(\Phi_{0},A_{0})

where
h_{\varepsilon}(r,t)=H(\rho_{t})
and where
\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)
is a cutoff function equal to 1 on a neighborhood of
r\leq\lambda(\varepsilon)
around
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. In the right hand side,
\cdot
still denotes the action of the complex gauge group on the normal planes.

Notice that since for
r\geq\lambda
, the function
h_{\varepsilon}(r,t)
is exponentially small in
\varepsilon
(provided
\lambda\geq\varepsilon^{2/3}
), the cutoff function changes it in a very minor way. With the cutoff, the de-singularized configurations extend to all of
Y
by setting them equal to
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
outside the tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
.

The following properties are retained from the 2-dimensional version:

Proposition 4.6
.

The de-singularized configurations satisfy the following properties:

•

The configuration
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
is smooth.

•

The pair converges to the limiting configuration
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
in
C^{\infty}_{loc}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))
exponentially quickly in the sense that there is a constant
c_{0}
such that for
r\geq c_{0}\varepsilon^{2/3}
, one has

\|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}-\Phi_{0}\|_{C^{k}}\leq C_{k}\varepsilon^{-2k/3}\text{Exp}(-\frac{cr^{3/2}}{\varepsilon})\hskip 28.45274pt\|A^{h_{\varepsilon}}-A_{0}\|_{C^{k}}\leq C_{k}r\varepsilon^{-2(k+1)/3}\text{Exp}(-\frac{cr^{3/2}}{\varepsilon})

•

There are pointwise bounds

\tfrac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|}{\varepsilon}\geq c\varepsilon^{-2/3}\hskip 28.45274pt|A^{h_{\varepsilon}}|\leq C\varepsilon^{-2/3}\hskip 28.45274pt|\nabla_{A_{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|\leq\frac{C}{r^{1/2}}.

and
|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|
is monotonically increasing in
r
for small
r
.

Proof.

For smoothness in the normal directions, notice the expansion of (
4.9
) from Proposition
4.4
shows
H(\rho)=\log(a_{0}\rho^{1/2}(1+O(\rho)^{2}))
where
O(\rho^{2})
contains only even powers and is therefore smooth. It follows that
e^{h_{\varepsilon}}=r^{1/2}(1+O(r^{2}))
where the
O(r^{2})
is also smooth, thus the leading order term of
\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}
is

\begin{pmatrix}e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}c(t)r^{1/2}\\
e^{-h_{\varepsilon}(r)}d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{h_{\varepsilon}(r)}\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\\
e^{-h_{\varepsilon}(r)}\overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

is smooth and constant at
r=0
in the top component, and vanishes like
\overline{z}
at
r=0
in the second. The same applies to the higher order terms from Proposition
3.7
which only contain additional factors of
z^{m}\overline{z}^{n}
. Similar considerations show that
\rho\sinh(2H(\rho))
is smooth and vanishes to second order at the origin, which implies the same for
f_{\varepsilon}(r)
thus
A^{h_{\varepsilon}}=f_{\varepsilon}(r)\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)
is smooth and vanishes to first order. For smoothness in the
t
-directions, notice that

\frac{d}{dt}e^{H(\rho_{t})}=e^{H(\rho_{t})}\frac{dH}{d\rho_{t}}\frac{d\rho_{t}}{dt}=e^{H(\rho_{t})}\frac{dH}{d\rho_{t}}\frac{2K^{\prime}(t)}{3K(t)}\rho_{t}

(4.10)

is again smooth across the origin since
\rho_{t}\tfrac{\partial H}{\partial\rho_{t}}\sim\text{const}
is also smooth across the origin.

The second and third bullet points follow directly from rescaling the corresponding properties of
H(\rho)
from Proposition
4.4
, and using the expression (
4.10
) to bound the
t
-derivatives.

∎

4.3
Calculation of Error

Denote by
E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}
the error by which the de-singularized configurations fail to solve the (un-renormalized) Seiberg-Witten equations. That is,

SW(\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})=E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}.

The superscript is present to indicate that this is initial error in an eventual iteration process.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\gamma<<1
be a small positive constant. There is an
\varepsilon_{0}
such that for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, the error
E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\|E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\leq C\varepsilon^{-\gamma},

where
C
is constant independent of
\varepsilon
. Moreover, the error is exponentially concentrated along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in the sense that there is a constant
c_{0}
such that for
r\geq c_{0}\varepsilon^{2/3}
there is a pointwise bound

\|E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\text{Exp}(-\frac{cr^{3/2}}{\varepsilon}).

Remark 4.8
.

One should think of, say,
\gamma=10^{-6}
. The purpose of this small constant is so that in the region where
r\geq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma}
, the difference of the de-singularized configurations from the limiting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
is exponentially small. It could just as easily be a power of
\log(\varepsilon)
, and is in fact probably not necessary at all, but the proof is more intricate.

Proof.

The second statement is immediate from the exponential decay properties in the second bullet point of (
4.6
) and the fact that
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
solves the Seiberg-Witten equations in the region
r\geq c_{0}\varepsilon^{2/3}
.

Write
E_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}=(E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})
for the spinor and form components of the error respectively, so that

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\star F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}+\frac{\mu(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}.

We calculate the error in two regions, the “interior” region
\text{Int}=\{r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}\}
where
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma/10
for
\gamma
as in the statement of the lemma, and the “exterior” region
Y-\text{Int}
where
r\geq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}
, so that

\|E_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|E_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|E_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}\|_{L^{2}(Y-\text{Int})}.

The boundedness in the exterior region is immediate given the exponential decay from the first sentence of the proof. For the interior region, the triangle inequality implies that:

\displaystyle\|E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\not{D}^{0}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|(\not{D}-\not{D}^{0})\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|\text{cl}(B_{0})\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|(\text{cl}-\text{cl}^{0})A^{h_{\varepsilon}}\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

\displaystyle\|E_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\star F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}+\tfrac{\mu(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\star_{0}F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}+\tfrac{\mu^{0}(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|(\star-\star_{0})F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|\tfrac{(\mu-\mu^{0})(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

where
\not{D}^{0},\star_{0},\mu^{0},\text{cl}^{0}
denote the Dirac operator, hodge star, moment map, and Clifford multiplication in the product metric.

Investing the first term, recall that by definition of the de-singularized solutions these solve the leading order term in the product metric for the 2-dimensional Dirac operator, hence

\displaystyle\|\not{D}^{0}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\tfrac{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}+\|(\not{D}^{0}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}})^{\mathbb{C}}\tfrac{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\text{Int})}

where
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}
denotes the desingularization of the
O(r^{3/2})
terms, and
\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the two-dimensional Dirac operator (
4.1
). Since there are bounds
|\Phi|,|\partial_{t}\Phi_{0}|<Cr^{1/2}
, the first of these terms is bounded by

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{S^{1}}\left(\int(|\partial_{t}e^{H(\rho_{t})}|+|\partial_{t}e^{-H(\rho_{t})}|)^{2}|\Phi_{0}|^{2}+(e^{H(\rho_{t})}+e^{-H(\rho_{t})})^{2}|\partial_{t}\Phi_{0}|^{2})rdrd\theta\right)dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{S^{1}}\left(\int(|\partial_{t}e^{H(\rho_{t})}|+|\partial_{t}e^{-H(\rho_{t})}|)^{2}r+(e^{H(\rho_{t})}+e^{-H(\rho_{t})})^{2}r)rdrd\theta\right)dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{S^{1}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K}\right)^{2}\left(\int_{\rho_{t}\leq C\varepsilon^{-\gamma^{\prime}}}(e^{H(\rho_{t})}+e^{-H(\rho_{t})})^{2}\Big|\frac{dH}{d\rho_{t}}\frac{2\dot{K}(t)}{3K(t)}\rho_{t}\Big|^{2}\rho_{t}^{2}d\rho_{t}d\theta\right)dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

Const

since the integrand is a bounded function of
\rho_{t}
at the origin (by the same reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
4.6
) and decays exponentially as
\rho_{t}\to\infty
. For the second term, using complex gauge-invariance, one has

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{S^{1}}\left(\int_{r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}}|e^{H(\rho_{t})}+e^{-H(\rho_{t})}|^{2}|\nabla_{A_{0}}(\Phi_{0})^{h.o.}|^{2}rdrd\theta\right)dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{S^{1}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K(t)}\right)^{2}\left(\int_{\rho_{t}\leq\varepsilon^{-\gamma^{\prime}}}|e^{H(\rho_{t})}+e^{-H(\rho_{t})}|^{2}\rho_{t}^{2}d\rho_{t}d\theta\right)dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{-\gamma}

since
|\nabla_{A_{0}}(\Phi_{0})^{h.o.}|\leq Cr^{1/2}
.

The other terms are similar using the fact that
g-g^{0}=O(r)
where
g^{0}
is the product metric: for each, one rescales to the
\rho_{t}
coordinates, collects powers of
\varepsilon
and observes that the rescaled integrand is a fixed integrable function of
\rho_{t}
.
∎

5
Re-scaling

To prove Theorem
1.2
we must correct the de-singularized configurations to fiducial solutions on a tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda(\varepsilon)}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
we must solve the non-linear equation

(\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}+Q)(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})=E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}.

(5.1)

The solvability of this equation—and therefore the conclusion of Theorem
1.2
—follows from a standard application of the Implicit Function Theorem after showing a version of the following statement:

Theorem: In the proper context, the linearization of the Seiberg-Witten Equations at the de-singularized configurations
\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}
is invertible, and the norm of the inverse is suitably controlled as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The precise version of this theorem, wherein the meaning of “proper context” and “suitably controlled” are clarified, is given in Theorem
7.1
in Section 7.

The remainder of the paper is devoted to the set-up and proof of Theorem
7.1
. In the present section, we define weighted Sobolev spaces which provide the functional-analytic setting for the theorem, and begin the study of the the linearization in the model case that the metric on
Y
is a product near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

5.1
Function Spaces

Let
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
be the tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
of radius
\lambda
. Eventually,
\lambda=\lambda(\varepsilon)
will depend on the parameter
\varepsilon
as in the statements of Theorem
1.2
-
1.4
. .

We now define a family of weighted Sobolev spaces, which naturally arise from the Weitzenböck formula in Section
2.4
. To this end, let
R_{\varepsilon}
denote a weight function given by

R_{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{4/3}+r^{2}}

(5.2)

where
r=\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and

\kappa=\frac{1}{\underset{t\in S^{1}}{\min}\ K(t)^{2/3}}

on a tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
\lambda_{0}
, and smoothing off so that
R_{\varepsilon}=\text{constant }
outside this neighborhood. This weight function is approximately equal to
r
for
r>O(\varepsilon^{2/3})
, and for
r\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
it levels off so that it is globally bounded below by a constant times
\varepsilon^{2/3}
. In the invariant scale, this leveling off occurs at
\rho_{t}=O(1)
. Furthermore, taking the minimum over
S^{1}
ensures the weights satisfy

\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K(t)}\right)^{4/3}\frac{1}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{{1+\rho_{t}^{2}}}

for every
t
.

Consider the norms on sections of
(\varphi,a)\in\Gamma(S_{E})\oplus(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1})(i\mathbb{R})
given by

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\varphi|^{2}+|a|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(5.4)

in which
\nabla
and
dV
denote the connection formed from the Levi-Civita connection, the spin connection, and the connection
B
on
E
, and
dV
the Riemannian volume form.

We then define

Definition 5.1
.

Let

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\varphi,a)\ |\ \|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}<\infty\}

\displaystyle L^{2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\varphi,a)\ |\ \|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}<\infty\}

denote the Hilbert spaces of sections on which the above norms are finite, equipped with the inner products arising from the polarizations of the respective norms (
5.3
-
5.4
). When it is clear from the context, we will omit the reference to the domain
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
from the notation.

Note that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}
is uniformly bounded (in
\varepsilon
) on these spaces. These norms are natural in the sense all but the middle term of the norm arise from the positive terms of the Weitzenböck formula for
\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}
by omitting the cross term
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{B}
. Since
\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\sim\Phi_{0}
outside a
\rho_{t}\sim 1
neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, the fourth term gives the
\varphi^{\text{Im}}
component a stronger weight that
\varphi^{\text{Re}}
.

We also have the following weighted versions of the above spaces: let
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
be a real number. Then we define the
(\varepsilon,\nu)
-weighted norms by

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\varphi|^{2}+|a|^{2}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

and

Definition 5.2
.

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\varphi,a)\ |\ \|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}<\infty\}

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\varphi,a)\ |\ \|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}<\infty\}

to be the spaces of sections on on which these norms are finite.

Remark 5.3
.

Since
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is compact, there is an equivalence of norms showing
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}=L^{1,2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))
for every
\varepsilon
, just not uniformly in
\varepsilon
.

5.2
The Model Operator

The operator
\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}
can be treated as a small perturbation of the the operator in the case that the metric near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is a product and
\Phi_{0}
has only leading order terms. In this section we begin the study of this model case. The general case is deduced easily from this one in Section
7.6
.

Thus assume from here until Section
7.6
, that for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
\lambda_{0}
, the pair
(g,B)
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
are given by

g=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}\hskip 56.9055ptB_{0}=\text{d}\text{ is the product connection on $\mathbb{H}$}

and

\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}c(t)r^{1/2}\\
d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\\
\overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\hskip 68.28644ptA_{0}=\frac{i}{2}d\theta

in the geodesic normal coordinates and trivialization of
S_{E}
on
N_{\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
. Moreover, we assume that
\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)h_{\varepsilon}(r)=h_{\varepsilon}(r)
on
N_{\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
.

Definition 5.4
.

The operator

\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}&\mathcal{\gamma}(\_)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\_,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}&\mathbb{d}\end{pmatrix}

defined using the above data is referred to as the
model Linearization
at the de-singularized configurations. It may be written as

\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}=\sigma(dt)\partial_{t}+\mathcal{N}_{t}

where
\sigma
is the principal symbol and
\mathcal{N}_{t}
is the
Normal Operator
defined by

\mathcal{N}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}&\mathcal{\gamma}(\_)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\_,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}&\mathbb{d}^{\mathbb{C}}\end{pmatrix}

(5.5)

where
\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{d}^{\mathbb{C}}
are the operators on the normal disks
\{t\}\times D_{\lambda_{0}}
. Explicitly, writing
a=(a_{0},a_{t}dt+a_{x}dx+a_{y}dy)
,

\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}^{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}&0\end{pmatrix}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{d}^{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-d^{\star}\\
0&0&d\\
-d&-\star d&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}\\
a_{t}\\
a_{x}dx+a_{y}dy\end{pmatrix}

where
d,\star
now denote the operators on
D_{\lambda_{0}}
.

Without changing notation, we continue to use
H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}
and
L^{2}_{\nu}
to refer to the Hilbert spaces defined using the data in the model case.

5.3
Re-Scaling

Since the
\varepsilon
-paramterized family of de-singularizing complex gauge transformations
h_{\varepsilon}(r)=H(\rho)
for fixed
t
are all dilations of a single
\varepsilon
-invariant function, the
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of normal operators
\mathcal{N}_{t}
are likewise all scalings of a single
\varepsilon
-independent operator. In this subsection, we rescale the normal disks to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
to express
\mathcal{N}_{t}
in terms of the scale-invariant coordinate
\rho_{t}
of Definition
4.5
. Throughout this subsection, we omit the
t
-dependence from the notation, and use
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
to denote the rescaled version of
\mathcal{N}
and similarly for other structures.

Let

\mathbb{r}:=\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\lambda

(5.6)

so that the scaling

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}:D_{\mathbb{r}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle r=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K}\right)^{2/3}\rho

defines an isomorphism between the disk in the rescaled coordinate
\rho
and that in the original coordinate
r
. We consider the re-scaled disk
D_{\mathbb{r}}
equipped with polar coordinates
(\rho,\theta)
, with accompanying euclidean and complex coordinates
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
and
(w,\overline{w})
respectively. It is considered with the Euclidean metric
(dx^{\prime})^{2}+(dy^{\prime})^{2}
(as opposed to the pullback of the Euclidean metric on
D_{\lambda}
). Since only the normal coordinates are scaled, the
dx,dy
components of
a
scale as
1
-forms, while the remaining components and spinor scale as functions. Explicitly, the pullback of forms and spinors from
D_{\lambda}
are related by

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{*}_{\varepsilon}(dx)=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K}\right)^{2/3}dx^{\prime}\ \hskip 28.45274pt\Upsilon^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi(r))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(\rho(r)).

\displaystyle\ \Upsilon^{*}_{\varepsilon}(dy)=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K}\right)^{2/3}dy^{\prime}\hskip 28.45274pt\Upsilon^{*}_{\varepsilon}(a_{0}(r))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{0}(\rho(r))

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\Upsilon^{*}_{\varepsilon}(a_{t}(r))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{t}(\rho(r)).

Definition 5.5
.

We define the
Scale-invariant Configurations
by

\displaystyle\Phi^{H}:=\frac{1}{K}\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/3}\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\left(\begin{pmatrix}e^{H}c\rho^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}d\rho^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{H}\overline{d}\rho^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}\overline{c}\rho^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\right)

\displaystyle A^{H}=\Upsilon^{*}(A^{h_{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\rho\partial_{\rho}H(\rho)\right)\left(\frac{dw}{w}-\frac{d\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}\right),

And the
Scale-invariant Normal Operator
by

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}:=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}&\gamma(\_)\Phi^{H}\\
\mu(\_,\Phi^{H})&\mathbb{d}^{\mathbb{C}}\end{pmatrix}

(5.7)

where
\partial_{A^{H}},\gamma,\mu,\mathbb{d}^{\mathbb{C}}
are defined using the Euclidean metric in
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
coordinates.

The rescaling
\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}
extends to a map of sections, which is weighted to be a pointwise isometry:

\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}:\Gamma(D_{\lambda};S_{E}\oplus(\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{1})(i\mathbb{R}))\to\Gamma(D_{\mathbb{r}};S_{E}\oplus(\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{0}\oplus\Lambda^{1})(i\mathbb{R}))

by

\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a_{0},a_{t},a_{1}):=\left(\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{*}\varphi,\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{*}(a_{0},a_{t}),\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\Upsilon^{*}_{\varepsilon}a_{1}\right)

so that, explicitly

\displaystyle\varphi(r)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\varphi(\rho)

\displaystyle(a_{0}(r),a_{t}(r))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(a_{0}(\rho),a_{t}(\rho))

\displaystyle a_{x}dx+a_{y}dy

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle a_{x}(\rho)dx^{\prime}+a_{y}(\rho)dy^{\prime}.

This map preserves the pointwise norms since
dx,dx^{\prime}
are unit norm in the Euclidean metrics on
D_{\lambda},D_{\mathbb{r}}
respectively.

Remark 1
.

It’s equivalent to use
\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{*}
in place of
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}^{*}
and define the Scale-invariant Normal Operator using the pullback metric in place of the Euclidean metric
g_{\rho}
. Re-scaling the 1-form components by hand, however, makes the operator manifestly
\varepsilon
-independent.

Proposition 5.6
.

The Normal operator and the Scale-invariant Normal operator are related by

\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{N}_{t}(\varphi,a)=\left(\frac{K(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}(\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)).

Proof.

Changing
\nabla_{r}
derivatives to
\nabla_{\rho}
one has

\nabla_{x}=\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\nabla_{x^{\prime}}

and similarly for
y
. Likewise, for the connection, Clifford multiplication by the unit forms
dw
and
dz
is identical in the two Euclidean metrics, and

\frac{1}{z}=\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\frac{1}{w}

and identically for
\overline{w}
. Thus the diagonal terms scale as claimed. For the off-diagonal terms, one has

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\begin{pmatrix}e^{H}cr^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}dr^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{H}\overline{d}r^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}\overline{c}r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{K}\right)^{1/3}\left(\begin{pmatrix}e^{H}c\rho^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}d\rho^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{H}\overline{d}r^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}\overline{c}\rho^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\Phi^{H}

by definition of
\Phi^{H}
, hence the off-diagonal terms scale in the same way.
∎

Scale-Invariant Hilbert Spaces

We also define a scale-invariant version of the Hilbert space
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. Let
\mathbb{r}>>0
continue denote the re-scaled radius as in (
5.6
) (eventually, we will take
\mathbb{r}=O(\varepsilon^{-1/6}))
. Let
R=\sqrt{1+\rho^{2}}
denote a weight function

Definition 5.7
.

The
scale-invariant norm
on sections of
S_{E}\oplus(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1})
on
D_{\mathbb{r}}
is given by

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}(D_{\mathbb{r}})}:=\left(\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R^{2}}+|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{H})|^{2}+|a|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(5.8)

and the
scale-invariant
L^{2}
norm
by

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})}:=\left(\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\varphi|^{2}+|a|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(5.9)

where,
dV
denotes the Euclidean volume form and
\nabla
the product connection induced by the chosen trivialization defined using structures defined by the scale invariant coordinate
\rho
. As in the unscaled case, there is an equivalence of norms so that
H^{1}(D_{\mathbb{r}})=L^{1,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})
for every
\mathbb{r}
, just not uniformly so.

The scale-invariant norm is the two-dimensional and scale-invariant version of the
\varepsilon
-weighted norm of
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
in the following sense. The two-dimensional version of the
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
-norm, i.e. assuming that all configurations and
\Phi_{0}
are
t
-invariant, is given by the positive square root of

\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ dV.

(5.10)

Then
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}
provides an equivalence (of
t
-invariant configurations)

\|\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}\simeq\|(\varphi,a)\|_{{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

i.e. the two are bounded by a universal constant times the other once
\varepsilon<<1
. The equivalence is only not an isometry because
R_{\varepsilon}
was defined in Equation (
5.2
) to be a minimum over
t
: if we had defined
R_{\varepsilon}
to be a
t
-dependent weight then the above expression for the norms is an equality.

The
L^{2}
norm is not scale-invariant: instead one has

\|\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}=\left(\tfrac{K(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}.

Combining this with the relation

\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{N}(\varphi,a)=\left(\tfrac{K(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a))

from Proposition
5.6
, we see the diagram
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D_{\lambda})
\widehat{H}^{1}(D_{\mathbb{r}})
L^{2}(D_{\lambda},dV_{r})
L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}},dV_{\rho})
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}
\mathcal{N}
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}

commutes.

6
The Normal Operator

This section analyzes the scale-invariant normal operator
\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{t}}
as a boundary value problem on disks in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Explicitly, writing a pair
(\varphi,a)
as
\varphi=(\alpha,\beta)
and
a=(a_{0},a_{t}dt+a_{x}d\hat{x}+a_{y}d\hat{y})
, the scale invariant Normal operator is given by

\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{t}}(\alpha,\beta,a)=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}&\gamma(\_\ )\Phi^{H}\\
\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\_,\Phi^{H})\\
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\_,\Phi^{H})\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&-d^{\star}+d\\
-d-\star d&0\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\\
a_{0}+a_{t}dt\\
a_{x}d\widehat{x}+a_{y}d\widehat{y}\end{pmatrix}

(6.1)

where all structures are defined using the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The main result is the below Proposition
6.1
, which identifies the kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
and shows that on its orthogonal complement the inverse is bounded uniformly in the scale-invariant norms. The proof of Proposition
6.1
requires several steps, and parts of the proof are somewhat subtle.

The two key ingredients of the proof are a holomorphic description of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
in the case that
a_{0}=a_{t}=0
, which gives control of the operator on disks of fixed radius, and the Weitzenböck formula, which gives control of the operator for large radii. The subtlety of the proof lies in making these ideas work in congress.
This Section is organized as follows. Section 6.1 provides some brief set-up and gives the precise statement for the properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
. Section 6.2 is devoted to a review of the relevant Fredholm theory for first order boundary value problems with Atiyah-Patodi-Singer boundary conditions and for polynomially weighted Sobolev spaces, and Section 6.3 gives the precise boundary conditions. Section 6.4 contains the proof in the case that
a_{0}=a_{t}=0
, and Section 6.5 completes the general case. Finally, in Section 6.6, different projection operators for the kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
are discussed to be used in the next section.

6.1
Set-up

We will impose Atiyah-Patodi-Singer boundary conditions on pairs
(\varphi,a)
on
D_{\mathbb{r}}
to make
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
a Fredholm operator. The boundary conditions are that pairs
(\varphi,a)
lie in the kernel of a certain boundary projection

\widehat{\Pi}^{H^{1}}(D_{\mathbb{r}}):L^{1,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})\longrightarrow H^{+}\subseteq L^{1/2,2}(S^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\ ;\ S_{E}\oplus(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1}))

(6.2)

where
H^{+}
is a “half-dimensional” spectral subspace of boundary values. The precise definition of
\widehat{\Pi}^{H^{1}}
will be given at the end of Section
6.2
in Definition
6.3
. Define the following Hilbert Spaces:

\displaystyle\widehat{H^{1}}(D_{\mathbb{r}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\varphi,a)\ |\ \ \|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}(D_{\mathbb{r}})}<\infty\ \text{ and }\ \widehat{\Pi}^{H^{1}}(\varphi,a)=0\}

(6.3)

\displaystyle L(D_{\mathbb{r}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}}\ ;\ S_{E}\oplus(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1}))

(6.4)

where the first is equipped with the inner product resulting arising from the polarization of the norm (
5.8
), and the latter with the standard
L^{2}
product. We may now state the main result of Section 6.

Proposition 6.1
.

The operator

\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{t}}:\widehat{H}^{1}(D_{\mathbb{r}})\longrightarrow L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})

(6.5)

is a bounded Fredholm operator of (real) Index 2. For
\mathbb{r}
sufficiently large, it is surjective with a kernel of real dimension 2 and the inverse on the complement of the kernel is uniformly bounded. That is,

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}\leq C(\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{t}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}+\|p^{\ker}(\varphi,a)\|_{2})

(6.6)

holds for
C
independent of
\mathbb{r},t
and
p^{\ker}
is a projection operator to
\mathbb{C}
.

\diamondsuit

The presence of a non-trivial kernel merits explanation. Indeed, its appearance may, at first, be surprising since the Seiberg-Witten equations are self-adjoint in 3-dimensions, and thus in most contexts have index 0. However, since the boundary conditions imposed here are somewhat immaterial (when pasting the fiducial solutions onto a 3-manifold, they are cut off near the boundary), we are free to choose any boundary conditions we wish without affecting any eventual gluing construction, and we could have, of course, selected boundary conditions of index zero. The subtelty is that such a choice will
never
result in a uniform bound on the inverse.

This is an essential consequence of the geometry, and is the first manifestation of the convergence of the linearization to a non-Fredholm limit discussed in the introduction. The limiting operator in the normal planes
\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}
has two-dimensional kernel on
L^{2}
which consists of elements that decay like
O(r^{-1/2})
away from
\mathcal{Z}
. Since
\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\to\not{D}_{A_{0}}
(in no precise sense, since the difference in not bounded in
L^{2}
), there is a two-dimensional space of configurations approaching this limiting kernel, which have similar asymptotics. But because these elements decay toward the boundary, they
cannot
be excluded by disallowing their boundary values; cuttong off these elements with
r^{-1/2}
decay towards the boundary will necessarily lead to a violation of any uniform bound on the inverse. Thus we cannot use the naive index 0 boundary condition, and must instead allow boundary modes capturing these kernel elements, and project to their orthogonal complement in the correct norm. This problem becomes quite subtle in the 3-dimensional case, when the limiting operator has
\not{D}_{A_{0}}
has an infinite-dimensional kernel in
L^{2}
as is discussed in Section 7. The two dimensional kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}
therefore plays an essential role in this and the following section. It is identified explicitly over the course of the proof.

Proposition
6.1
combined with the relation
5.3
from Proposition
5.6
immediately implies the following result for the un-rescaled operator
\mathcal{N}_{t}
:

Corollary 6.2
.

For every fixed
t_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
, on smooth configurations
(\varphi,a)
satisfying the un-rescaled version of the boundary conditions
\Pi^{H_{\varepsilon}^{1}}(\varphi,a)=0
, the following estimate on the normal disk
\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}
holds uniformly in
t,\varepsilon
.

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}\leq C\|\mathcal{N}_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}+\|\pi^{\ker}_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{2}

Here, the left side denotes (
5.10
) (and does not include
\nabla_{t}
terms) formed using the Euclidean norm. ∎

The remainder of Section 6 is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.1
. Before beginning the proof, it is convenient place the form components
(a_{x}d\widehat{x}+a_{y}d\widehat{y}\ ,\ a_{0}+a_{t}dt)
in a holomorphic context. There are isomorphisms

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}(i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\to}

\displaystyle\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{C})\hskip 78.24507pt\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{0}(i\mathbb{R})\overset{(3)}{\to}\Omega^{1,0}(\mathbb{C})

(6.7)

\displaystyle i(a_{x}d\widehat{x}+a_{y}d\widehat{y})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(a_{y}-ia_{x})d\overline{w}\hskip 56.9055pti(a_{0}+a_{t}dt)\mapsto(a_{0}+ia_{t})dw

(6.8)

on the domain, and

\displaystyle\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{2}(i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{\to}

\displaystyle\Omega^{1,1}(\mathbb{C})\hskip 105.2751pt\Omega^{1}(i\mathbb{R})\overset{(4)}{\to}\Omega^{1,1}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle(ih_{2},ih_{2}d\widehat{x}\wedge d\widehat{y})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(h_{1}-ih_{2})dw\wedge d\overline{w}\hskip 25.6073pti(pd\widehat{x}+q\widehat{d}y)\mapsto(p+iq)dw\wedge d\overline{w}

on the codomain. Setting

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(a_{0}+ia_{t})dw

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(a_{y}-ia_{x})d\overline{w}

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}
may be considered as an operator
\Gamma(\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\Omega^{1,0}\oplus\Omega^{0,1})\to\Gamma(\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\Omega^{1,1}\oplus\Omega^{1,1})

now given by

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}&\gamma(\ \ )\Phi^{H}\\
\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\ ,\Phi^{H})\\
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\ ,\Phi^{H})\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&2\partial\\
-2\overline{\partial}&0\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\\
\zeta\\
\omega\end{pmatrix}

where Clifford multiplication becomes

\gamma(pdw,qd\overline{w})=\begin{pmatrix}ip&-\overline{q}\\
-q&i\overline{p}\end{pmatrix}

(6.9)

and the moment map on
\psi=(\alpha,\beta)
is

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi,\Phi^{H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}+\overline{\beta}_{1}\beta_{1}^{H}-\alpha_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}+\overline{\beta}_{2}\beta_{2}^{H}

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\psi,\Phi^{H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta^{H}_{1}-\beta_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}-\overline{\alpha}_{2}\beta^{H}_{2}-\beta_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}

(6.11)

its adjoint as before. We view
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}
in this guise for the remainder of Section 6. We also leave the
t
-dependence implicit for the remainder of the section.

6.2
Fredholm Theory

This subsection discusses Fredholm theory for Dirac operators in two different contexts: 1) as boundary-value problems with Atiyah-Patodi-Singer boundary conditions, and 2) on non-compact domains with polynomially weighted Sobolev spaces. The proof of Proposition
6.1
will require both of these perspectives, as the weighted spaces are needed for estimates to be uniform in the radius
\mathbb{r}
. Since it suffices for our purposes, the discussion here is limited to the relevant cases of the operators
\partial,\overline{\partial}
on the disk; the reader is referred to
[
16
,
18
,
23
]
for more general discussions.

APS Boundary Conditions

First, we consider boundary-value problems for
\partial,\overline{\partial}
. Let
D\subseteq\mathbb{C}
denote the unit disk, and
L^{k,2}(D;\mathbb{C})
the standard Sobolev spaces of complex-valued functions. The continuous restriction or trace map

\text{Tr}:L^{k,2}(D;\mathbb{C})\to L^{k-1/2,2}(\partial D;\mathbb{C})

gives functions well-defined boundary values for
k\geq 1
. Within the space of boundary values for
k=1
, we have the half-dimensional subspaces

\displaystyle H^{+}_{[m]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{u\in L^{1/2,2}\ |\ u=\sum_{k\geq m}a_{k}e^{ik\theta}\}\subseteq L^{1/2,2}(\partial D,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle H^{-}_{[m]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{u\in L^{1/2,2}\ |\ u=\sum_{k\leq m}a_{k}e^{ik\theta}\}\subseteq L^{1/2,2}(\partial D,\mathbb{C})

of functions whose Fourier series have non-vanishing components only on the positive and negative sides of
m\in\mathbb{Z}
(inclusive) respectively. We denote the projections to these spaces by

\Pi_{[m]}^{\pm}:L^{1/2,2}(\partial D;\mathbb{C})\to H^{\pm}_{[m]}

(6.12)

respectively.

Now consider
\overline{\partial}
on
D
. Its (infinite dimensional) kernel consists of holomorphic functions on the disk, whose boundary values lie in
H^{+}_{[0]}
. The following two propositions are standard results, whose proofs can be found in
[
23
]
page 85.

Proposition 6.3
.

The operator

(\overline{\partial},\Pi^{+}_{[0]}):L^{1,2}(D;\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(D;\mathbb{C})\oplus H^{+}_{[0]}

(6.13)

is invertible, and,
a fortiori
, Fredholm of Index 0.

∎

More generally,

Proposition 6.4
.

The operator

(\overline{\partial},\Pi^{+}_{[m]}):L^{1,2}(D;\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(D;\mathbb{C})\oplus H^{+}_{[m]}

(6.14)

has

•

(if
m>0
) empty cokernel and kernel of dimension
m
spanned by
\{1,z,\ldots,z^{m-1}\}
.

•

(if
m<0
) empty kernel and cokernel of dimension
-m
spanned by
\{(0,e^{-i\theta}),\ldots,(0,e^{-im\theta})\}.

∎

The corresponding statement holds for the anti-holomorphic case

(\partial,\Pi^{-}_{[m]}):L^{1,2}(D;\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(D;\mathbb{C})\oplus H^{-}_{[m]},

(6.15)

and for the Sobolev spaces
L^{k,2}
for
k>1
.

Alternatively, one may consider restricting to the space of functions on which the boundary values are 0. Denote the kernel of the projection by

L^{1,2}_{m,+}:=\{u\in L^{1,2}\ |\ \Pi^{+}_{[m]}(u)=0\}

and similarly for
L^{1,2}_{m,-}
. To keep the notation clear, the reader may find it helpful to read
L^{1,2}_{m,+}
as “
L^{1,2}
functions whose restriction to the boundary has vanishing Fourier components on the
+
side of
m
(inclusive)”. Proposition
6.4
becomes the following statement.

Proposition 6.5
.

The operator

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}:L^{1,2}_{m,+}(D;\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle L^{2}(D;\mathbb{C})

is Fredholm with

•

(if
m\geq 0
) empty cokernel and kernel of dimension
m
spanned by
\{1,z,\ldots,z^{m-1}\}
.

•

(if
m<0
) empty kernel and cokernel of dimension
-m
spanned by
\{1,\overline{z},\ldots,\overline{z}^{m-1}\}
.

and similarly for the anti-holomorphic case.

Proof.

The statement about the kernels follows directly from Proposition
6.4
. To see the cokernel is as stated, let
m<0
and
\varphi\in\text{coker}(\overline{\partial})
. Then intergration by parts shows that
\forall u\in L^{1,2}_{m,+}
.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D}\langle\overline{\partial}u,\varphi\rangle dz\wedge d\overline{z}=-\int_{D}\langle u,\partial\varphi\rangle dz\wedge d\overline{z}-\int_{\partial D}\langle u,\varphi\rangle re^{-i\theta}d\theta.

Varying
u
over compactly supported functions, we see
\partial\varphi=0
on the interior of
D
. On the boundary,

u|_{\partial D}=....+u_{m-2}e^{i(m-2)\theta}+u_{m-1}e^{i(m-1)\theta}

hence varying
u
over functions with such boundary values shows that
\varphi
satisfies

\begin{cases}\partial\varphi=0\\
\Pi^{-}_{[m]}(\varphi)=0.\ \end{cases}

Note the
+1
shift in the boundary values resulting from the
e^{i\theta}
factor in the boundary integral. The form of the cokernel then follows from the statement about the kernel for
\partial
with the above boundary conditions.
∎

Polynomial Weights

When considered on all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the operators
\partial,\overline{\partial}:L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
are not Fredholm, as they have dense spectrum at zero, and therefore fail to have closed range. The same phenomenon prevents the inverse on finite disks from being uniformly bounded in the size of the disk. In order to get a Fredholm problem on the entire plane, one must use polynomially weighted spaces. These same weights make the required estimates uniform in the radius of the disk. Here again, we content ourselves with an exposition within the scope of our purposes. The general theory is that of elliptic operators on manifolds with cylindrical ends, which can be found in
[
16
]
(Chapter 17), or
[
17
,
18
]
.

Let
R:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
be a positive monotonically increasing weight function equal to 1 near the origin and equal to
r
far from the origin. We define weighted norms

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{k,2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(R^{2k}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+\ldots+|u|^{2}\right)R^{2\nu}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

and

Definition 6.6
.

The
Polynomially Weighted Sobolev Spaces

L^{k,2}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{u\ |\ \|u\|_{L^{k,2}_{\nu}}<\infty\}

to be the completion of compactly supported smooth functions with respect to these norms.

It is easy to check, using
0\leq\frac{dR}{dr}\leq 1
that:

Lemma 6.7
.

The map

f\mapsto R^{-\mu+\nu}f

is an isomorphism

L^{k,2}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{k,2}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

inducing an equivalence of norms.

The following result summarizes the Fredholm theory for
\partial,\overline{\partial}
in the non-compact setting
[
17
]
:

Proposition 6.8
.

The operators

\overline{\partial},\partial:L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})

are Fredholm for
\nu\notin\mathbb{Z}
. Specifically,

•

for
\nu\in(0,1)
they are isomorphisms.

•

for
\nu\in(-n-1,-n)
they are surjective with kernel of dimension
n
spanned by
\{1,z,\ldots,z^{n-1}\}
(resp.
\overline{z}
).

•

for
\nu\in(n,n+1)
they injective with cokernel of dimension
n
spanned by
\{1,\overline{z},\ldots,\overline{z}^{n-1}\}
(resp.
z
).

∎

The next proposition is the appropriate version of the first bullet point for disks of finite radius. Combining these weighted spaces with the boundary conditions as in the previous subsection, we have the spaces
\{u\in L^{1,2}_{\nu}\ |\ \Pi^{\pm}_{[m]}u=0\}
. Likewise for
L^{k,2}_{\nu}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

For
\nu=1/2
the
\overline{\partial}
-operator subject to the boundary conditions
\Pi^{+}_{[0]}=0

\overline{\partial}:L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}_{\nu}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})

is invertible, and there is a constant
C
such that

\|u\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}}\leq C\,\|\overline{\partial}u\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
. The corresponding statement holds for
\partial
. In fact, both statements hold for any
\nu\in(0,1)
where the constant
C
may depend on
\nu
.

Proof.

The fact that
\overline{\partial}
is an isomorphism with these boundary conditions follows from Proposition
6.5
, so it suffices to show the uniform estimate here. In fact, by Lemma
6.7
it suffices to show it on the spaces
\{u\in L^{1,2}_{-1}\ |\ \Pi^{+}_{[0]}u=0\}\to L^{2}
with the operator

\displaystyle R^{1/2}\circ\overline{\partial}\circ R^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\left(\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}-\frac{1}{2r}\frac{r}{R}\frac{dR}{dr}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\left(\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)\right)

where
\chi
is a function smoothly rising from
0
at the origin and equal to 1 once
R=r
. To show the estimate, we integrate by parts: for
f\in\{u\in L^{1,2}_{-1}\ |\ \Pi^{+}_{[0]}u=0\}
one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|R^{1/2}\circ\partial\circ R^{-1/2}f|^{2}R^{-1}dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)\ ,\ \partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\partial_{r}f|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)f|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\ \langle\partial_{r}f\ ,\ \frac{1}{r}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)f\rangle+\langle\frac{1}{r}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)f\ ,\ \partial_{r}f\rangle\ rdrd\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\partial_{r}f|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)f|^{2}\ +\langle f,\frac{d\chi}{dr}f\rangle\ dV+\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle f,i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}f\rangle d\theta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\partial_{r}f|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)f|^{2}\ dV+\sum_{k<0}(-k-\tfrac{\chi}{2})|f_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla f|^{2}+\frac{\chi^{2}}{r^{2}}|f|^{2}\ dV

where we have integrated by parts in r, and observed that the boundary term is strictly positive as a result of our boundary conditions, and used that
\frac{d\chi}{dr}>0
. The last line follows because
i\partial_{\theta}-\tfrac{\chi}{2}
is an invertible operator with lowest eigenvalue equal to
\tfrac{\chi}{2}
on every circle of fixed radius. This is the desired estimate except for the fact that the second term is supported away from the origin. To remedy this, we apply the Poincaré inequality to
f
times a large cutoff function equal to 1 where
\chi\neq 1
.

More generally, for
\nu\in(0,1)
the proof is identical replacing
\tfrac{\chi}{2}
by
\chi\nu
. ∎

We also note the following specific corollary in the case when
\nu=-1/2
and the boundary conditions for which the kernel is the constant functions, i.e. on the space
\{u\in L^{1,2}_{-3/2}\ |\ \Pi^{-}_{[-1]}u=0\}
:

Proposition 6.10
.

For
\nu=-1/2
and subject to the boundary
\Pi^{-}_{[-1]}
the operator

\partial:L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}_{\nu}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})

is surjective with kernel equal to the constants. Moreover, the estimate

\|u\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}}\leq C\left(\|\partial u\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\pi^{\text{const}}u\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}}\right)

(6.16)

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
once
\mathbb{r}>>0
where the projection is that arising from the
L^{1,2}_{-3/2}
-inner product. The equivalent also holds for
\nu\in(-1,0)
with the constant being allowed to depend on
\nu
.

Proof.

The statement about surjectivity and the form of the kernel follows again from Proposition
6.5
. By conjugation, it suffices to prove the statement for
\overline{\partial}
. For
\nu=-1/2
, switching weights to
L^{1,2}_{-1}
as in the previous proposition, and integrating by parts again yields the same result with
\chi
replaced by
-\chi
and the boundary sum replaced by
k\leq 0
. The boundary term is therefore still positive, but the
\frac{d\chi}{dr}
term is negative. It is compactly supported, hence we obtain an estimate for a compactly supported operator
K
.

\|u\|_{L^{1,2}_{-3/2}}\leq C_{1}\left(\|\partial u\|_{L^{2}_{-1/2}}+\|Ku\|_{L^{2}_{-1/2}}\right).

Now we proceed by contradiction: assume there were a sequence
u_{n}
on disks of radius
r_{n}
having unit
L^{1,2}_{-3/2}
norm, and violating the inequality to prove with constant
1/n
. The above estimate shows one must have
\|Ku_{n}\|>\tfrac{1}{C_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{n}
and so
u_{n}
must have non-zero portion of its norm on the compact support of
K
. Cutting off
u_{n}
with increasingly large logarithmic cutoff functions
\chi_{n}
shows that
\chi_{n}u_{n}
eventually violates the equivalent inequality with projection to the kernel on all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, contradicting Proposition
6.8
.

The general case of
\nu\in(-1,0)
is follows similarly.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

Notice that if we consider the space
\{u\in L^{1,2}_{-3/2}\ |\ \Pi^{-}_{[0]}u=0\}
with the index 0 boundary conditions the elliptic estimate (
6.16
)
cannot
be made uniform in the radius. Indeed, letting
\mathbb{r}_{n}=n
, taking a logarithmic cutoff function
\chi_{n}
equal to
1
for
r<n/2
and vanishing on the boundary with
|d\chi_{n}|\leq c|\log n|^{-1}/r
, choosing constants
c_{n}
so that
\|c_{n}\chi_{n}\|_{L^{1,2}_{-3/2}}=1
, one can see that the sequence of functions
c_{n}\chi_{n}
satisfy the boundary conditions yet
\|\partial(c_{n}\chi_{n})\|_{L^{2}_{-1/2}}\to 0
, contradicting
6.16
.

The relevance of the two above specific cases to our situation is that the connection
A^{H}
implicitly adds a
\nu=+1/2
weight to
\overline{\partial}
and a
\nu=-1/2
weight to
\partial
. Indeed, recalling from Section
4.1
we wrote

\partial_{A^{H}}=\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\left(\partial_{\rho}+\frac{1}{\rho}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi_{H}}{2}\right)\right)\hskip 28.45274pt\partial_{A^{H}}=\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}\left(\partial_{\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\left(i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\chi_{H}}{2}\right)\right)

where
\tfrac{\chi_{H}}{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}+\rho\partial_{\rho}H
.

The above two propositions translate into the following statements about these operators. In it, we use the space

L^{1,2}_{-1}
whose norm is

\|u\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}=\int|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{|u|^{2}}{R^{2}}dV

Proposition 6.12
.

The operators
\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}},\partial_{A^{H}}
satisfy the following respectively:

(1)
With the Index 0 boundary conditions
\Pi^{+}_{[0]}=0
, the operator

\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}:L^{1,2}_{-1}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})

is invertible and

\|\alpha\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}\leq C\|\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha\|_{L^{2}}

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
.

(2)
With the Index 2 boundary conditions,
\Pi^{+}_{[-1]}=0
the operator

\partial_{A^{H}}:L^{1,2}_{-1}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})

is surjective with kernel of dimension 2, and
