\|\beta\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}\leq C(\|\partial_{A^{H}}\beta\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\beta)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
for
\mathbb{r}>>0
, where
p
is the orthogonal projection to the kernel in the
L^{1,2}_{1}
-inner product.

(3)
More generally, the same statements hold for
\nu\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
, i.e.

\|\alpha\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}}\leq C(\nu)\|\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\hskip 28.45274pt\|\beta\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}}\leq C(\nu)(\|\partial_{A^{H}}\beta\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|p(\beta)\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu-1}})

Proof.

The operator
\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}
has the same form of the operator
R^{1/2}\circ\overline{\partial}\circ R^{-1/2}
, and as in the proof of Proposition
6.9
, this operator acting on
L^{1,2}_{-1}
is equivalent to
\overline{\partial}
acting on
L^{1,2}_{-1/2}
. Item
(1)
therefore follows directly from Proposition
6.9
. The only minor caveat is that the effective weight function for
\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}
is asymptotically exponentially close to
r
not equal to it outside a compact region, but this is of no consequence in the proof as one can easily check.

Likewise,
\partial_{A^{H}}
on
L^{1,2}_{-1}
has the same form as the operator
R^{-1/2}\circ\partial\circ R^{1/2}
acting on the
L^{1,2}_{-3/2}
, and is thus equivalent to the situation of Proposition
6.10
with the same minor caveat, and item (
2
) follows.

More generally,
\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}
with weight
\nu
is equivalent to
\overline{\partial}
with the weight
\nu+1/2
and
\partial_{A^{H}}
is equivalent for
\partial
with weight
\nu-1/2
.
(3)
therefore follows from Proposition
6.9
and Proposition
6.10
in the cases for
\nu\in(0,1)
and
\nu\in(-1,0)
respectively.
∎

6.3
Boundary Conditions for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}

We now give boundary conditions for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
on
D_{\mathbb{r}}
. Given that
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
consists of the operators
\partial,\overline{\partial}
and lower order terms, it would be natural to consider
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
acting on the following Sobolev spaces:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}L^{1,2}_{0,+}\ (D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\ \ \\
\oplus\\
L^{1,2}_{0,-}\ (D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\ \ \ \\
\oplus\\
L^{1,2}_{0,+}\ (D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{1,0})\ \\
\oplus\\
L^{1,2}_{0,-}\ (D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{0,1})\end{matrix}\ \ \ \ \overset{\widehat{\mathcal{N}}}{\longrightarrow}\ \ \ \ \begin{matrix}L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\ \ \\
\oplus\\
L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\ \ \ \\
\oplus\\
L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{1,1})\ \\
\oplus\\
L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{1,1}).\end{matrix}

(6.17)

Indeed, the above discussion of APS boundary conditions for the
\partial,\overline{\partial}
operators shows that when the zeroth-order terms are omitted from
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, the resulting operator is invertible on the above spaces, and thus
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
is index 0 (since the off diagonal terms are compact on the compact domain
D_{\mathbb{r}}
). Explicitly, this space is comprised of tuples
(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega)
having Fourier expansions on the boundary in which
\alpha,\zeta
have only negative Fourier modes, and
\beta,\omega
have only positive ones.

The actual boundary conditions we will take are a slight modification of the above. We will expand the above space by allowing the
\beta
component to have a constant Fourier mode
\beta_{0}
on the boundary, and restrict it by disallowing a particular linear combination of the the
\alpha_{-1}
and
\beta_{0}
modes. As in Remark
6.11
, the index 0 boundary conditions allows the space to contain kernel elements that decay towards the boundary necessarily violating any uniform estimates. Notationally, this shift in the boundary values of
\beta
is also necessitated by the
e^{-i\theta}
on the
\beta
-component of
\Phi^{H}
.

We now define these boundary conditions in terms of projection operators. Let

\Pi^{\pm}_{[m]}
be the boundary projections defined by (
6.12
) in Section
6.2
. We also define a two-dimensional projection

\mu^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}:L^{1,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};S_{E}\otimes(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1}))\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

given on a spinor
(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega)
as follows. Let
a_{1},a_{2}
be the components of the
e^{-i\theta}
boundary mode of
\alpha
, and
b_{1},b_{2}
be the components of the constant boundary mode of
\beta
so that

\alpha_{-1}=a_{1}\otimes 1+a_{2}\otimes j\hskip 28.45274pt\beta_{0}=b_{1}\otimes 1+b_{2}\otimes j

(6.18)

where the subscript on the left hand sides denotes the Fourier mode. Then

\mu^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega\right)=b_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}+\overline{a}_{1}\beta_{1}^{H}+b_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}+\overline{a}_{2}\beta_{2}^{H}.

(6.19)

Here,
\alpha_{i}^{H}
and
\beta_{i}^{H}
are the components of
\Phi^{H}
restricted to the boundary (the subscripts on these denote the
\otimes 1
and
\otimes j
components, not the Fourier modes).

Definition 6.13
.

We define the
twisted boundary conditions
for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
by the requirement

\widehat{\Pi}^{H^{1}}=0

where

\widehat{\Pi}^{H^{1}}:L^{1,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};S_{E}\otimes(\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{1}))\longrightarrow H^{+}_{[0]}\oplus H^{-}_{[-1]}\oplus H^{+}_{[0]}\oplus H^{-}_{[0]}\oplus\mathbb{C}

is given by

\widehat{\Pi}^{H^{1}}:=\Pi^{+}_{[0]}\oplus\Pi^{-}_{[-1]}\oplus\Pi^{+}_{[0]}\oplus\Pi^{-}_{[0]}\oplus\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}.

Explicitly, the boundary conditions require that tuples
(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega)
have boundary Fourier expansions of the following form:

Fourier mode

\displaystyle\ldots{\underline{k=-2}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\underline{k=-1}}\hskip 22.76228pt{\underline{k=0}}\ \hskip 8.5359pt{\underline{k=1}}\ \ \ \hskip 7.11317pt{\underline{k=2}}\ \ldots\hskip 48.36958pt

(6.20)

\displaystyle\alpha|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\alpha_{-1}e^{-i\theta}

(6.21)

\displaystyle\beta|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 122.34692pt\beta_{0}\ +\ \beta_{1}e^{i\theta}\ +\ \beta_{2}e^{2i\theta}\ +\ldots

(6.22)

\displaystyle\zeta|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots\zeta_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\zeta_{-1}e^{-i\theta}

(6.23)

\displaystyle\omega|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 122.34692pt0\ \ +\ \ \omega_{1}e^{i\theta}\ +\ \omega_{2}e^{2i\theta}+\ldots.

(6.24)

such that
\alpha_{-1},\beta_{0}
are constrained to linear combinations which satisfy

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}(\alpha_{-1},\beta_{0})=0

(6.25)

with the notation of (
6.18
) and (
6.19
).

This completes the definition of the domain
\widehat{H}^{1}
of the operator
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
advertised in (
6.3
). We can also immediately conclude the first statement of Proposition
6.1
which claimed that
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
with these boundary conditions is a Fredholm operator of real index 2.

Proof of the Index statement in Proposition
6.1
. On the compact domain
D_{\mathbb{r}}
, the zeroth order terms of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
are compact, so it suffices to show the statement for the first order terms. Relative to the Index 0 setting of
6.17
, we have allowed a zeroeth order mode in
\beta
, which one complex dimension for each of the two copies of
\mathbb{C}
in the domain of
\beta
, hence four real dimensions. Since the map
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}:L^{1,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})\to\mathbb{C}
has full rank (which is a consequence of
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
), adding this condition subtracts two from the real index.
∎

6.4
The Holomorphic Interpretation

This subsection proves Proposition
6.1
in the case that
\zeta=0
. In this context, we can interpret the form
\omega
as endowing the vector bundle
\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2}
with a particular holomorphic structure, which is necessarily complex gauge equivalent to the standard one on the disk. Specifically, in this subsection we consider the reduced “holomorphic” operator

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha,\beta,\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}&\gamma(\ \ )\Phi^{H}\\
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\ ,\Phi^{H})&2\partial\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\\
\omega\end{pmatrix},

with the reduced boundary conditions given by

\displaystyle\alpha|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\alpha_{-1}e^{-i\theta}

(6.26)

\displaystyle\beta|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}e^{i\theta}+\beta_{2}e^{2i\theta}\ +\ldots

(6.27)

\displaystyle\omega|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt0\ +\ \omega_{1}e^{i\theta}+\omega_{2}e^{2i\theta}+\ldots.

(6.28)

obtained by omitting the requirements on
\zeta
and
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}
from Equations
6.3
. Let
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(D_{\mathbb{r}})
and
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(D_{\mathbb{r}})
denote the Hilbert Spaces omitting the
\zeta
component and the
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
term in the first, and the fourth factor in
L^{2}
. We will often abbreviate them
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
and
L^{2}_{\mathbb{r}}
. The norm is now given by

\|(\alpha,\beta,\omega)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}}:=\left(\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\nabla(\alpha,\beta,\omega)|^{2}+\frac{|(\alpha,\beta)|^{2}}{R^{2}}+|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}((\alpha,\beta),\Phi^{H})|^{2}+|\omega|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}dV\right)^{1/2}

(6.29)

Proposition 6.14
.

The operator

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}:\widehat{{H}}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(D_{\mathbb{r}})\longrightarrow L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(D_{\mathbb{r}})

is Fredholm of real Index 4. For
\mathbb{r}
sufficiently large, it is surjective with kernel of dimension 4, and there is a projection
\pi^{\psi}:\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that the estimate

\|(\alpha,\beta,\omega)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}\leq C(\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha,\beta,\omega)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\pi^{\psi}(\alpha,\beta)\|)

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
, and
t
.

Proof.

The index statement is immediate from the above discussion of boundary conditions, since we have added 4 real dimensions in the
\beta_{0}
component compared to the index 0 boundary conditions. The remainder of the proof consists of three steps, each of which requires several lemmas.

Step 1 (Complex Gauge Action):
To begin, we decompose the domain into a slice of the complex gauge action and its complement.

Define

T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}:=\{h\in L^{2,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{0}(\mathbb{C}))\ |\ \Pi^{+}_{[0]}(h)=0\text{ and }\Pi^{-}_{[0]}(\overline{\partial}h)=0\}.

(6.30)

to be the
L^{2,2}
configurations with
double APS boundary conditions
. Here
h|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}
is understood via the restriction map
L^{2,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})\to L^{3/2,2}(\partial D_{\mathbb{r}})
and
\overline{\partial}h
via the same with one lower regularity. There is the linearized action at
(\Phi^{H},A^{H})

\mathbb{d}_{(\Phi^{H},A^{H})}:T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}\to\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}

given by

h\mapsto(h\alpha^{H},-\overline{h}\beta^{H},2\overline{\partial}h).

Since we are interested only in a holomorphic description of the linearized operator here, rather than the moduli space of solutions to the non-linear equation, it’s not necessary to introduce the complex gauge group itself. The decomposition of
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
is philosophically decomposing into a slice of the complex gauge action and its complement, but our approach here only retains this philosophy (and suggestive notation) and we do not need to explicitly check the above space is the Lie algebra of a well-defined Hilbert Lie Group.

Remark 6.15
.

A few remarks are in order:

(1) The “double APS” boundary conditions are rather non-standard in the theory of second-order elliptic PDE, but are the natural boundary conditions for the square of a Dirac operator, as they require the boundary term to vanish when integrating by parts. In our case, explicitly, (
6.30
) requires

h|_{\partial D}=\sum_{\ell<0}h_{\ell}e^{i\ell\theta}\hskip 28.45274pt\overline{\partial}h|_{\partial D}=\sum_{\ell>0}a_{\ell}e^{i\ell\theta}

so that the boundary term
\langle e^{\pm i\theta}h,\overline{\partial}h\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial D)}=0
vanishes (see Lemma
6.19
).

More generally, on a manifold with boundary
(X,\partial X)
, one could split
L^{2}(\partial X)=H^{+}\oplus H^{-}
where
H^{\pm}
are respectively the positive and negative eigenspaces of
\not{D}|_{\partial X}
and require
\gamma(\vec{n})\Phi\in H^{-}
and
\not{D}\Phi\in H^{+}

so that the integration by parts formula (
[
16
]
, Lemma 4.5.1)

\int_{X}\langle\Phi,\not{D}\not{D}\Phi\rangle=\int_{X}|\not{D}\Phi|^{2}-\int_{\partial X}\langle\gamma(\vec{n})\Phi,\not{D}\Phi\rangle

has vanishing boundary term.

(2) Note that the Index 0 boundary conditions (
6.17
) do not allow an action of the complex gauge group in the desired way. Writing
h|_{\partial D}=\sum_{\ell<0}h_{\ell}e^{i\ell\theta}
as required by (
6.30
), and using

\Phi^{H}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{H}cr^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}dr^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+...\otimes j\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\hskip 28.45274pth\cdot\Phi^{H}=\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}e^{-i\theta}+f_{2}e^{-2i\theta}+...\\
g_{0}+g_{1}e^{i\theta}+...\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+...\otimes j

so that the
\beta
component of
h\cdot\Phi^{H}
may have a non-zero constant component on the boundary for
h\in T\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{r}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. This is another reason for introducing the twisted boundary conditions.

Lemma 6.16
.

The Linearized action

\mathbb{d}_{(\Phi^{H},A^{H})}:T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}\to{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}

is an isomorphism onto its image, which a graph over the form component
\omega\in L^{1,2}_{-,0}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{C}))
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the projection of
\mathbb{d}_{(\Phi^{H},A^{H})}
to the third component of triples
(\alpha,\beta,\omega)
is an isomorphism. We have that

\overline{\partial}:L^{2,2}_{+,0}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})\to L^{1,2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\Omega^{0,1}(\mathbb{C}))

is an isomorphism, by the discussion following Proposition
6.4
, (see
6.15
). Here again, the domain denotes the space of functions
h
on which
\Pi^{+}_{[0]}h=0
. Thus all that needs to be shown is that adding the second boundary condition to the domain restricts the image to those
L^{1,2}
configurations satisfying the first-order boundary condition, i.e. that

\Pi^{+}_{[0]}h=0\ \text{ and }\Pi^{-}_{[0]}(\overline{\partial}h)=0\Leftrightarrow h\in L^{2,2}_{0,+}\text{ and }\overline{\partial}h\in L^{1,2}_{0,-}

but the left side is exactly the definition of the spaces on the right.
∎

As a consequence of the Lemma, there is a splitting into the tangent directions of the complex gauge action and a horizontal slice complementing it. Explicitly, there is an isomorphsim

\begin{matrix}\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}\\
\oplus\\
T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}\end{matrix}\ \ \overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\ \ {\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}

(6.31)

where

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}=L^{1,2}_{0,+}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\oplus L^{1,2}_{-1,-}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})

are the “horizontal” components of
{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}
. Explicitly, the isomorphism is given by
(Id,\mathbb{d}_{(\Phi^{H},A^{H})})
i.e.

(\psi,h)\mapsto(\psi,0)+(h\alpha^{H},-\overline{h}\beta^{H},\overline{\partial}h).

Conversely, any configuration can be written uniquely
(\alpha,\beta,\omega)=(\psi,0)+(h\cdot\Phi^{H},\overline{\partial}h)
.

Lemma 6.17
.

The operator
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}
acting on triples
(\alpha,\beta,\omega)=(\psi,0)+(h\cdot\Phi^{H},2\overline{\partial}h)
is given by the mixed-order operator

\Box(\psi,h)=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}&0\\
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\Phi^{H})&-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
h\end{pmatrix}

where

\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

The Lemma is a direct computation of

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\end{pmatrix}&\gamma(\ \ )\Phi^{H}\\
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\ ,\Phi^{H})&2\partial\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi+\begin{pmatrix}h\alpha^{H}\\
-\overline{h}\beta^{H}\end{pmatrix}\\
2\overline{\partial}h\end{pmatrix}.

For the spinor component,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi+\begin{pmatrix}h\alpha^{H}\\
-\overline{h}\beta^{H}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\psi-2\partial(-\overline{h})\beta^{H}+2\overline{\partial}(h)\alpha^{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\psi+2\partial\overline{h}\beta^{H}+2\overline{\partial}h\alpha^{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\psi-\gamma(2\partial\overline{h}dw-2\overline{\partial}hd\overline{w})\Phi^{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\psi-\gamma(2\overline{\partial}h)\Phi^{H}

where we’ve expressed Clifford multiplication on
\Omega^{1}
in terms of
\Omega^{0,1}
via
6.9
.
For the form component,

\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi+h\cdot\Phi^{H})+4\partial\overline{\partial}h=-\Delta h+\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi,\Phi^{H})+\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(h\cdot\Phi,\Phi).

And using (
6.11
),

\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(h\cdot\Phi^{H},\Phi^{H})=-h|\alpha_{1}^{H}|^{2}-h|\beta_{1}^{H}|^{2}-h|\alpha_{2}^{H}|^{2}-h|\beta_{2}^{H}|^{2}=-|\Phi^{H}|^{2}.

∎

Step 2: (The Diagonal Terms)
The splitting
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}\oplus T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}
does not respect the norm. The norm on the
{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}
side is

\|(\psi+h\cdot\Phi^{H},2\overline{\partial}h)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}

while the natural norm on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}\oplus T\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{r}}^{\mathbb{C}}
is

\left(\|\psi\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}^{2}+\|h\|_{L^{2,2}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

These two norms are not uniformly equivalent in
\mathbb{r}
. The norm on
{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}
is “larger” in the sense that it contains the
|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})|^{2}
term, while it is “smaller” in the sense that for some configurations
\psi+h\cdot\Phi^{H}
is small, while
\psi,h
are individually large but nearly cancel. This problem becomes more pronounced for as
\mathbb{r}\to\infty
: in regions where
\Phi^{H}
is large, then
h\Phi^{H}
– hence the
\widehat{H}^{1}
norm – is large when
h
is of unit size. Viewing
T\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
as a graph over the
\omega
-component again, this behavior means the slope of the graph diverges for such configurations. To keep track of this we define the following norms on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}},T\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{r}}^{\mathbb{C}}
respectively:

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\nabla\psi|^{2}+\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(6.32)

\displaystyle\|h\|_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\nabla^{2}h|^{2}+|\Phi^{H}|^{2}|\nabla h|^{2}+|\Phi^{H}|^{4}|h|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(6.33)

and the
Graph Norm
on
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}\oplus T\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{r}}^{\mathbb{C}}
by

\|(\psi,h)\|_{Gr}=\left(\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}+\|h\|^{2}_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}\right)^{1/2}.

(6.34)

The proof of Proposition
6.14
, rests on the following abstract lemma which identifies the kernel of
\Box
and provides uniform bounds on the inverse on the complement of the kernel. The lemma references two norms on the domain,
\|-\|
and
\|-\|^{\prime}
, which will be taken to be the Graph norm and
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
norm respectively.

Lemma 6.18
.

Suppose that
(X^{i}_{\mathbb{r}},\|-\|_{X_{i},\mathbb{r}})
and
(Y^{i}_{\mathbb{r}},\|-\|_{Y_{i},\mathbb{r}})
for
i=1,2
are families of Banach spaces parameterized by
\mathbb{r}\in(0,\infty)
. Set
X_{\mathbb{r}}=X_{\mathbb{r}}^{1}\oplus X_{\mathbb{r}}^{2}
and
Y_{\mathbb{r}}=Y^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\oplus Y^{2}_{\mathbb{r}}
and suppose
N:X_{\mathbb{r}}\to Y_{\mathbb{r}}
is a linear operator bounded for each
\mathbb{r}
and admitting a block lower-triangular decomposition as

N=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
B&C\end{pmatrix}.

Then

(1)

Assume that
A:X^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\to Y^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
and
C:X^{2}_{\mathbb{r}}\to Y^{2}_{\mathbb{r}}
are invertible, then
N
is invertible for every
\mathbb{r}
. If instead,
A:X^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\to Y^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
is surjective with kernel of some finite dimension independent of
\mathbb{r}
, then
N
is surjective, and
\dim\ker(N)=\dim\ker(A)
. If
x_{i}\in X_{\mathbb{r}}
for
i=1,...,m
are a basis for
\ker(A)
then

\begin{pmatrix}x_{i}\\
-C^{-1}Bx_{i}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for }i=1,...,m

form a basis of
\ker(N)
. Additionally, if
p:X_{\mathbb{r}}\to V
is a projection to a finite dimensional space restricting to an isomorphism on
\ker(N)
, then

N\oplus p:X_{\mathbb{r}}\to Y_{\mathbb{r}}\oplus V

is an isomorphism.

(2)

Moreover, assume there exists a norm
\|-\|^{\prime}
on
X_{\mathbb{r}}
equivalent for each
\mathbb{r}
to the norm induced by the direct sum. Suppose additionally that there is a family of operators and projections

K:X_{\mathbb{r}}\to Y_{\mathbb{r}}\hskip 28.45274ptp:X_{\mathbb{r}}\to V

respectively, where
p
is as above, satisfying the following estimates:

(
i)

There is a constant
\kappa_{1}
such that

\|Kx\|_{Y_{\mathbb{r}}}\leq\kappa_{1}(\|Nx\|_{\mathbb{r}}+\|px\|_{V})

(
ii)

There is a constant
\kappa_{2}
such that:

\|x\|^{\prime}\leq\kappa_{2}(\|Nx\|_{Y_{\mathbb{r}}}+\|Kx\|_{Y_{\mathbb{r}}})

where
\kappa_{i}
are uniform in
\mathbb{r}
.

Then, denoting
X^{\prime}_{\mathbb{r}}=(X_{\mathbb{r}},\|-\|^{\prime})
, the operator
N:X_{\mathbb{r}}^{\prime}\to Y_{\mathbb{r}}\oplus V
is uniformly invertible, i.e. there is a constant
\kappa
independent of
\mathbb{r}
such that

\|x\|^{\prime}\leq\kappa(\|Nx\|_{Y_{\mathbb{r}}}+\|px\|).

Proof.

The first statement of (1) follows directly from
A,C
being invertible. The inverse is given explicitly by

N^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{-1}&0\\
-C^{-1}BA^{-1}&C^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

If
A
has kernel, but
C
is invertible, the form of the kernel follows directly from the form of
N
. The statement involving
N\oplus p
is immediate.
For assertion (2), the conclusion follows directly from applying the estimate
(ii)
then
(i)
successively.
∎

This lemma will be applied in that case that
N=\Box
with
p
the projection to the kernel of
\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, as suggested by the notation. The remainder of Step 2 focuses on the diagonal terms
A=\not{D}_{A^{H}}
and
C=-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2}
to verify the hypotheses of part 1. of the lemma. The subsequent Step 3 addresses the hypotheses of part 2 of Lemma
6.18
.

The following Integration by parts identities are needed:

Lemma 6.19
.

For
u,v\in T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}
, the following integration by parts formulas hold:

•

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\Delta u,v\rangle\ dV=\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\overline{\partial}u,2\overline{\partial}v\rangle\ dV.

•

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\Delta u,v\rangle\ dV=\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle\ dV+\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle i\partial_{\theta}u,v\rangle\ d\theta

Proof.

One has the following integration by parts formlae for
\partial,\overline{\partial}
:

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\partial u,v\rangle+\langle u,2\overline{\partial}v\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle u,v\rangle\rho e^{i\theta}d\theta

(6.35)

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\overline{\partial}u,v\rangle+\langle u,2\partial v\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle u,v\rangle\rho e^{-i\theta}d\theta

(6.36)

Since for
u\in T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}
,

u=u_{-1}e^{-i\theta}+u_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\ldots\hskip 28.45274pt\overline{\partial}u=f_{1}e^{i\theta}+f_{2}e^{2i\theta}+\ldots

and likewise for
v
, the boundary term

\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\overline{\partial}u,v\rangle\rho e^{i\theta}d\theta=0

vanishes. Consequently,

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\langle\Delta u,v\rangle dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle-4\partial\overline{\partial}u,v\rangle\ dV=\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\overline{\partial}u,2\overline{\partial}v\rangle\ dV

yielding the first equality. For the second recall in polar coordinates
\Delta=-\tfrac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}(\rho\partial\rho)-\tfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}
. Then the equality

d(\langle\partial_{\rho}u,v\rangle\rho d\theta)=(\langle\partial_{\rho}u,\partial_{\rho}v\rangle+\langle\tfrac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}(\rho\partial_{\rho}u),v\rangle)\rho d\rho d\theta

implies

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\Delta u,v\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\partial_{\rho}u,\partial_{\rho}v\rangle+\tfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}\langle\partial_{\theta}u,\partial_{\theta}v\rangle\ dV-\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\partial_{\rho}u,v\rangle\rho d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle-\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\overline{\partial}u,v\rangle e^{-i\theta}\rho d\theta+\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle i\partial_{\theta}u,v\rangle d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle+\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle i\partial_{\theta}u,v\rangle d\theta

where we have used
\overline{\partial}=e^{i\theta}(\partial_{\rho}+\tfrac{i}{\rho}\partial_{\theta})
and observed the boundary term involving
\overline{\partial}
vanishes for the same reason as in the first bullet point.
∎

This next lemma verifies the necessary hypotheses for the operator
C=-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2}
. The lemma after it verifies the same for
A=\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Consider
(T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}},\|-\|_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}})
equipped the norm described in
6.33
. Then

-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2}:T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}\to L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}}\ ;\ \Omega^{1,1})

is uniformly invertible.

Proof.

First observe that the first bullet point of Lemma
7.5
shows
\langle\Delta h,h\rangle
is positive, hence

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h,h\rangle\geq\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}|h|^{2}\ dV\geq c\|h\|_{L^{2}}

since
|\Phi^{H}|
is bounded below uniformly. This operator is therefore positive with a uniform lower bound on the lowest eigenvalue. Consequently, there is a uniform estimate:

\|(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h\|_{L^{2}}\geq c\|h\|_{L^{2}}.

(6.37)

Next, expanding and using the second integration by parts formula from Lemma
6.19
,

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\Delta h|^{2}+|\Phi|^{4}|h|^{2}+2\langle\Delta h,h|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\Delta h|^{2}+|\Phi^{H}|^{4}|h|^{2}+2\langle\nabla h,\nabla(h|\Phi^{H}|^{2})\rangle\ dV+\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}2\langle i\partial_{\theta}h,|\Phi^{H}|^{2}h\rangle d\theta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\Delta h|^{2}+|\Phi^{H}|^{4}|h|^{2}+|\nabla h|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\ dV+\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}2\langle i\partial_{\theta}h,|\Phi^{H}|^{2}h\rangle d\theta

\displaystyle-\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\Big|2\langle\nabla h,h\rangle(\nabla|\Phi^{H}|^{2})\Big|\ dV

and writing
h=\sum_{\ell>0}h_{\ell}e^{-i\ell\theta}
shows

\int_{\partial D}2\langle i\partial_{\theta}h,|\Phi^{H}|^{2}h\rangle d\theta=\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\sum_{\ell>0}\ell|h_{\ell}|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}d\theta\geq 0.

Young’s inequality shows

\displaystyle\langle\nabla h,h\rangle(\nabla|\Phi^{H}|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}|\nabla h|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2\epsilon}|h|^{2}(\nabla|\Phi^{H}|^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla h|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}+C|h|^{2}

for
\epsilon
sufficiently small, since
|\Phi^{H}|
is bounded below uniformly and
|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\sim\rho
so
\nabla|\Phi^{H}|^{2}
is uniformly bounded. Absorbing the first term and moving the second to the the other side yields

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\Delta h|^{2}+|\Phi^{H}|^{4}|h|^{2}+|\nabla h|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h|^{2}\ dV+C\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|h|^{2}\ dV

(6.38)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}|(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h|^{2}\ dV

(6.39)

after applying (
6.37
). To conclude, we note that the estimate

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\nabla^{2}h|^{2}\ dV\leq C\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\Delta h|^{2}\ dV

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
. It is trivial on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
via integration by parts, and if it were not true uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
then on a sequence
h_{n}
of unit norm in
T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{r_{n}}
violating the inequality,
\chi_{n}h_{n}
would violate the inequality on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
for a sequence of large cutoffs.
∎

Lemma 6.21
.

Consider
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}},\|-\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})
equipped with the norm described in
6.33
. Then

\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}\longrightarrow L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{4})

is surjective with kernel of real dimension 4 given by the complex span of

\beta_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\cdot\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\hskip 28.45274pt\beta_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\cdot\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

Moreover, if
p:\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}\to\ker(\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}})
is the orthogonal projection to the kernel (with respect to the
\|-\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}
norm), then

\|\psi\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}\leq C(\|\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}\psi\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\psi)\|)

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
.

Proof.

First, we identify the kernel. It follows from the discussion of APS boundary conditions that

\displaystyle D^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A^{H}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}&0\end{pmatrix}:\begin{matrix}L^{1,2}_{0,+}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\\
\oplus\\
L^{1,2}_{-,-1}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\end{matrix}\longrightarrow\begin{matrix}L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\\
\oplus\\
L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\end{matrix}

is a bounded Fredholm operator of (real) Index 4, since
\gamma(A^{H})
is a compact perturbation. To see the kernel is as claimed, we (complex) gauge transform to the standard complex structure. Let
U=\log(\rho^{1/2})
, and
A_{0}=\tfrac{1}{4}\left(\tfrac{dw}{w}-\tfrac{d\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}\right)
be the singular connection in
\rho
-coordinates. Then we have the relations

\displaystyle e^{U}\cdot\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle e^{H}\cdot A_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{H}

where
\Gamma_{0}
is the trivial connection (the second equality is the definition of
A^{H}
). Thus letting
G=H+U
, the gauge transformation
e^{G}~r^{1/2}
acts by

e^{G}\cdot\Gamma_{0}\mapsto A_{H}.

Notice that
G
is non-singular at the origin, since
U,H
have the same leading order term with opposite signs. Since
G
is rotational invariant, it preserves the boundary values and thus the property of lying in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}
. Consequently

\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}(e^{G}u)=e^{G}\overline{\partial}u\hskip 28.45274pt\partial_{A^{H}}(e^{-G}v)=e^{-G}\partial v

where
\overline{\partial},\partial
are the standard operators on
\mathbb{C}
. Since
e^{\pm G}\neq 0
we see an element
(\alpha,\beta)
is in the kernel if and only if
(e^{-G}\alpha,e^{G}\beta)
are holomorphic and anti-holomorphic respectively in the standard complex structure. Since with the boundary conditions allow no holomorphic functions, and only the constant anti-holomorphic functions, we find the kernel consists of elements
\beta
such that
e^{G}\beta=\text{const}
. The assertion for the form of the kernel follows, and the surjectivity from the index computation.

The uniform estimate is given by Proposition
6.12
, with the inconsequential caveat that the we are free to instead take the weight
R
used in that Proposition
6.12
(which is equal to
\rho
outside a compact region) to be
\sqrt{1+\rho^{2}}
.

∎

Step 3: (Uniform Invertibility)

To obtain the uniform estimate of Proposition
6.14
, we apply part 2. of Lemma
6.18
. In this step, we verify the estimates
(ii)
and then
(i)
. The first,
(i)
is obtained from the solvability of
\Box
, and
(ii)
follows from the Weitzenböck formula. The next two Lemmas establish first
(ii)
and then
(i)
.

We define the projection operator

K:\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\to L^{2}_{\mathbb{r}}

by

K(\varphi,a):=\frac{\varphi}{R}.

(6.40)

Since
\|(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}
contains the term
\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\tfrac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV
,
K
is well-defined and bounded.

Lemma 6.22
.

There is a constant
\kappa_{2}
such that the estimate

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}}\leq\kappa_{2}(\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}+\|K(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}})

(6.41)

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
.

Proof.

This proposition is Weitzenbock formula (Proposition
2.13
) combined with the observation that the cross-term is bounded by
K
. We begin by showing that the chosen boundary conditions have no boundary terms when integrating by parts.

Claim 6.22.1
.

For
(\varphi,\omega)\in\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
one has

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi,\gamma(\omega)\Phi^{H}\rangle\ dV=\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\varphi,\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}\gamma(\omega)\Phi^{H}\rangle\ dV.

Proof.

Explicitly (recalling the expression
6.9
for Clifford multiplication) the left side in terms of the
\alpha,\beta
component of the spinor is

\text{Re}\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle-2\partial_{A^{H}}\beta,-\overline{\omega}\beta^{H}\rangle+\langle 2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha,-\omega\alpha^{H}\rangle\ dV.

Using the integration by parts formulae

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\partial u,v\rangle+\langle u,2\overline{\partial}v\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle u,v\rangle\rho e^{i\theta}d\theta

(6.42)

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\overline{\partial}u,v\rangle+\langle u,2\partial v\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle u,v\rangle\rho e^{-i\theta}d\theta

(6.43)

we see the boundary term is

\text{Re}\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle-\beta,-\overline{\omega}\beta^{H}\rangle e^{i\theta}+\langle\alpha,-\omega\alpha^{H}\rangle e^{-i\theta}\ \rho d\theta

Since
\alpha^{H},\beta^{H}
have only constant and
e^{-i\theta}
boundary modes respectively, and

\displaystyle\alpha|_{\partial D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\alpha_{-1}e^{-i\theta}

\displaystyle\beta|_{\partial D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}e^{i\theta}+\beta_{2}e^{2i\theta}+\ldots

\displaystyle\omega|_{\partial D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \omega_{1}e^{i\theta}+\omega_{2}e^{2i\theta}+\ldots

there are no overlapping Fourier modes on the boundary. This completes the claim.
∎

We may write

\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,a)=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi+\gamma(\omega)\Phi^{H}\\
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})+2\partial\omega\end{pmatrix}.

The above claim combined with the cancellation of the first-order terms as in Proposition [Weitzenbock] shows that

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi)|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi|^{2}+|2\partial\omega|^{2}+|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})|^{2}+|\omega|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}

(6.45)

\displaystyle\ +\ \langle(\varphi,\omega),\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,\omega)\rangle_{L^{2}}\ dV

where the cross-term
\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,\omega)
are as in Proposition
2.13
, i.e.

\mathfrak{B}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
\omega\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-2\omega\cdot\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}\\
2i\langle i\varphi,\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}\rangle\end{pmatrix}

where
\cdot
denotes the contraction of form components (where
\omega=(-\text{Im}(\omega)id\hat{x}+\text{Re}(\omega)id\hat{y})
as in
6.8
), and the bottom component is a 1-form. We now claim the following two estimates:

Claim 6.22.2
.

The estimate

\displaystyle\left(\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R^{2}}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}(\|\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\tfrac{\varphi}{R}\|_{L^{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}(\|\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}+\|K(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}})

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
.

Claim 6.22.3
.

There is a constant
C_{2}
such that

\langle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix},\mathfrak{B}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C_{2}\|K(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})|^{2}+|\omega|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\ dV

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
.

To conclude the proof of Lemma
6.22
, move the
\mathfrak{B}
term to the right side and apply Claim
6.22.3
, then absorb the
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}
and
|\omega|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}
terms. Possibly increasing the constant on the
K(\varphi,a)
term, applying claim
6.22.2
makes the left side into the
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
norm.
∎

We now prove the two claims.

Proof.

(of Claim
6.22.2
)
For the
\alpha
component this follows directly from Proposition
6.9
. For the
\beta
component, integration by parts as in Proposition
6.10
shows such an estimate with an operator
K_{1}
being a term arising from the exponentially decaying curvature
F_{A^{H}}
(which is positive but acts by
-i
on the
\beta
component). This term is dominated by
K
for a sufficiently large
C_{1}
.
∎

Proof.

(of Claim
6.22.3
)

Recall
R=\sqrt{(1+\rho^{2})}
. Observe that there are constants
C_{1},c_{1}
such that

|\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}|^{2}\leq C_{1}R^{-1}\hskip 42.67912ptc_{1}R\leq|\Phi^{H}|^{2}.

The first of these follows since
A^{H},\Phi^{H}
as exponentially close to
A_{0}
and
\Phi_{0}
respectively, and
\nabla_{A_{0}}\Phi_{0}\sim\rho^{-1/2}
since
\Phi_{0}\sim\rho^{1/2}
. Additionally,
\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}
is bounded across the origin. Likewise, the second holds since
|\Phi^{H}|
is non-zero and increasing, and exponentially close to
\Phi_{0}\sim\rho^{1/2}
. Combining these, there is a positive constant
c_{2}<<1
such that

c_{2}|\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}|^{2}R^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{4}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}.

(6.46)

Then

\displaystyle\langle\varphi,-2\omega\cdot\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}\rangle\leq\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{2c_{2}R^{2}}+\frac{|\omega|^{2}}{2}c_{2}|\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}|^{2}R^{2}\leq c_{3}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{1}{4}|\omega|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}

and identically for the second component. The claim follows.
∎

As a consequence, hypothesis
(ii)
in the abstract Lemma
6.18
is satisfied. The following final lemma establishes hypothesis
(i)
in the abstract Lemma
6.18
:

Lemma 6.23
.

There is a constant
\kappa_{1}
such that the estimate

\|K(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\kappa_{1}(\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})

(6.47)

holds uniformly in
\mathbb{r}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(y_{1},y_{2})
satisfy
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,a)=(y_{1},y_{2})
. Then writing
(\varphi,a)=(\psi+h\cdot\Phi^{H},\overline{\partial}h)
one has

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{H}}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h+\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\psi,\Phi^{H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{2}

Since
K\varphi=\tfrac{\varphi}{R}
times a constant, it suffices to show

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|\psi+h\cdot\Phi^{H}|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV\leq C(\|(y_{1},y_{2})\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}).

One has

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|\psi+h\cdot\Phi^{H}|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{|h|^{2}|\Phi|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}+\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|h|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|y_{1}\|^{2}_{2}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}^{2}+\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|h|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}}{R^{2}}\ dV.

where we have applied Proposition
6.21
. We wish to show a uniform bound on the second term when

(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h=\mu(\psi,\Phi^{H})-y_{2}.

To see this, note that the right hand side may be written as
R^{3/2}f
for a function
f\in L^{2}
with
\|f\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\|(y_{1},y_{2})\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})
, since

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\Big|\frac{\mu(\psi,\Phi^{H})-y_{2}}{R^{3/2}}\Big|^{2}\ dV\leq C\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{R^{2}}+|y_{2}|^{2}\ dV\leq C(\|(y_{1},y_{2})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|p(\psi)\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})

where
C
is such that
|\Phi^{H}|\leq Cr^{1/2}
. The second inequality follows from applying the uniform estimate on
\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}
from Proposition
6.21
again.

Now let
h_{1}\in T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}
be the unique solution of
(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h_{1}=f
. By the definition of the norm on
T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}
one has
\|R^{1/2}\nabla h_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\||\Phi^{H}|^{2}\nabla h_{1}\|_{L^{2}}
so
R^{1/2}\nabla h_{1}\in L^{2}
, and clearly
|R^{-1/2}h_{1}|\in L^{2}
. Thus

\Delta R^{3/2}h_{1}+\nabla R^{3/2}\cdot\nabla h_{1}\in L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}};\mathbb{C})

and its
T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}
norm is bounded by a constant time
\|(y_{1},y_{2})\|_{2}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}
. Now let
g\in T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{r}}
be the unique solution of

(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})g=\Delta R^{3/2}h_{1}+\nabla R^{3/2}\cdot\nabla h_{1}

satisfying the given boundary conditions, which also satisfies
\|g\|_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}\leq C(\|(y_{1},y_{2})\|_{2}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})
and define

h:=R^{3/2}h_{1}-g.

Then

\displaystyle(\Delta+|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{3/2}(\Delta h_{1}+|\Phi^{H}|^{2}h_{1})+\Delta R^{3/2}h_{1}+\nabla R^{3/2}\cdot\nabla h_{1}-(\Delta+\|\Phi^{H}|^{2})g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{3/2}f

is the unique solution sought. And it now follows that

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|h\cdot\Phi^{H}|^{2}}{R^{2}}\leq C\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{|h|^{2}}{R}\ dV\leq C_{1}\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}R^{2}|h_{1}|^{2}+\frac{|g|^{2}}{R}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}\|h_{1}\|^{2}_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}+\|g\|^{2}_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{3}(\|(y_{1},y_{2})\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}}).

∎

We can now conclude the proof of Proposition
6.14
. Indeed, Lemmas
6.20
and
6.21
show that the hypothesis of part (1) of Lemma
6.18
are satisfied for
N=\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}=\Box
. Subsequently, Lemmas
6.23
and
6.22
show that the two hypothesis of part (2) are satisfied for
K
as defined in Equation (
6.40
) and
p
as in Lemma
6.21
. The proposition now follows from applying Lemma
6.18
.

∎

6.5
The General Case

This subsection completes the proof of Proposition
6.1
by deducing the general case from the case that
\zeta=0
studied in the previous subsection. This involves two steps: first an integration by parts that shows the terms arising from
\zeta
are strictly positive, and second, replacing the projection to the 4-dimensional kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}
with the 2-dimensional one of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
.

Step 1:

We have the following integration by parts:

Lemma 6.24
.

\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\varphi,\omega,\zeta)|^{2}\ dV=\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\omega,0)|^{2}+|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})|^{2}+|\nabla\zeta|^{2}+|\zeta|^{2}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\ dV

Proof.

We may write

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\varphi,\omega,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\omega)+\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(\zeta)\Phi^{H}\\
0\\
-2\overline{\partial}\zeta+\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{H}}^{\mathbb{C}}&\gamma(\ \ )\Phi^{H}\\
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\ ,\Phi^{H})&2\partial\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
\omega\\
\zeta\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(\zeta)\Phi^{H}\\
0\\
-2\overline{\partial}\zeta+\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})\end{pmatrix}.

Next, we integrate by parts to show the cross terms cancel, as in the Weitzenböck formula Proposition
2.13
. The cross terms are:

2\text{Re}\langle-2\partial_{A^{H}}\beta\ ,\ i\zeta\alpha^{H}\rangle\hskip 28.45274pt2\text{Re}\langle 2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha\ ,\ i\overline{\zeta}\beta^{H}\rangle\hskip 28.45274pt2\text{Re}\langle-2\overline{\partial}\zeta\ ,\ \mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})\rangle.

(6.48)

Provided we may integrate by parts with no boundary terms, the lemma follows from the same cancellation that occurs is Proposition
2.13
after it is pushed through the appropriate isomorphisms with the original form expressions via
6.8
. In order to show the boundary term vanishes, note that the allowed boundary modes are illustrated by

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle-2\partial_{A^{H}}\beta\ ,\ i\zeta\alpha^{H}\rangle+\langle\beta\ ,\ -2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}i\zeta\alpha^{H}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\beta,i\zeta\alpha^{H}\rangle\rho e^{i\theta}d\theta

(6.49)

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle 2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha\ ,\ i\overline{\zeta}\beta^{H}\rangle+\langle\alpha\ ,\ 2\partial_{A^{H}}i\overline{\zeta}\beta^{H}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}\langle\alpha,i\overline{\zeta}\beta^{H}\rangle\rho e^{-i\theta}d\theta

(6.50)

and recall the boundary conditions require

\displaystyle\alpha|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\alpha_{-1}e^{-i\theta}

(6.51)

\displaystyle\beta|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}e^{i\theta}+\beta_{2}e^{2i\theta}\ +\ldots

(6.52)

\displaystyle\zeta|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots\zeta_{-2}e^{-2i\theta}+\zeta_{-1}e^{-i\theta}

(6.53)

\displaystyle\omega|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt0\ +\ \omega_{1}e^{i\theta}+\omega_{2}e^{2i\theta}+\ldots.

(6.54)

and, writing

\alpha_{-1}=a_{1}\otimes 1+a_{2}\otimes j\hskip 28.45274pt\beta_{0}=b_{1}\otimes 1+b_{2}\otimes j

where the subscript on the left hand sides denotes the Fourier mode, it is additionally required that

0=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}(\alpha,\beta)=b_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}+\overline{a}_{1}\beta_{1}^{H}+b_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}+\overline{a}_{2}\beta_{2}^{H}.

Using (
6.51
)-(
6.54
), most modes on right hand side of (
6.49
) and (
6.50
) vanish. The real part of the boundary term becomes

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Re}\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}-b_{1}\overline{(i\zeta_{-1})}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}-b_{2}\overline{(i\zeta_{-1})}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}+a_{1}\overline{(i\overline{\zeta}_{-1})}\overline{\beta}_{1}^{H}+a_{2}\overline{(i\overline{\zeta}_{-1})}\overline{\beta}_{2}^{H}\rho d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\text{Re}(b_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}(i\overline{\zeta}_{-1})+b_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}(i\overline{\zeta}_{-1})+a_{1}\overline{\beta}_{1}^{H}(-i\zeta_{-1}+a_{2}\overline{\beta}_{2}^{H}(-i\zeta_{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\text{Re}(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}(\alpha,\beta)\cdot(i\overline{\zeta}_{-1}))=0

where we have conjugated the second two terms. Thus the boundary term is 0 when integrating by parts.

Then since
\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha^{H}=\partial_{A^{H}}\beta^{H}=0
, the cross term vanishes by the cancellation in the Weitzenbock formula. Indeed, in this context, one has

\displaystyle\text{Re}(\langle-2\partial_{A^{H}}\beta\ ,\ i\zeta\alpha^{H}\rangle+\langle 2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}\alpha\ ,\ i\overline{\zeta}\beta^{H}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Re}(\langle\beta,2\partial_{A^{H}}(i\overline{\zeta}\alpha^{H})\rangle+\langle\beta,-2\overline{\partial}_{A^{H}}(i\zeta\alpha^{H})\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Re}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}-\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \begin{pmatrix}-i2\overline{\partial}\zeta&0\\
0&-i2\overline{\overline{\partial}\zeta}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{H}\\
\beta^{H}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Re}\Big\langle\mu_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{H}\\
\beta^{H}\end{pmatrix}\right)\ ,\ -2\overline{\partial}\zeta\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\text{Re}\langle-2\overline{\partial}\zeta,\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})\rangle

since

\mu_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{H}\\
\beta^{H}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\beta_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}+\overline{\alpha}_{1}\beta_{1}^{H}+\beta_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}+\overline{\alpha}_{2}\beta_{2}^{H}=-\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi,\Phi^{H})

thus the cross terms (
6.48
) cancel after integrating by parts.
∎

It now follows from the above identity of Lemma
6.24
in conjunction with the result for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, Proposition
6.14
that the estimate

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}\leq C(\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}+\|p(\psi)\|_{L^{1,2}_{-1}})

(6.55)

holds for
C
independent of
\mathbb{r}
.

Step 2:
The final step is to adjust the projection
p:\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
to one valued in
\mathbb{C}
(denoted by the same letter). The point here is simply that when adding the
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
term, only two dimensions of the four (real) dimension are still kernel elements.

Recall that the elements of the four (real) dimensional kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
can be written as the complex span of

\beta_{i}=\beta_{i}^{\circ}+h_{i}\cdot\Phi^{H}

where

\beta_{1}^{\circ}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\cdot\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\hskip 28.45274pt\beta^{\circ}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\cdot\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

(6.56)

and
h_{i}
is the unique solution of

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h_{i}=-\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\beta_{i}^{\circ},\Phi^{H})

where
h
satisfies the boundary conditions of (
6.30
).

Since
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
is complex gauge invariant, one has
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(h\cdot\Phi^{H},\Phi^{H})=0
, hence

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\beta_{i},\Phi^{H})=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\beta_{i}^{\circ},\Phi^{H})

The expressions (
6.56
) and the form of
\Phi^{H}
show that for an kernel element
k_{1}\beta_{1}+k_{2}\beta_{2}
, one has

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(k_{1}\beta_{1}+k_{2}\beta_{2},\Phi^{H})=-k_{1}e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\cdot\overline{c}e^{H}\rho^{1/2}-k_{2}e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\cdot(-d)e^{H}\rho^{1/2}=-k_{1}\overline{c}+k_{2}d

is constant on
D_{\mathbb{r}}
.

Assumption
2
implies
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
which shows that

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}:\ker(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathbb{C}

has full rank, and it is complex linear on the span of
\beta_{i}^{\circ}
. Let
\beta_{t}
be an element whose complex span is the subset
\{\beta\in\ker(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}})\ |\ \mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\beta)=0\}
. By construction
\beta_{t}|_{\partial D_{\mathbb{r}}}
satisfies
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}(\beta_{t})=0
, and so satisfies the boundary conditions. It is then not hard to show (argue by contradiction), that the four dimensional projection
p(\psi)
can be replaced by the two dimensional one
p^{\text{ker}}(\psi)
. The details are omitted since in the next subsection we replace
p(\psi)
with a projection that is more natural for the
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
-norm, rather than
p(\psi)
which is natural in the graph norm. This concludes the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

∎

6.6
The
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection

The final detail to consider is switching the projection
p^{\ker}
, which is natural in the “graph” decomposition
(\psi,h)
from (
6.31
), to a projection which is more natural in the pair
(\varphi,a)
. From here on we fix the size of the neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
to have radius

\boxed{\lambda=\varepsilon^{1/2}.}

so that
\mathbb{r}=(K(t))^{2/3}\varepsilon^{-1/6}
.

Let
\beta_{t}
continue to denote the kernel of
\mathcal{N}_{t}
for every
t\in S^{1}
.

Definition 6.25
.

The (normalized)
L^{2}
- kernel projection
\pi_{t,\nu}^{\text{ker}}:{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon,}(D_{\lambda})\to\mathbb{C}
is defined to be

\pi_{t}^{\text{ker}}(\varphi,a)=\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\frac{\langle(\varphi,a),\beta_{t}\rangle}{\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}}dV

The denominator normalizes it so that
\pi_{t}^{\ker}(\beta_{t})=1
, since
\beta_{t}
is normalized in the
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
norm rather than the
L^{2}
norm.

The version of Proposition
6.1
which we will employ in the next section is then the following.

Corollary 6.26
.

For any fixed
t\in\mathcal{Z}
, assume that
(\varphi,a)\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})
is a configuration satisfying the boundary conditions given in (
6.3
). Then the estimate on the normal disk
\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/12}}\left(\|\mathcal{N}_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}+\|\pi^{\ker}_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{2}\right)

holds for a constant
C
independent of
t,\varepsilon
.

Remark 6.27
.

The above estimate is (obviously) not uniform in
\varepsilon
. By using the natural orthogonal projection in
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})
, it is straighforward to obtain a uniform estimate. Using the
L^{2}
-norm is essential in the next section, however, where the projection must be controlled by the
t
-derivatives
\|\nabla_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}
. Finally, we remark that the constant
\varepsilon^{-1/12}
depends on our choice of
\lambda=\varepsilon^{1/2}
; for
\lambda=\varepsilon^{\alpha}
, the power of
\varepsilon
that appears in the estimate is
(\tfrac{\alpha}{2}-\tfrac{1}{3})
so the estimate becomes uniform as the radius approaches the invariant radius
O(\varepsilon^{2/3})
. However, it can never be uniform and also allow an intermediate region where the exponential decay estimates from Corollary
1.3
to apply. Our choice of
\alpha=1/2
is purely aesthetic, and any
\tfrac{2}{3}>\alpha>\tfrac{1}{3}
would work.

Proof.

By the scaling invariance in
5.3
and Proposition
5.6
, it suffices to show the estimate

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}(D_{\mathbb{r}})}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/12}}\left(\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}(D_{\mathbb{r}})}+\|\pi^{\ker}_{t}(\varphi,a)\|_{2}\right)

in the invariant scale instead. Here we have not scaled the projection, and in a slight abuse of notation we have abbreviated
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)
by still denoting it
(\varphi,a)
.

We proceed now by contradiction using
6.55
. Suppose there is no such
C
satisfying the conclusion. Then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is an
\varepsilon_{n}
and an element
(\varphi_{n},a_{n})
of unit
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
norm on the disk of radius
r_{n}=K(t)^{2/3}\varepsilon_{n}^{-1/6}
such that

\frac{\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}\geq\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\varphi_{n},a_{n})\|_{L^{2}}+\|\pi^{\text{ker}}(\varphi_{n})\|.

We may write
(\varphi_{n},a_{n})=(\psi_{n}+h_{n}\cdot\Phi^{H},\omega_{n},\zeta_{n})
where
\omega_{n}=2\overline{\partial}h_{n}
. Since
p(\psi)\neq 0
else
6.55
would be violated,
\psi
must have some component in the kernel elements. Write
\psi_{n}=\psi_{n}^{\text{ker}}+\xi_{n}
the orthogonal decomposition in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{r}}
so that
p(\xi_{n})=0
. Writing
h_{n}=h_{n}^{\text{ker}}+h_{n}^{\prime}
, the element can be expressed

(\varphi_{n},a_{n})=(\psi_{n}^{\text{ker}}+h^{\text{ker}}_{n}\cdot\Phi^{H},0,0)+(\xi_{n}+h_{n}^{\prime}\cdot\Phi^{H}\ ,\ \omega_{n}\ ,\ \zeta_{n}).

where the first is in the kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

The equality

\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\varphi_{n},\omega_{n},\zeta_{n})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi_{n},\omega_{n},0)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi_{n},\Phi^{H})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\zeta_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\zeta_{n}|\Phi^{H}|\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(6.57)

from Lemma
6.24
, shows that the
L^{2}
norm of each term on the right hand side must be individually less than
\tfrac{\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}
. In particular, from the first term,

\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\xi_{n}+h_{n}^{\prime}\cdot\Phi^{H},\omega_{n},0)\|=\|\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\varphi_{n},\omega_{n},0)\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}

and since
p(\xi_{n})=0
vanishes on
\xi_{n}
by construction, and trivially on
(h_{n}^{\prime}\cdot\Phi^{H},\omega_{n},\zeta_{n})
since
p
does not see those components, the result for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}
(Proposition
6.14
), combined with the bounds on the third and fourth terms implies a bound on all the parts of the
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{r}}
-norm except the
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
part. That is, letting
\phi_{n}=\xi_{n}+h_{n}^{\prime}\cdot\Phi^{H}

\|(\phi_{n},\omega_{n})\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}}+\|\nabla\zeta_{n}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\zeta_{n}|\Phi^{H}|\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}

(6.58)

for
C
independent of
\mathbb{r}
. Recall in this that the
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
-norm is given by omitting the
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
component, as in (
6.29
).

Now write

\beta^{\text{ker}}_{n}=\psi^{\text{ker}}_{n}+h^{\text{ker}}_{n}\cdot\Phi^{H}

for the kernel element, and decompose it

\beta_{n}^{\text{ker}}=k_{n}\beta_{t}+j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}

(6.59)

where
\beta_{t}
is the true kernel element and
\beta_{t}^{\perp}
is the element in the kernel of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathbb{C}}
not satisfying
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=0
normalized in the
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
-norm.

Next, we claim
j_{n}
is small. On the unit disk, we have

\int_{D_{1}}|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}+\phi_{n},\Phi^{H})|^{2}dV\leq\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}+\phi_{n},\Phi^{H})|^{2}dV\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2/12}}{n^{2}}

but on the unit disk,
\Phi^{H},R
are universally bounded, hence
|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\phi_{n},\Phi^{H})|\leq C|\phi_{n}|\leq C\tfrac{|\phi_{n}|}{R}
on
D_{1}
, for universal constants. Therefore (
6.58
) implies

\|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\phi_{n},\Phi^{H})\|_{L^{2}(D_{1})}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}

on the unit disk, thus since
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\beta^{\perp}_{t},\Phi^{H})
is constant on the unit disk, we must have
j_{n}\leq\tfrac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}
, and thus
\|j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}
.

Combining this with (
6.58
) and the inequality on the second term of (
6.57
), we have

\displaystyle\|(j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}+(\phi_{n},\omega_{n},\zeta_{n}))\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}+(\phi_{n},\omega_{n})\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}}+\|\nabla\zeta_{n}\|_{L^{2}}

(6.61)

\displaystyle\ +\ \|\zeta_{n}|\Phi^{H}|\|_{L^{2}}+\|\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi_{n},\Phi^{H})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}+\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}.

(6.62)

In the last term we have used that
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi_{n},\Phi^{H})=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}(j_{n}\beta_{t}^{\perp}+\phi_{n},\Phi^{H})
since
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
vanishes on
\beta_{t}
.

Finally, we are able to conclude that

\|k_{n}\beta_{t}\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}\geq 1-\tfrac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}

and so
|k_{n}|\geq 1-\tfrac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}
as well. This now yields a contradiction to
\|\pi_{t}^{\text{ker}}(\varphi_{n},a_{n})\|\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}
. Indeed, writing
(\varphi_{n},a_{n})=k_{n}\beta_{t}+q_{n}
then (
6.62
) and re-scaling back shows that
\|q_{n}\|_{\widehat{H}^{1}}=\|\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}q_{n}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leq\tfrac{C\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}
. Then by Cauchy-Schwartz, working now on the disk of radius
\varepsilon^{1/2}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{1/12}}{n}\geq|\pi_{t}^{\text{ker}}(\varphi_{n},a_{n})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{D_{\mathbb{r}}}\frac{\langle k_{n}\beta_{t}+\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}q_{n},\beta_{t}\rangle}{|\beta_{t}|_{L^{2}}^{2}}dV\Big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|k_{n}|-\frac{\|\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}q_{n}\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}}\cdot\frac{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|k_{n}|-\|\tfrac{\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}q_{n}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/2}\tfrac{1}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|k_{n}|-\|\overline{\Upsilon}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}q_{n}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\tfrac{\varepsilon^{1/2}}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}}

but,
\beta_{t}\sim\rho^{-1/2}
(the detailed proof of this is given in the next section in Lemma
6.28
), so it
\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}\geq c\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}
. Therefore, the latter term is bounded by a constant times
\tfrac{1}{n}
, while
k_{n}\sim 1
, yielding a contradiction. This completes the proof.

∎

The last order of business is to show the bounds
\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}\geq c\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}
used in the final two sentences of the above proof of Corollary
6.26
, and also that similar bounds hold for the
t
-derivative
\dot{\beta}_{t}
(which are used in Section
7
). These follow from basic integration if one knows that
\beta_{t}\sim\rho^{-1/2}
and similarly for the derivative
\dot{\beta}_{t}
.

Lemma 6.28
.

The elements
\beta_{t}
have non-vanishing leading order term so that

\beta_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{-1/2}

for
\rho_{t}>\!>1
. As a consequence, we have the following bounds where the constants
C,c,\kappa_{1}
are independent of
\varepsilon,t

1)

c\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}\leq\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}

2)

For
\rho_{t}>\!>1
sufficiently large,
|\dot{\beta}_{t}|\leq\kappa_{1}|\beta_{t}|
pointwise.

3)

{\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}}\leq\kappa_{1}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}}
and
{\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{\lambda})}}\leq\kappa_{1}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{\lambda})}}

As in equation (
6.59
) in the proof of Corollary
6.26
, the kernel element may be decomposed

\beta_{t}=\psi_{t}+h_{t}\cdot\Phi_{0}

where
h_{t}\cdot\Phi_{0}
is the component of the kernel tangent to the complex gauge orbits. It was shown in Lemma
6.21
that
\psi_{t}\sim\rho^{-1/2}
, thus the above lemma simply asserts that
h_{t}
has asymptotics that does not disrupt this. The proof of this is straightforward but slightly intricate, and is given in Appendix
B
.

7
The Linearization

This section proves that in the proper context, the linearization
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
at the de-singularized configuration
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
is invertible. The precise statement is given in the below Theorem
7.1
, which is the main technical result of this article. It proves a precise version of the “proto-theorem” stated in Section 5 below Equation (
5.1
), and the main results Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.4
follow directly from Theorem
7.1
as is shown in Section 8.

The statement of Theorem
7.1
is almost identical to the statement of Theorem
1.4
, but replaces the linearization at the approximate solutions
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
with the linearization at the de-singularized configurations
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
. To review the statement briefly, the operator
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
is viewed as a first-order boundary value problem on the tubular neighborhood
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
where
\lambda=c\varepsilon^{1/2}
. This is done by introducing a Hilbert space
H
and a projection

\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}:L^{1,2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\to H

(7.1)

so that
\ker(\Pi^{\mathcal{L}})
is the subspace of sections satisfying certain boundary and orthogonality conditions. The statement also relies on the weighted norms
\|-\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}
and
\|-\|_{L^{2}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}
defined in Section
5.1
.

Theorem 7.1
.

(Invertibility of
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}})

Subject to the boundary and orthogonality conditions defined by (
7.1
), the extended gauge-fixed linearization at the de-singularized configurations
(\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})

\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}:\ker(\Pi^{\mathcal{L}})\subseteq L^{1,2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\longrightarrow L^{2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

(7.2)

is Fredholm of Index 0. Additionally, there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
it is invertible, and there are positive constants
C,\gamma^{\text{in}}\!<\!<1
independent of
\varepsilon
such that the bounds

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/12+\gamma^{\text{in}}}}\ \|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

(7.3)

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma^{\text{in}}}\|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}

(7.4)

hold for weights
0\leq\nu<\tfrac{1}{4}
. Notice the distinction is that there is no weight on the codomain in the latter bound.

Specifically, when
\gamma^{\prime}
is the small constant that was used to define the interior region in the proof of Lemma
4.7
, then

\gamma^{\text{in}}=\tfrac{2}{3}\left(\tfrac{1}{4}-\nu\right)+\nu\gamma^{\prime}

hence
\gamma^{\text{in}}\!<\!<1
when
\gamma^{\prime}
is chosen suitably small and
\nu
suitably close to
\tfrac{1}{4}
.

The remainder of this Section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.1
, and is organized as follows. Akin to Section 6.2, Section 7.1 develops the Fredholm theory of Atiyah-Patodi-SInger boundary value problems in for the Dirac operator on a 3-manifold though in a slightly non-standard context more suitable to the problem at hand. Section 7.2 describes a distinguished subspace of configurations–section of the “kernel subbundle”– which play a prominent role in the proof. Section 7.3 gives the precise definition
H
and
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
in the statement of
7.1
, and Section 7.4 concludes the proof via an integration by parts argument. Section 7.5 then deduces the case of a general metric from the model case. We continue to assume, until that section, that the assumptions of the model case (definition
5.4
) hold.

7.1
APS Boundary Conditions in 3d

7.1.1
Untwisted Boundary Conditions

Consider
Y=S^{1}\times D^{2}
equipped with the product metric. Let
(t,r,\theta)
be cylindrical coordinates. As in Section
6.2
there is a restriction (or trace) map

\text{Tr}:L^{1,2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{1/2,2}(T^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})

to the boundary values and we will choose a “half-dimensional” subspace
H_{0}\subseteq L^{1/2,2}(T^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Typically, one choose the negative eigenspace of the induced Dirac operator on the boundary (see
[
16
]
Section 17), which leads to an Index 0 problem. For our purposes, an alternative choice of a
t
-independent space
H_{0}
is more suitable. The restriction to the boundary torus of a spinor
\varphi=(\alpha,\beta)
can be decomposed in Fourier series

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\Big|_{S^{1}\times\partial D}=\sum_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{k\ell}\\
\beta_{k\ell}\end{pmatrix}e^{i\ell t}e^{ik\theta}.

We define the subspace
H_{0}\subseteq L^{1/2,2}(T^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})
by

H_{0}=\Big\{(\alpha,\beta)\in L^{2}\ |\ \alpha=\sum_{k<0,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{k\ell}e^{i\ell t}e^{ik\theta}\ ,\ \beta=\sum_{k>0,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\beta_{k\ell}e^{i\ell t}e^{ik\theta}\Big\}\cap L^{1/2,2}(T^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Equivalently, in the notation of Section
6.2
(recall Equation
6.12
) we require
\alpha_{\ell}(\theta)
has vanishing
H^{+}_{[0]}
component and
\beta_{\ell}(\theta)
has vanishing
H^{-}_{[0]}
component for every
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let

\Pi_{0}:L^{1,2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2})\to H_{0}^{\perp}

be the projection to the orthogonal complement of
H_{0}
, so that

\varphi\in\ker(\Pi_{0})\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \varphi|_{S^{1}\times\partial D}\in H_{0}.

Pictorially, associating the boundary Fourier modes with the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
where
\ell
is the vertical index and
k
the horizontal, the condition to lie in
\ker(\Pi_{0})
says that
\alpha
has non-zero boundary modes on the left half-lattice

Fourier mode

\displaystyle\ldots{\underline{k=\!-\!2}}\hskip 8.5359pt{\underline{k=\!-\!1}}\hskip 8.5359pt{\underline{k=0}}\ \hskip 8.5359pt{\underline{k>0}}\ \ \ldots

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \vdots\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \vdots\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \hskip 1.42271pt0\hskip 71.13188pt

\displaystyle\underline{\ell=2}\ \

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2,2}\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{-1,2}\ \ \ \ \ 0\hskip 71.13188pt

\displaystyle\underline{\ell=1}\ \

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2,1}\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{-1,1}\ \ \ \ \ 0\hskip 71.13188pt

\displaystyle\underline{\ell=0}\ \

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2,0}\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{-1,0}\ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ldots 0\ldots

\displaystyle\underline{\ell=\!-\!1}\ \

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2,0}\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{-1,0}\ \ \ \ \ 0\hskip 71.13188pt

\displaystyle\underline{\ell=\!-\!2}\ \

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2,0}\ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{-1,0}\ \ \ \ \ 0\hskip 71.13188pt

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \vdots\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \vdots\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \hskip 1.42271pt0\hskip 71.13188pt

while
\beta
has non-zero modes in the right half-lattice. Equivalently, since the boundary conditions only restrict the
\theta
Fourier modes, we can express the condition
(\alpha,\beta)\in\ker(\Pi_{0})
as

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt\underline{k=-1}\ \ \

\displaystyle\underline{k=0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \underline{k=1}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{-1}(t)\ \ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \beta_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \beta_{2}(t)\ldots

The next proposition shows that the Dirac operator with these boundary conditions has Index 0. Although this result is quite standard, it is beneficial to give a proof here that is suggestive of the eventual proof of the invertibility of
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
. The key point is that an estimate on the operator defined on slices of constant
t
is applied for each
t
and then integrated over
t\in S^{1}

Proposition 7.2
.

The boundary value problem

(\not{D},\Pi_{0}):L^{1,2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};S_{E})\longrightarrow L^{2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};S_{E})\oplus H_{0}^{\perp}

is invertible, and
a fortiori
Fredholm of Index 0. Equivalently, the same holds for the operator

\not{D}:\ker(\Pi_{0})\longrightarrow L^{2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};S_{E}).

∎

The proof is a standard application of integration by parts.

7.1.2
Twisted Boundary Conditions

The boundary conditions we will impose on
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
are based on a twisted variation of the boundary conditions given in the previous Subsection
7.1.1
. In this subsection, define an abstract version of the twisted boundary conditions and calculate the Fredholm index of the resulting Dirac operator. This result will be employed later to calculate the index of
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
in the context of Theorem
7.1
.

The idea of the twisted boundary conditions is that for each fixed
t
, we allow four new modes by allowing
\beta_{0}
to be non-vanishing and then impose four constraints on linear combinations of
\beta_{0}
and
\alpha_{-1}
. An example of such a linear constraint is provided by the condition
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}
that appear in the boundary conditions for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}
in Definition
6.54
.

To make this precise, let
E_{-1,0}\to S^{1}
be the trivial vector bundle with fiber
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, where the fiber is thought of as the complex span

(E_{-1,0})_{t}=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\ \ ,\ \ \begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\ \ ,\ \ \begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\ \ ,\ \ \begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\right\}

(7.5)

so that the space of sections
\Gamma(E_{-1,0})\subseteq L^{2}(\partial(S^{1}\times D);S_{E})
is the closed subspace consisting of boundary configurations

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}(t)e^{-i\theta}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\hskip 28.45274pt\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\beta_{0}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

In terms of the previous diagram, it is the subspace spanned by the boxed modes.

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt\underline{k=-1}\ \ \

\displaystyle\underline{k=0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \underline{k=1}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}\ \ \

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(t)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \alpha_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{2}(t)\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots\beta_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\beta_{-1}(t)}\ \ \ \

\displaystyle\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \beta_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \beta_{2}(t)\ldots

Let

V_{t}\subseteq E_{-1,0}

denote a real 4-plane distribution, and set

H_{1}:=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in L^{2}\ |\ \alpha=\sum_{k<-1,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{k\ell}e^{i\ell t}e^{ik\theta}\ ,\ \beta=\sum_{k\geq 0,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\beta_{k\ell}e^{i\ell t}e^{ik\theta}\}

(7.6)

to be the previously allowed modes omitting the
\alpha_{-1}
and
\beta_{0}
modes. Then consider

H_{\text{Tw}}=(H_{1}\oplus L^{2}(S^{1};V_{t}))\cap L^{1/2,2}\hskip 42.67912ptH_{\text{Tw}}^{\perp}=(H_{1}\oplus L^{2}(S^{1};V_{t}))^{\perp}\cap L^{1/2,2}

and denote

\Pi_{\text{Tw}}:L^{1,2}\to H_{\text{Tw}}^{\perp}

the projection to the orthogonal complement.

Definition 7.3
.

The
V_{t}
-Twisted Boundary Conditions
are given by the requirement that

\varphi|_{\partial(S^{1}\times D^{2})}\in H_{\text{Tw}}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \Pi_{\text{Tw}}(\varphi)=0

so that the allowed modes are illustrated by

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt\underline{k=-1}\ \ \

\displaystyle\underline{k=0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \underline{k=1}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}\ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \beta_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \beta_{2}(t)\ldots

subject to the constraint that

\hskip 25.6073pt\boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}+\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}\in V_{t}\ \ \forall t\in S^{1}.

\diamondsuit

As an example, the untwisted case considered in the previous subsection is the special case that
V_{t}=\text{span}\{\alpha_{-1}\}
, i.e.
\beta_{0}=0
for all
t
. We constrain the distribution
V_{t}
in two ways. These constraints are expressed in terms of two anti-involutions, which we now define. Write the fiber of
E_{-1,0}
as
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{H}
so elements may be written

\varphi=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}\\
\beta_{0}\end{pmatrix}

where the two components are
\mathbb{H}
-valued. Let

J:\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{H}\hskip 42.67912pt\sigma_{1}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{H}

denote, respectively, the involutions

J\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}\\
\beta_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\beta_{0}\\
\alpha_{-1}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 56.9055pt\sigma_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix}

(Note that
J
is complex linear, and is not the charge conjugation map often denoted by the same letter).

Lemma 7.4
.

The following hold. Throughout, we use the real inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{H}
.

(1)

(\sigma_{1}J)^{2}=-Id
, hence
\sigma_{1}J
is an almost-complex structure. In particular,
(\sigma_{1}J)v\perp v
for any
v\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{H}
.

(2)

For spinors
\varphi=(\alpha,\beta)\in\ker(\Pi_{\text{Tw}})
, the operator

\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial\\
2\overline{\partial}&0\end{pmatrix}

satisfies the integration by parts formula

\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}\langle\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle\ -\ \langle\varphi,\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi\rangle\ dV\ =\int_{T^{2}}\Big\langle J\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}\\
\beta_{0}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}\\
\beta_{0}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\ dA.

Proof.

By definition

(\sigma_{1}J)^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}=\sigma_{1}J\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}-\beta\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}=\sigma_{1}J\begin{pmatrix}-i\beta\\
-i\alpha\end{pmatrix}=\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}i\alpha\\
-i\beta\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}

and taking the real inner-product and conjugating the bottom term,

\text{Re}\Big\langle\sigma_{1}J\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle=\text{Re}(\overline{-i\beta}\alpha)+\text{Re}(\overline{-i\alpha}\beta)=\text{Re}(i\overline{\beta}\alpha)+\text{Re}(-i\alpha\overline{\beta})=0.

which completes item (1).

Item (2) follows immediately from the previously used integration by parts formulas

\displaystyle\int_{D^{2}}\langle-2\partial\beta,\alpha\rangle+\langle\beta,-2\overline{\partial}\alpha\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D}\langle-\beta,\alpha\rangle e^{i\theta}d\theta

(7.7)

\displaystyle\int_{D^{2}}\langle 2\overline{\partial}\alpha,\beta\rangle+\langle\alpha,2\partial\beta\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle e^{-i\theta}d\theta

(7.8)

and the observation that the condition
\Pi_{\text{Tw}}(\alpha,\beta)=0
implies the only non-zero inner product for the boundary modes occurs in the
\alpha_{-1}
and
\beta_{0}
modes.
∎

As a consequence of the above Lemma
7.4
, there is a complex-linear isomorphism
(E_{-1,0},\sigma_{1}J)\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{4},i)
where the latter is given the standard almost-complex structure. This endows the former with a symplectic structure for which
\sigma_{1}J
is a compatible almost-complex structure given by the pullback of the standard symplectic structure on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. We impose the following hypotheses on the distribution
V_{t}
:

Hypothesis (I):
Assume that
V_{t}\subseteq E_{-1,0}
is a bundle of Lagrangian subspaces with respect to the

symplectic structure compatible with the almost-complex
\sigma_{1}J
. In particular, this

requires
(\sigma_{1}J)V_{t}\perp V_{t}
for all
t\in S^{1}
.

Hypothesis (II):

V_{t}
is homotopic through distributions satisfying
(I)
to a constant distribution.

The twisted analogue of Proposition
7.2
is the following:

Proposition 7.5
.

Suppose that the 4-plane distribution
V_{t}
satisfies hypotheses
(I)
and
(II)
. Then the operator

(\not{D},\Pi_{\text{Tw}}):L^{1,2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};S_{E})\longrightarrow L^{2}(S^{1}\times D^{2};S_{E})\oplus H_{\text{Tw}}^{\perp}

(7.9)

is Fredholm of Index 0.

Proof.

First, the hypothesis
(I)
implies that this operator is Fredholm. Integrating by parts and using Young’s inequality and
(I)
shows the boundary is bounded above by
\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\|(\Pi_{\text{Tw}})^{\perp}\varphi\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2\epsilon}\|\Pi_{\text{Tw}}\varphi\|^{2}
. Choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small and absorbing the first into the left-hand side shows

\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}}\leq C(\|\not{D}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\Pi_{\text{Tw}}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{1/2,2}}+\|K\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}})

(7.10)

where
K:L^{1,2}\to L^{2}
is a compact operator. Using this, it follows from standard theory that
(\not{D},\Pi_{\text{Tw}})
has closed range and finite dimensional kernel. Integrating by parts on
\langle\not{D}\varphi,\psi\rangle
shows an element of the complement of the range must solve
\not{D}\psi=0
subject to the twisted boundary conditions for the distribution
W_{t}=\sigma_{1}V_{t}
, which also satisfies
(I)
hence (
7.10
) applies to show the cokernel is finite dimensional.

Hypotheses
(II)
implies that
(\not{D},\Pi_{\text{Tw}})
is homotopic through Fredholm operators to one for which
V_{t}
