is constant. Since the space of 4-planes in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
satisfying hypothesis
(I)
is homeomorphic to the Lagrangian Grassmannian, it is connected, and
V_{t}
it is therefore homotopic to the distribution

V_{0}=\text{span}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

which obviously satisfies hypothesis
(I)
. The twisted boundary condition for
V_{0}
is the untwisted boundary condition of Proposition
7.1.1
, which has index 0.

∎

7.1.3
The Degenerating Family

The boundary and orthogonality conditions
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
for
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
used in Theorem
7.1
are more intricate than a simple choice of twisted Lagrangian distribution
V_{t}
. Before proceeding, we describe the geometric intuition motivating their definition.

In order to identify a proper context in which
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
is invertible, one must understand more precisely how the family of Fredholm operators

\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\to\not{D}_{A_{0}}

(7.11)

degenerate to the singular semi-Fredholm operator in the limit, and in particular how the infinite-dimensional cokernel of Proposition
3.3
arises. (Here, the limit of the operators should be interpreted only in an imprecise sense, as the difference is not bounded in
L^{2}
). One might expect that there is an infinite-dimensional family of eigenfunctions with small eigenvalues approaching 0 for which the ratio of the
L^{1,2}
to the
L^{2}
norm becomes infinite, which gives rise to the infinite-dimensional cokernel in the limit (which consists of kernel elements that are
L^{2}
but not
L^{1,2}
).

Indeed, this occurs in the two-dimensional case. Here, assuming the metric on
D_{\lambda}
is a product, the cokernel of the limiting operator
\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}
is finite-dimensional and spanned by

k_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
r^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\ \ \ \text{and }\ \ k_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
r^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

on
D_{\lambda}
. The kernel of the de-singularized operator
\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
in
L^{1,2}
is spanned by the configurations

\beta_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1=\left(\frac{K(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{-\tfrac{1}{3}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-h_{\varepsilon}}r^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1

(7.12)

(and likewise for the
\otimes j
component) that were described in Lemma
6.21
. For every
\varepsilon>0
, these are smoothed off versions of the function
r^{-1/2}
where the smoothing occurs closer and closer to the origin. Thus these elements converge in
L^{2}
(after renormalizing in
L^{2}
) to the limiting cokernel element
k_{1}
which fails to be in
L^{1,2}
.

Counterintuitively, in the three-dimensional case on
S^{1}\times D_{\lambda}
this picture is only correct for the constant Fourier mode in the
t
-direction. The reason for this is that for the
\ell\neq 0
modes, the two spinor components
\alpha,\beta
are coupled. Again assuming the metric on
S^{1}\times D_{\lambda}
is Euclidean, the infinite-dimensional cokernel is spanned (over
\mathbb{C}
) in
L^{2}
by the elements

\psi^{1}_{\ell}=\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta}\ \ \tfrac{e^{-|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{r}}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell)\tfrac{e^{-|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{r}}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1\hskip 28.45274pt\psi^{j}_{\ell}=\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta}\ \ \tfrac{e^{-|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{r}}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell)\tfrac{e^{-|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{r}}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

(7.13)

for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
. Recall that the complex gauge transformation acts by
e^{-h_{\varepsilon}}\sim r^{1/2}
in the bottom component, but by
e^{h_{\varepsilon}}\sim r^{-1/2}
in the top component. For
\ell\neq 0
, one sees that after smoothing the bottom component behaves as in the two-dimensional case, but the top component becomes
more
singular. Consequently, in this case the smoothed cokernel elements analogous to (
7.12
) are now
neither

L^{1,2}

nor

L^{2}
along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. This suggests that the infinite-dimensional cokernel that appears in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
does not arise from a family of
L^{1,2}
- eigenspaces; instead each
\psi_{\ell}
appears to arise from an
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of elements that are not even in
L^{2}
for
\varepsilon>0
, but which limit to an element of
L^{2}
.

One can confirm this picture with the following basic calculation. Consider replacing
A^{h_{\varepsilon}}
with the nearby non-smooth connection given as follows. Let
\rho_{0}\sim\varepsilon^{2/3}
be a fixed constant, and define a connection
A_{1}
piecewise by setting it to be the product connection for
\rho<\rho_{0}
and setting it equal to
A_{0}
for
\rho\geq\rho_{0}
. Writing the Dirac operator in Fourier series leads to ODEs in both regions, and it is straightforward to check by matching boundary conditions at
r=\rho_{0}
that there are no solutions with exponential decay away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
that are locally
L^{2}
along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. This property should persist under the minor smoothing that corrects
A_{1}
to
A^{h_{\varepsilon}}
.

A more accurate picture of what occurs for the boundary-value problem is as follows. Rather surprisingly, the infinite-dimensional kernel that appears in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
arises from elements with exponential growth away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. For the discontinuous connection
A_{1}
from the previous paragraph, there is an finite-dimensional family of kernel elements which are
L^{2}
along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, but which look like

\sim e^{i\ell t}\frac{e^{+|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{r}}

(7.14)

for large
r
. But on the disk
D_{\lambda}
of radius
\lambda=c\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, the elements (
7.14
) are still monotonically decreasing toward the boundary for values of
|\ell|\leq O(\varepsilon^{-1/2})
. Cutting these elements off near the boundary leads to a family of spinors (satisfying the boundary conditions) with unit
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
-norm for which applying
\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
result in very small elements in
L^{2}
. For precisely the same reason as in Remark
6.11
, such elements violate any uniform bound on the inverse. Indeed, as one can check, if (and only if!)
|\ell|\leq O(\varepsilon^{1/2})
, the configurations
e^{i\ell t}\beta_{t}
where
\beta_{t}
is a kernel element of
\mathcal{N}_{t}
(as in
7.12
above) results approximate kernel elements on which
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
is very small.

Given this picture, to find a setting in which there is a uniform bound on the inverse of
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
, we must choose boundary conditions that allow these approximate kernel elements and then project orthogonal to them, just as we did for
\mathcal{N}_{t}
. Once
\ell
is sufficiently large, the
t
-derivative becomes violent enough that these elements are no longer almost in the kernel and projection orthogonal to them is no longer necessary. Thus
the boundary conditions on
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
are taken to be combination of boundary conditions and orthogonal projections on the interior
. Specifically, we choose boundary conditions that allow the boundary values of
e^{i\ell t}\beta_{t}
for low
\ell
but disallow them for high
\ell
, while at the same time we impose orthogonal projections that disallow the elements
e^{i\ell t}\beta_{t}
in the interior for low
\ell
while leaving the projections unrestricted for high
\ell
. The key point of the proof of Theorem
7.1
is to show that these can be done simultaneously without imposing so many constraints as to result in a large negative index.

The upshot of this intuitive dicussion is that it is the exponentially growing elements of the form (
7.14
) that are the impediment to obtaining uniform elliptic estimates for
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
, rather than exponentially decaying ones limiting to (
7.13
). This observation identifies the correct setup for Theorem
7.1
. Using this setup, the proof proceeds in the next four subsections without reference to the the above intuitive picture (in particular, no claims about exponentially growth are explicitly made).
Although this intuitive geometric picture guides our setup for Theorem
7.1
, it is not necessary to make it precise. The justification of this picture’s correctness lies in the fact that a setup designed with it in mind actually yields a proof of Theorem
7.1
, while attempts to prove Theorem
7.1
envisioning other pictures (such as one in which every mode is analogous to the zeroth mode) are completely confounding.

7.2
The Kernel Bundle

As explained in the previous subsection
7.1.3
, the constraints
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
for the operator
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
used in Theorem
7.1
are a mixture of boundary conditions and orthogonality constraints. The boundary portion of these constraints are a specific case of the twisted boundary conditions discussed in the previous subsection
7.1
. In this subsection, we define the accompanying orthogonality constraints. These project orthogonal to (a subspace) of the configurations that lie in the kernel of
\mathcal{N}_{t}
for every
t\in S^{1}
.

Let
D_{\lambda}
continue to denote the disk of radius
\lambda=c\varepsilon^{1/2}
. For each
t\in S^{1}
, recall that
\beta_{t}
is the element whose complex span is
\ker(\mathcal{N}_{t})\subseteq H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\{t\}\times D_{\varepsilon})
such that it is normalized in the
{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D_{\lambda})}
-norm.

Definition 7.6
.

Define the
Kernel Subbundle
as

K(\mathcal{N}_{t})\subseteq S^{1}\times H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})

where the latter is viewed as the trivial vector bundle over
S^{1}
having fiber
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D_{\lambda})
. Thus its sections are

\Gamma(K(\mathcal{N}_{t}))=\{\eta(t)\beta_{t}\ |\ \eta:S^{1}\to\mathbb{C}\}.

Before proceeding, let us make a brief remark on function spaces. We have versions of the space
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
in both two and three dimensions. To distinguish we rename them
H^{1}_{slice}
and
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
respectively. Explicitly, where
\nabla
denotes only the derivatives in the
D_{\lambda}
-directions, the norms are given by

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{slice}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ rdrd\theta\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\|\varphi,a\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{S^{1}}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}\varphi|^{2}+|\partial_{t}a|^{2}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ rdrd\theta dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{S^{1}}\left(\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}(\varphi,a)|^{2}\ rdrd\theta+\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{slice}}\right)dt\right)^{1/2}

Configurations
\eta(t)\beta_{t}\in\Gamma(K(\mathcal{N}_{t}))
, play a distinguished role because for
\eta
with sufficiently small derivative, these form the the
O(\varepsilon^{-1/2})
-dimensional space approximate kernel elements described in the previous subsection
7.1.3
.

The sections of the kernel bundle
K(\mathcal{N}_{t})
are, by design, distinguished by being precisely the configurations on which
\mathcal{N}_{t}
vanishes for every
t
. More precisely,

Lemma 7.7
.

The inclusion

\displaystyle L^{1,2}(S^{1};\ker(\mathcal{N}_{t}))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\eta(t)\beta_{t}

of sections of the kernel bundle has image characterized by being precisely the sections
\xi
such that

\mathcal{\|}\mathcal{N}_{t}\xi\|_{L^{2}(S^{1}\times D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

Proof.

Notice first that
\eta(t)\beta_{t}\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
since

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)\beta_{t}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{S^{1}}\left(\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\dot{\eta}\beta_{t}+\eta\dot{\beta}|^{2}\ \ rdrd\theta+\|\eta(t)\beta_{t}\|_{H^{1}_{slice}}\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{S^{1}}|\dot{\eta}|^{2}\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}+|\eta|^{2}\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}+|\eta(t)|^{2}\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{slice}}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{S^{1}}|\dot{\eta}|^{2}\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}+|\eta|^{2}\kappa_{1}\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}+|\eta(t)|^{2}dt\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\|\eta\|_{L^{1,2}}

where in the third line we have invoked item (3) of Lemma
6.28
.On such configurations,
\mathcal{N}_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})=0
by definition. Supposing conversely that a smooth element
\xi
had
\mathcal{N}_{t}\xi=0
. Then, by definition of the kernel subbundle, we may write
\xi=\eta(t)\beta_{t}
for a function
\eta(t)
. Clearly
\eta\in L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
if and only if
\xi\in L^{2}(S^{1}\times D_{\lambda})
, and then reversing the above inequality shows such a configuration is in
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
only if
\eta\in L^{1,2}
.
∎

The projection
\pi^{\text{ker}}_{t}:H^{1}_{slice}\to\mathbb{C}
now becomes a
t
-parameterized family of projections resulting in a function in
L^{1,2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
.

Definition 7.8
.

the
normalized projection to the kernel bundle

P:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}\to L^{1,2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})

defined by

[P(\xi)](t)=\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\frac{\langle\xi(t),\beta_{t}\rangle}{\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}}\ rdrd\theta

so that for each fixed
t
, the value is the value of the slice projection
\pi^{\text{ker}}_{t}
for
\mathcal{N}_{t}
. Notice that since
\partial_{t}\xi,\partial_{t}\beta_{t}\in L^{2}
this is a bounded map into
L^{1,2}
by Cauchy-Schwartz, and since
\eta(t)\beta_{t}
projects to
\eta(t)
, the previous Lemma
7.7
shows its image is all of
L^{1,2}
.

We can also view the projection as a
\mathbb{Z}
-parameterized family of projections to

P^{\ell}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

giving the Fourier modes:

P^{\ell}(\xi)=\int_{S^{1}}\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\frac{\langle\xi,e^{i\ell t}\beta_{t}\rangle}{\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}}\ rdrd\theta dt

(7.15)

so that if
\xi=\eta(t)\beta_{t}
then
P^{\ell}(\xi)=\langle\eta,e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})}
is the
\ell^{th}
Fourier coefficient, and the original projection is given by

P(\xi)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}P^{\ell}(\xi)e^{i\ell t}.

Clearly since
P(\xi)\in L^{1,2}
the sequence satisfies
\{P^{\ell}(\xi)\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\in l^{1,2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Additionally, we can split this family of projections into two regimes: the high and low Fourier modes. Let
L_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
denote a large constant to be chosen later. In a slight abuse of notation, we write
\frac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}
to mean the smallest integer greater than
\tfrac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}
if
\varepsilon^{-1/2}\notin\mathbb{Z}
.

\displaystyle P^{\text{low}}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}}\hskip 159.3356pt\xi\to\bigoplus_{|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}}P^{\ell}(\xi)

(7.16)

\displaystyle P^{\text{high}}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\{\eta\in L^{1,2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})\ |\ \eta_{\ell}=0\text{ for }|\ell|\leq\tfrac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\}\hskip 25.6073pt\xi\to\sum_{|\ell|\geq\frac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}}P^{\ell}(\xi)e^{i\ell t}

(7.17)

7.3
Boundary and Projection Conditions

In this section we define the precise constraints
\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}
imposed on the operator
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
in Theorem
7.1
. As explained in subsection
7.1.3
, these are a combination of boundary conditions and interior orthogonal projections using
P_{\ell}
. First, we cover the boundary conditions, which are a particular case of the twisted boundary conditions which appeared in subsection
7.3
, and subsequently define the projection conditions.

7.3.1
Pure Boundary Conditions

We define a twisted boundary condition by specifying a 4-dimensional distribution
V_{t}\subseteq E_{-1,0}
as in subsection
7.1.2
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}
be the components of the
e^{-i\theta}
boundary mode of
\alpha
, and
b_{1},b_{2}
be the components of the constant boundary mode of
\beta
so that

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}(t)\\
\beta_{0}(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}(t)\\
b_{1}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}a_{2}(t)\\
b_{2}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

(7.18)

Recall that

\mu^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega\right)=b_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}+\overline{a}_{1}\beta_{1}^{H}+b_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}+\overline{a}_{2}\beta_{2}^{H}.

(7.19)

was the Index 2 boundary condition imposed on
\mathcal{N}_{t}
, where,
\alpha_{i}^{H}
and
\beta_{i}^{H}
are the components of
\Phi^{H}
restricted to the boundary (the subscripts on these denote the
\otimes 1
and
\otimes j
components, not the Fourier modes). Here, we split this condition to give an index zero one.

Definition 7.9
.

Define the 4-dimensional Lagrangian distribution
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
determined by
\Phi_{0}
by setting

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}:=\overline{a}_{1}\beta_{1}^{H}+\overline{a}_{2}\beta_{2}^{H}\hskip 56.9055pt\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\beta}:=b_{1}\overline{\alpha}_{1}^{H}+b_{2}\overline{\alpha}_{2}^{H}

(7.20)

and taking

V^{\Phi_{0}}_{t}:=\{(\alpha_{-1},\beta_{0})\in E_{-1,0}\ |\ \mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\beta}=0\}

Lemma 7.10
.

The distribution
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
fits into the proper complex filtration

\{0\}\ \subsetneq\ \ker(\mathcal{N}_{t})|_{\partial D_{\varepsilon}}\ \subsetneq\ V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}\ \subsetneq\ (\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial})^{-1}(0)\ \subsetneq\ E_{-1,0}.

in particular, configurations with boundary values in
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
satisfy the boundary conditions for
\mathcal{N}_{t}
and the kernel elements
\beta_{t}
have boundary values in
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}\subsetneq(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial})^{-1}(0)
is obvious since

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial}=\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}+\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\beta}

hence vanishes if they vanish individually.

Next, recalling that the coefficients of the leading order term of
\Phi_{0}
are denoted
c(t),d(t)
, notice that up a factors of
r^{-1/2}
and
e^{-i\theta}
on the boundary, we have

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}=\overline{a}_{1}d(t)+\overline{a}_{2}\overline{c}(t)\hskip 56.9055pt\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\beta}=b_{1}\overline{c}(t)+b_{2}(-d(t))

thus these see only the
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
components respectively. For each
t
,
(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha})^{-1}(0)\cap\{\beta_{0}=0\}
and
(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\beta})^{-1}(0)\cap\{\alpha_{-1}=0\}
are spanned over
\mathbb{C}
by

v_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}c(t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\hskip 42.67912ptv_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
d(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\overline{c}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

(7.21)

respectively. Thus
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
is the complex span the above elements. The condition
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
shows it is indeed
4
-dimensional over
\mathbb{R}
.

To observe that
\beta_{t}|_{\partial D_{\varepsilon}}\in V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
, recall that
\beta_{t}
is the linear combination of

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+h_{1}\cdot\Phi^{H}

\displaystyle\beta_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j+h_{j}\cdot\Phi^{H}

satisfying
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=0
with
h_{1},h_{j}
as in (the proof of) Lemma
6.28
. Since

\Phi^{H}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{H}c(t)\rho^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}d(t)\rho^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{H}\overline{d}(t)\rho^{1/2}\\
e^{-H}\overline{c}(t)\rho^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

and
h_{1},h_{j}
have only negative Fourier modes, it is immediate that the
h_{1}\cdot\Phi^{H}
and
h_{j}\cdot\Phi^{H}
contribute boundary terms that are complex multiples of (
7.21
) in the relevant Fourier modes. Thus

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\alpha}(\beta_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\beta}(\beta_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}(\beta_{t})=0

since the linear combination of the first terms of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{j}
is exactly the one satisfying
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=0
(notice that
h_{j}=\tfrac{\overline{c}(t)}{d(t)})h_{1}
in a projective sense so the lower order terms contribute the same linear combinations as the leading order). This shows
\beta_{t}\in V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
.

For completeness, we note that the expression for
\beta_{t}
restricted to the boundary is

\displaystyle\beta_{t}|_{T^{2}}=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0\\
d(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\overline{c}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j+\frac{3K(t)}{4}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-c(t)e^{-i\theta}\\
d(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}\overline{d}(t)e^{-i\theta}\\
\overline{c}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\right)\right]\rho_{t}^{-1/2}+O(\rho_{t}^{-1}).

∎

Next, we show this subbundle
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
7.5
for the Dirac operator with these twisted boundary conditions to be Fredholm.

Lemma 7.11
.

The distribution
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
satisfies the hypotheses

(I)

(\sigma_{1}J)V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}\perp V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
for all
t\in S^{1}
.

(II)

V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
is homotopic through distributions satisfying
(I)
to a constant one.

of Proposition
7.5
.

Proof.

Let
v_{1},v_{2}
denote the elements spanning
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
as in (
7.21
). For
(I)
it suffices to show the three hermitian inner products

\langle\sigma_{1}Jv_{1},v_{1}\rangle=0\hskip 42.67912pt\langle\sigma_{1}Jv_{2},v_{2}\rangle=0\hskip 42.67912pt\langle\sigma_{1}Jv_{1},v_{2}\rangle=0

all vanish. Notice the third implies the same reversing
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
since
\sigma_{1}J
is orthogonal and squares to
-I
.

The first two are obvious, since
v_{1}
has only
\alpha
-components, while
Jv_{1}
has only
\beta
-components. Likewise for
v_{2}
. For the third,

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{1}Jv_{1},v_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\sigma_{1}J\begin{pmatrix}c(t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\sigma_{1}J\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)\\
0\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\ ,\ \begin{pmatrix}0\\
d(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{0}\\
\overline{c}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
c(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\overline{d}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\ ,\ \begin{pmatrix}0\\
d(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{0}\\
\overline{c}(t)\end{pmatrix}\otimes j\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i(\overline{c}d+(\overline{-\overline{d}})\overline{c})=i(\overline{c}d-d\overline{c})=0.

For
(II)
, observe that the subspace
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
depends only on the functions
c(t)
and
d(t)
which are required to satisfy
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
by Assumption
2
. Thus normalizing, we view them as map

\displaystyle S^{1}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle S^{3}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{(c(t),d(t))}{|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}}

and since
S^{3}
is simply connected, there is a homotopy through pairs satisfying
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
connecting them to the constant pair
(1,0)
.
∎

We now define

Definition 7.12
.

Write
(\varphi,a)=(\alpha,\beta,\zeta,\omega)
as in Section
6.3
. Then
(\varphi,a)
satisfies the
Pure Boundary Conditions
on
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
if

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)|_{\partial(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\in H_{\text{Tw}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Pi_{\text{Tw}}(\alpha,\beta)=0

\displaystyle(\zeta,\omega)|_{\partial(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\in H_{0}\ \

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}(\zeta,\omega)=0,

where
H_{\text{Tw}}
uses the distribution
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
defined above in Definition
7.9
, and
H_{0}
is the untwisted version from Section
7.1
. The allowed boundary modes are illustrated by

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt\underline{k=-1}\ \ \

\displaystyle\underline{k=0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \underline{k=1}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}\ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \beta_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \beta_{2}(t)\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots\zeta_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\zeta_{-1}(t)}\ \ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \omega_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \omega_{2}(t)\ldots

where the boxed modes are constrained so that

\hskip 25.6073pt\boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}+\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}\in V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}\ \ \forall t\in S^{1}.

Corollary 7.13
.

Subject to the pure boundary conditions, the boundary-value problem

(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Pi_{\text{Tw}}\oplus\Pi_{0}):H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\longrightarrow L^{2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\oplus H_{\text{Tw}}^{\perp}\oplus H_{0}^{\perp}

(7.22)

is Fredholm of Index 0.

Proof.

The spaces in question are equivalent to the space
L^{1,2}
and
L^{2}
respectively since the domain is compact, so we may disregard the weighted norms. The compactness of the domain also implies that, (although the off-diagonal and connection terms are large),

\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}&\gamma(\_)\frac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\frac{\mu(\_,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}&\mathbb{d}\end{pmatrix}\ =\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}&\frac{}{}&0\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
0&&\mathbb{d}\end{pmatrix}\mod\mathcal{K}

where
\mathcal{K}
is the space of compact operators. The previous Lemma
7.11
shows that the conditions of Proposition
7.5
are satisfied for the twisted boundary conditions defined by
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
, hence Proposition
7.5
applies to show that the top block is Fredholm of Index 0. Under the association
(a_{0}+a_{t}dt,a_{x}dx+a_{y}dy)\sim(\zeta,\omega)
the operator
\mathbb{d}
is the Dirac operator up to a sign, hence the bottom block is also Fredholm of Index 0 by the untwisted case of Proposition
7.2
.
∎

7.3.2
Mixed Boundary and Projection Conditions

The eventual proof to proving the invertibility of
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
follows from an integration by parts argument. Holistically, it has the following form. Let
\xi=(\varphi,a)
, then

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}\xi|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}\xi|^{2}+|\mathcal{N}_{t}\xi|^{2}+\langle\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\xi\ ,\ \mathcal{N}_{t}\xi\rangle+\langle\mathcal{N}_{t}\xi\ ,\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\xi\rangle\ dV

(7.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}\xi|^{2}+|\mathcal{N}_{t}\xi|^{2}+\langle\xi,\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\xi\rangle\ dV+\int_{\partial(N_{\lambda})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\xi,\partial_{t}\xi\rangle

(7.24)

where
\sigma_{t}
is the symbol of
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
. The cross term
\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{n},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}
is comparatively small, and can be absorbed. We would wish to impose constraints so that

i)
the boundary term vanishes

ii)

\xi
is orthogonal to the subspace consisting of sections of
\Gamma(K(\mathcal{N}_{t}))
.

Given both of these, one could then apply the estimate for
\mathcal{N}_{t}
on each slice of fixed
t
to conclude the theorem, as in the proof of Proposition
7.2
. The problem is that imposing both these constraints does not lead to a Fredholm problem (it is “
\text{Ind}=-L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
”). The solution is to observe that for sufficiently low Fourier modes, the boundary term can be absorbed. On the other hand, for sufficiently high Fourier modes in
K(\mathcal{N}_{t})
, the
\partial_{t}\xi
term becomes sufficiently large to dominate the norm of these configurations, rendering the projections unnecessary. The actual conditions we impose therefore allow the low Fourier modes for an extra boundary component, and also allow non-zero projections to
K(\mathcal{N}_{t})
for the high fourier modes.

We define the mixed boundary and orthogonality conditions as a direct sum

\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}:=(\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}\oplus\Pi_{0})\oplus P^{\text{low}}

wherein
P^{\text{low}}
is the orthogonal projection defined in Equation (
7.16
),
\Pi_{0}
is the untwisted boundary projection on the form components
(\zeta,\omega)
identical to that in Definition
7.12
, and
\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}
is a boundary condition which has
1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}
fewer constraints than
\Pi^{\text{Tw}}
obtained by removing the boundary conditions in certain low modes.

Let us now explain these more precisely. Just as we split the kernel projection
P
into a family of projections
P^{\ell}
parameterized by Fourier modes, we can do the same for the twisted boundary projection
\Pi_{\text{Tw}}
to obtain a family of projections indexed by
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
. As in Equation (
7.21
) let
v_{1}(t)
and
v_{2}(t)
be two vectors whose complex span is
V^{\Phi_{0}}_{t}\subseteq E_{-1,0}
for each
t\in S^{1}
. Similarly let
w_{1}(t),w_{2}(t)
be two vectors whose complex span is
(V^{\Phi_{0}}_{t})^{\perp}
for each
t\in S^{1}
. By Lemma
7.10
, we may choose
w_{1}(t)
so that

\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{v_{1}(t),v_{2}(t),w_{1}(t)\}=(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial})^{-1}(0)\subseteq E_{-1,0}.

(7.25)

Writing the
E_{-1,0}
components of the boundary values as

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{-1}(t)\\
\beta_{0}(t)\end{pmatrix}=a_{1}(t)v_{1}(t)+a_{2}(t)v_{2}(t)+b_{1}(t)w_{1}(t)+b_{2}(t)w_{2}(t)

where
a_{i}(t),b_{i}(t)\in L^{1/2,2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
then the condition that the boundary values lie in
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
can be expressed as

b_{1}(t)=b_{2}(t)=0.

Definition 7.14
.

The
Mixed Boundary and Projection Constraints
are defined by the condition that

(\varphi,a)|_{\partial(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\in\ker(\Pi^{\mathcal{L}})

(7.26)

where

\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}:=(\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}\oplus\Pi_{0})\oplus P^{\text{low}}

is given by

•

P^{\text{low}}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\to\mathbb{C}^{1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}}
is the projection to the low modes of the kernel bundle defined in Equation (
7.16
).

•

\Pi_{0}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\to H_{0}^{\perp}
is the untwisted boundary condition of Subsection
7.1.1
on the form components
(\zeta,\omega)
.

•

\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\to(H_{\text{Tw}}\ \oplus\ \mathbb{C}^{1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}})^{\perp}
is defined (using the notation above) by the constraints that

\pi^{\text{high}}(b_{1}(t))=0\hskip 56.9055ptb_{2}(t)=0

where
\pi^{\text{high}}
denotes the projection to Fourier modes
|\ell|\geq\tfrac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}
, and
\pi^{\text{low}}=1-\pi^{\text{high}}
so that
\Pi_{\text{Tw}}=\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}\oplus\pi^{\text{low}}
.

The allowed modes on the boundary are illustrated by

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt\underline{k=-1}\ \ \

\displaystyle\underline{k=0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \underline{k=1}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt

\displaystyle\ldots\alpha_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}\ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \beta_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \beta_{2}(t)\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots\zeta_{-2}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\zeta_{-1}(t)}\ \ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \omega_{1}(t)\ \ \ \ \ \ \omega_{2}(t)\ldots

where the boxed modes are constrained so that

\hskip 25.6073pt\boxed{\alpha_{-1}(t)}+\boxed{\beta_{0}(t)}\in V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}\ \bigoplus\ \Big\{\sum_{|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}}b_{\ell}e^{i\ell t}w_{1}(t)\Big\}\ \ \forall t\in S^{1}.

and configurations are further constrained by the requirement that

P^{\text{low}}(\varphi,a)=0.

A visualization of these conditions in comparison to the pure boundary condition is given in Figure 2 below.

Notice:
In addition to the above, configurations lying in
\ker(\Pi^{\mathcal{L}})
lie in the space of
(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial})^{-1}(0)
for each fixed
t
, which was the boundary condition imposed on
\mathcal{N}_{t}
.

Figure 2:
Illustration of the pure boundary conditions (left) versus the mixed boundary and orthogonality conditions (right). Allowed modes are indicated by blue boxes, and modes constrained to be 0 are indicated by red boxes. Compared to the pure boundary conditions, the mixed conditions remove
1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}
boundary constraints from
b_{1}
modes, and impose the same number of orthogonality constraints on low modes in the kernel bundle.

Proposition 7.15
.

The mixed boundary value and projection problem

(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}):H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}\longrightarrow L^{2}_{\nu}\ \oplus\ (H_{\text{Tw}}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}})^{\perp}\ \oplus\ H_{0}^{\perp}\ \oplus\ \mathbb{C}^{1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0}}

(7.27)

is Fredholm of Index 0.

Proof.

Compared to the pure boundary conditions in Corollary
7.13
, we removed
2(1+2\varepsilon^{-1/2}/L_{0})
real dimensions of constraints on the boundary modes, and added the same number via the interior projection
P^{\text{low}}
, thus the property of being Fredholm and the index are unchanged. The spaces with difference weights are equivalent, hence they do not change the Fredholmness property.
∎

7.4
Cross-terms, Boundary Terms, and Weights

This section proves several technical lemmas used in the proof of Theorem
7.1
. As explained in the previous subsection (recall Equation
7.24
), the approach to Theorem
7.1
inspired by the observation that the cross term is comparatively mild since configurations only concentrate in the directions of the normal disks. The the next three subsections give precise estimates on this cross term, the boundary term in this integration by parts, and improved weighted estimates for the Dirac operator:

7.4.1
Cross-Terms

The first technical lemma states that the cross term when integrating by parts is small in the weighted norms.
Denote the
dt\wedge dx
and
dt\wedge dy
components of the curvature
F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
by
F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}^{\perp}
. Additionally, we let
\mathfrak{B}_{t}
denote the
dt
-components of the cross term
\mathfrak{B}
from the Weitzenböck formula
2.13
. Explicitly,

\mathfrak{B}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma((-1)^{\text{deg}}a)\sigma_{1}\partial_{t}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}-2a_{t}\partial_{t}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\\
(-1)^{\text{deg}}\mu(\varphi,\sigma_{1}\partial_{t}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})+2i\langle i\varphi,\partial_{t}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\rangle dt\end{pmatrix}.

(7.28)

Lemma 7.16
.

The anti-commutator
\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}=\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\mathcal{N}_{t}+\mathcal{N}_{t}\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}
is given by

\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\gamma(F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}^{\perp}).\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{B}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}

It follows that for configurations
\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}
there is a constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{q}\ ,\ \{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\mathfrak{p}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\left(\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}+\|\mathfrak{p}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\right).

Proof.

This is an easy consequence of the Weitzenböck formula. Recall

\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\\
\mathbb{d}\mathbb{d}a\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\\
\mu(\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{B}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}

(7.29)

where

\mathfrak{B}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma((-1)^{\text{deg}}a)\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}-2a.\nabla\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\\
(-1)^{\text{deg}}\mu(\varphi,\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})+2i\langle i\varphi,\nabla\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\rangle\end{pmatrix}.

On the other hand, since
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}=\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{N}_{t}
, we have

\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=-\partial_{t}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathcal{N}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}+\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}

(7.30)

and the Wietzenbock formula for the linearization at
\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}(t)
for each fixed
t
applied to
t
-independent configurations shows

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathcal{N}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\\
\Delta^{\mathbb{C}}a\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\\
\mu(\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{B}^{\mathbb{C}}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}

where
\Delta^{\mathbb{C}}
is the Laplacian on normal planes, and

\mathfrak{B}^{\mathbb{C}}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma((-1)^{\text{deg}}a)\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}-2a.\nabla_{x,y}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\\
(-1)^{\text{deg}}\mu(\varphi,\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})+2i\langle i\varphi,\nabla_{x,y}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\rangle\end{pmatrix}.

Comparing (
7.29
) and (
7.30
) and using this expression and

\displaystyle\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{2}+\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}+\gamma(F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}^{\perp})

\displaystyle\mathbb{d}\mathbb{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{2}+\Delta^{\mathbb{C}}

yields the formula for
\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},{\mathcal{N}}_{t}\}
.

We now proceed to show the bound in the second assertion. To begin, we claim there is a pointwise bound

|\partial_{t}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|\leq C|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|.

(7.31)

To verify this claim, first recall that
\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}
is given by

\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}:=\begin{pmatrix}e^{H(\rho_{t})}c(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \\
e^{-H(\rho_{t})}d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-e^{H(\rho_{t})}\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \\
e^{-H(\rho_{t})}\overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j.

Differentiating the top left component shows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}e^{H(\rho_{t})}c(t)r^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{H(\rho_{t})}\dot{c}(t)r^{1/2}+e^{H(\rho_{t})}c(t)r^{1/2}\frac{\partial H}{\partial\rho_{t}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{H(\rho_{t})}\dot{c}(t)r^{1/2}+e^{H(\rho_{t})}c(t)r^{1/2}\ \cdot\ \frac{\partial H}{\partial\rho_{t}}\ \cdot\ \frac{2\dot{K}(t)}{3K(t)}\rho_{t}

where we have used the expression
\rho_{t}=\left({K(t)}/\varepsilon\right)^{2/3}r
to calculate
\tfrac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}
. By Assumption
2
,
3|K(t)|^{2}=2|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
is bounded independent of
\varepsilon
the ratio
{\dot{K}(t)}/{K(t)}
is bounded by a constant. Next, recall that
H(\rho_{t})\sim-\log(\rho_{t})^{-1/2}
, hence
\rho_{t}\partial_{\rho_{t}}H
is bounded at
\rho=0
and decays exponentially hence is bounded by a universal constant. Using Assumption
2
again, the above is bounded by
C|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|
. The other components are identical, yielding the bound (
7.31
) as claimed.

Using this bound yields a bound on the
\mathfrak{B}_{t}
portion of the cross term. Write
\mathfrak{q}=(\varphi,a)
and
\mathfrak{q}=(\psi,b)
. Combining the pointwise (
7.31
) with the expression in (
7.28
) and using Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\langle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{B}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
b\end{pmatrix}\rangle R^{2\nu}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\ \left(|\varphi|\frac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|}{\varepsilon}|b|+|\psi|\frac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|}{\varepsilon}|a|\right)R^{2\nu}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\left(\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{1/2}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/2}}{2}\frac{|b|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{1/2}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/2}}{2}\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{1/2}\left(\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|b|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{1/2}\ (\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H_{\varepsilon,\nu}^{1}}+\|\mathfrak{p}\|^{2}_{H_{\varepsilon,\nu}^{1}})

where in passing to the third line we have used
R_{\varepsilon}\leq c\varepsilon^{1/2}
on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
.

For the curvature term, recall that

{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}=f(\rho_{t})\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)

where
f_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{t})
is the function from Proposition (
4.4
). Using the chain rule and the expression
\partial_{t}\rho_{t}
from above shows that

\Big|(F_{A_{h_{\varepsilon}}})_{tz}\Big|=\Big|f^{\prime}(\rho_{t})\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}\frac{1}{z}\Big|\leq\frac{2\dot{K}(t)}{3K(t)^{1/3}}\Big|f^{\prime}(\rho_{t})\frac{\rho_{t}}{\rho_{t}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}\Big|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}|f^{\prime}(\rho_{t})|.

Moreover, since
f^{\prime}(\rho_{t})
decay exponentially in
\rho_{t}
, (recalling the weight
R\sim\sqrt{1+\rho^{2}}\sim\varepsilon^{-2/3}R_{\varepsilon}
) we have

\Big|(F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}})_{tz}}\Big|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}\Big|\frac{f^{\prime}(\rho_{t})R^{2}}{R^{2}}\Big|\leq C\varepsilon^{2/3}\frac{1}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}

and identically for the
t\overline{z}
component.
Then there’s a pointwise bound,

\displaystyle\langle\varphi,F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}^{\perp}\psi\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\leq C\varepsilon^{2/3}\left(\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}

and integrating yields the result.

∎

The other cross-term that arises comes from the
t
-derivative when decomposing a configuration

\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}+\eta(t)\beta_{t}

as a section of
K(\mathcal{N}_{t})
and a section that is slicewise
L^{2}
-orthogonal to it. Since the
t
-derivatives of
\beta_{t}
depend only on
\Phi_{0}
thus is bounded independent of
\varepsilon
, the condition that
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
is slicewise orthogonal to
\beta
implies that
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}^{\perp},\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})
are
almost
orthogonal. The next lemma gives a precise bound.

The fact that this lemma holds is the key reason we used a normalized
L^{2}
-projection to define the projection
P^{\ell}
in (
7.15
), rather than an
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
where nothing similar is true.

Lemma 7.17
.

Suppose that a configuration
\mathfrak{q}
is written

\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}+\eta(t)\beta_{t}

where
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
is slice-wise
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
\beta_{t}
and
P^{\text{low}}(\mathfrak{q})=0
, i.e.
\eta(t)
has only Fourier modes in the high range. Then

\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)\ \leq\ \|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\ +\ \varepsilon^{5/6}\Big\|\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we denote
t
-derivatives by
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}=\dot{\mathfrak{q}}
. Slicewise orthogonality implies

0\ =\ \partial_{t}\langle\mathfrak{q}^{\perp},\beta_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}\ =\ \langle{\dot{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\perp},\beta_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}+\langle\xi,\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}.

(7.32)

Then expanding, and with the understanding that we use the
L^{2}
norm and inner product throughout,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}(\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}+\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}\|^{2}+\|\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|^{2}+2\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}+\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}+2\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}+\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle.

Focusing on the third term momentarily, we have the following. Recall that by Lemma
6.28
we have the bound
\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}\leq C\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}
. Applying this,

\displaystyle\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}+\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}+\|\eta\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}+2\langle\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t},\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}+\|\eta\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}+2\int_{S^{1}}\langle\dot{\eta}(t),\eta_{t}\rangle\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\langle\beta_{t},\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}-2\Big|\int_{S^{1}}\langle\dot{\eta}(t),\eta_{t}\rangle\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\underbrace{\langle\beta_{t},\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle}_{\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(|\beta_{t}|^{2}+|\dot{\beta}_{t}|^{2})}dAdt\Big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}-2C\Big|\int_{S^{1}}\langle\dot{\eta}(t),\eta_{t}\rangle\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}dt\Big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{2C}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{1/2}\Big|\int_{S^{1}}|\dot{\eta}(t)|^{2}\|\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}dt\Big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}

once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.

Substituting this expression for the third term yields

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}+2\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}+\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}+2\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\rangle-\Big|\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}\|\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}\|^{2}}{2}+\frac{\|\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{1/6}}\Big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\dot{\xi}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}(\eta(t)\beta_{t})\|^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}+2\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\rangle

where we have again used
\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}\leq\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}(\{t\}\times D_{\lambda})}
by Lemma
6.28
, and that
\|\eta(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq L_{0}^{2}\varepsilon\|\dot{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
by the assumption of only high Fourier modes, hence the final term can be absorbed into
\tfrac{1}{4}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}
up to replacing it by
\tfrac{1}{8}
. For the remaining inner product, using the relation
(
7.32
)

\displaystyle|\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\rangle|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{S^{1}}\dot{\eta}(t)\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\langle\dot{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\beta_{t}\rangle dAdt\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{S^{1}}\dot{\eta}(t)\int_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}\langle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp},\dot{\beta}_{t}\rangle dAdt\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\|{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}\|^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{1/6}}+\varepsilon^{1/6}\frac{\|\dot{\eta}\dot{\beta}_{t}\|^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{5/6}{\Big\|\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\perp}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|^{2}}+\varepsilon^{1/6}\frac{\|\dot{\eta}\beta_{t}\|^{2}}{2}

and absorbing the second of these into
\tfrac{1}{8}\|\dot{\eta}(t)\beta_{t}\|^{2}
and moving the first to the other side yields the result.

∎

7.4.2
Weighted Estimates for Dirac Operator

The next two lemmas required for the proof of
7.1
are weighted estimates for the standard Dirac operator and the de-singularized
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-Dirac operator
\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
. These estimates reference a compact operator
K
similar to the one used in (
6.40
), which we now define.

For
\gamma^{\prime}<<1
as in the proof of Lemma
4.7
, define the operator
K_{\varepsilon}
by

K_{\varepsilon}\varphi=\frac{\varphi}{R_{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{1}_{\{r<\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}\}}

(7.33)

where
\mathbb{1}_{\{r<\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}\}}
denotes the indicator function of the ball of radius
r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}
.

Denote by
L^{1,2}_{\nu},L^{2}_{\nu}
as the completion of compactly supported smooth functions in
Y
with respect to the norms

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left({\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{|u|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(7.34)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left({\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}{|u|^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}}\ dV\right)^{1/2}

(7.35)

In these expression
\nabla,dV
denote the structures arising from the product metric on
Y
.

The first lemma is a basic estimate for the weighted Dirac operator (with the trivial connection). The subsequent lemma does the trickier case of the almost-singular connection
A^{h_{\varepsilon}}
. In both, one should have in mind that
\nu\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})
is chosen very close to the upper limit, say,
\nu=\tfrac{1}{4}-10^{-6}
.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
\not{D}
denote the standard Dirac operator with the trivial connection, and fix a weight
\nu\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})
. If
u
is a configuration on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
satisfying the Index 0 boundary conditions of Lemma
7.2
, then

\|u\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}\leq C_{\nu}\left(\|\not{D}u\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|K_{\varepsilon}u\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

where
K_{\varepsilon}
is the compact operator defined above in (
7.33
).

The proof is a standard application of the idea that the weight shifts the spectrum of the operator restricted to slices of constant
r
. This lemma actually holds for
\nu\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
. The upcoming estimate for the de-singularized operator, however, restricts to
\nu\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})
. In this second case, the estimate is almost certainly true for the same range
\nu\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
, but the proof in the more general case appears to require more sophisticated parametrix methods, and is not needed here (see
[
27
,
42
]
).

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
denote the de-singularized
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-Dirac operator, and fix a weight
\nu\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})
. If
u
is a configuration satisfying the boundary constraint portion of the mixed boundary and constraint conditions (Definition
7.14
), i.e.

\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}(\varphi)=0

then once
L_{0}
in the definition of
\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}
is chosen sufficiently large,

\|\varphi\|_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}\leq C_{\nu}\left(\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|K_{\varepsilon}\varphi\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

where
K_{\varepsilon}
is the compact operator defined in (
7.33
).

Proof.

The proof consists of three steps: an interior estimate where
K_{\varepsilon}\neq 0
, an outside estimate where
K_{\varepsilon}=0
, and parametrix patching combining them.

Step 1: Interior Estimate.

The following estimate holds on the interior domain
I_{r}=\{r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}\}
for configurations
\varphi
vanishing on the boundary
r=\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}
.

\int_{I_{r}}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV\leq C\left(\int_{I_{r}}|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi|^{2}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV+\int_{I_{r}}|K_{\varepsilon}\varphi|^{2}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV\right).

This is obvious: integrate by parts and one obtains the first derivative squared and error terms given by
F_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}},\tfrac{\nu}{R_{\varepsilon}}\tfrac{dR_{\varepsilon}}{dr}
. These and the
L^{2}
term on the left hand side are pointwise bounded by multiples of
K_{\varepsilon}
.

Step 2: Outside Estimate.

Let
\not{D}_{A_{0}}
denote the limiting Dirac operator. Recall that
|A^{h_{\varepsilon}}-A_{0}|
is exponentially small in the region
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})-I_{r}
. In this step, we show the estimate for the connection
A_{0}
on all of
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, and in the next step apply it to configurations supported on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})-I_{r}
.

Assume
\nu<1/4
as before, and the
\Pi_{\text{Tw}}^{\circ}(\varphi)=0
. Additionally, assume
\varphi
vanishes on a small neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
(say
r<\varepsilon
). Then

\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)r^{2\nu}\ dV\leq C_{\nu}\left(\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV\right)+C_{\nu}\Big|\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt\Big|.

Notice the weight function here is the genuine radial function
r
rather than the smoothed off version
R_{\varepsilon}
.

To begin, write

\not{D}_{A_{0}}=\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}+\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}

where

\sigma_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix}\hskip 28.45274pt\not{D}_{A_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial_{A_{0}}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{A_{0}}&0\end{pmatrix}

Claim 7.19.1
.

The following hold:

(1)

For
\nu<1/4
,

\int_{D_{\lambda}}\left(\frac{(\nu-\frac{1}{2})^{2}}{r^{2}}|u|^{2}\right)r^{2\nu}\ dV\leq\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}u|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV

(2)

There is a constant
c_{\nu}
such that

\int_{D_{\lambda}}\left(|\nabla^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)r^{2\nu}\ dV\leq c_{\nu}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}u|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV.

Here
\nabla^{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the derivatives in the
D_{\lambda}
-directions. Notice also that the first estimate is asserted without a constant
c_{\nu}
.

Proof.

Write
u=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{pmatrix}
. Since the components decouple, it suffices to show the result for each. First, consider the
\alpha
component. The recall the polar coordinate expression
2\overline{\partial}=e^{i\theta}(\partial_{r}+\tfrac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta})
, and write

\alpha=ar^{-\nu}

for
a
in the space defined by the
\nu=0
version of the norm on the left hand side of the statement of the proposition. Then

2\overline{\partial}_{A_{0}}\alpha=e^{i\theta}(\partial_{r}a+\tfrac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}+\tfrac{-\nu}{r}-\tfrac{1}{2r})r^{-\nu}

\displaystyle\|2\overline{\partial}_{A_{0}}\alpha\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\lambda}}\langle\partial_{r}a+\tfrac{1}{r}(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}))a\ ,\ \partial_{r}a+\tfrac{1}{r}(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}))a\rangle\ rdrd\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\partial_{r}a|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{r^{2}}|(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}))a|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle+\int_{D_{\lambda}}\langle a,-\partial_{r}(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}))a+(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}))\partial_{r}a\rangle drd\theta+\int_{\partial D_{\lambda}}\langle a,i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})a\rangle d\theta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\partial_{r}a|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{r^{2}}|(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}))a|^{2}\ dV.

since
F_{A_{0}}=0
and the restriction of
\alpha
to the boundary has only Fourier modes in
\theta
with, hence the boundary term is positive since
|\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}|<1
. A similar integration by parts holds for
b=\beta r^{-\nu}
, except
i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2})
is replaced by
-i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})
. Since
\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}<0
the boundary term is again positive since the allowed Fourier modes are
k\geq 0
. Both
i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2})
and
-i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})
have lowest eigenvalue
(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})
on the circle. The first bullet point of the claim follows.

For the second bullet point, notice that
i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2})
and
-i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})
are invertible on the circle hence there are estimates

\|a\|_{L^{1,2}(S^{1})}\leq c_{\nu}\|(i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2})a\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}

and likewise for
-i\partial_{\theta}-(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})
. Applying this instead of the
L^{2}
estimate from the eigenvalues shows

\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\nabla(r^{\nu}\varphi)|^{2}+\frac{|r^{\nu}\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\ dV\leq C_{\nu}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV

and the second bullet point follows.
∎

With the claim established, we integrate by parts:

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\partial_{t}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}+|\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}+\langle\varphi,\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A_{0}}\varphi+\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}

\displaystyle+\langle\varphi,\tfrac{2\nu}{r}\sigma_{r}\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}\ dV+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}-\frac{\nu^{2}}{r^{2}}|\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt.

Now apply the first bullet point from the above claim. Since
\nu<\frac{1}{4}
implies
(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}>\nu^{2}
, hence we find

\phantom{\hskip 19.91684pt}\geq C_{\nu}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}r^{2\nu}+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt.

(7.37)

Next, we integrate by parts again and substitute this inequality:

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\partial_{t}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}+|\not{D}^{\mathbb{C}}_{A_{0}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}

\displaystyle+\langle\varphi,\tfrac{2\nu}{r}\sigma_{r}\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\tfrac{1}{2}|\partial_{t}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}+|\not{D}_{A_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}-C_{\nu}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}r^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{\nu}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)r^{2\nu}\ dV-C_{\nu}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}r^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt

where we have now used the second bullet point in Lemma
7.19.1
. Moving the negative term to the other side and applying (
7.37
) yields

\int_{N_{\lambda}}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)r^{2\nu}\ dV\leq C_{\nu}\left(\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\not{D}_{A_{0}}\varphi|^{2}r^{2\nu}\ dV\right)+C_{\nu}\Big|\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle r^{2\nu}rd\theta dt\Big|

(7.38)

completing step 2.

Step 3: Parametrix Patching.
Let
\eta
denote a cutoff equal to 1 at the origin and supported in the region
I_{r}=\{r<\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}\}
such that
d\eta
has support in
r\in[\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}},\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}]
and satisfies

|d\eta|\leq\frac{c}{R_{\varepsilon}}.

We now complete the proof: let
\varphi
be a spinor satisfying
\Pi^{\circ}_{\text{Tw}}(\varphi)=0
and having finite
L^{1,2}_{\nu}
(as in
7.34
). Applying the estimates from Step 1 and Step 2 to
\eta u
and
(1-\eta)u
respectively, and using the fact that
A_{h_{\varepsilon}}
is exponentially close to
A_{0}
in the “outside” region,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\eta u+(1-\eta)u\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu}\left(\|\eta u\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}+\|(1-\eta)u\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu}\left(\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}(\eta u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\not{D}_{A_{0}}((1-\eta)u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|K_{\varepsilon}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\text{b.d. term}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu}\left(\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}(\eta u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}((1-\eta)u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|K_{\varepsilon}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+O(\text{Exp}(-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{\gamma}}))+\text{b.d. term}\right)

where the boundary term is as in (
7.38
). Then,

\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}(\eta u)=\eta\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}u+\gamma(d\eta)u

and likewise for
(1-\eta)
. Substituting this shows the above is bounded by

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\nu}\left(\|\eta\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|(1-\eta)\not{D}_{A_{0}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+2\|\gamma(d\chi)u\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}^{2}+\|K_{\varepsilon}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+O(\text{Exp}(-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{\gamma}}))+\text{b.d. term}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2C_{\nu}\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+4c\|K_{\varepsilon}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\text{b.d. term}

where we have used the definition of
K_{\varepsilon}
and to bound the derivative of the cutoff. The exponentially small term is easy to absorb into
\|u\|_{L^{1,2}|_{\nu}}
once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.

The final step is to absorb the boundary term. This a consequence of the lemma in the following subsection, combined with the fact that the twisted boundary conditions allow only pairings between boundary Fourier modes with
|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{L_{0}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}
, which gives an estimate

\Big|\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA\Big|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial N_{\lambda})}

which is proved precisely in Claim
7.52
during the proof of Theorem
7.1
. Given this, combining this estimate with the next lemma and choosing
L_{0}
sufficiently large completes Step 3 and the proof of Proposition
7.19
.
∎

7.4.3
Boundary Terms

Since the radius of
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is very small, scaling leads to a strong estimate on the (weighted)
L^{2}
-norm of the boundary values. This is one of the key reasons the size of the neighborhood must shrink as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Lemma 7.20
.

(boundary absorption Lemma)

There exists a constant
C_{\nu}
such that on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
,

\int_{\partial{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}}|\varphi|^{2}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ rd\theta dt\leq C_{\nu}\varepsilon^{1/2}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

Proof.

This follows from scaling the trace inequality from the disk of radius
r=1
. First we prove the inequality in the case that
\nu=0
. Let
C_{0}
be the constant for which the two-dimensional trace inequality

\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial D)}\leq C_{0}\int_{D}|\nabla\psi|^{2}+|\psi|^{2}\ dV

holds on the disk of radius
r=1
. Apply this to
\psi(y)=\varphi(\varepsilon^{-1/2}y)
to see

\varepsilon^{-1/2}\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}=\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D_{1})}\leq C_{0}\int_{D}|\nabla\psi|^{2}+|\psi|^{2}\ dV\leq C_{0}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{\varepsilon}\ dV\leq C_{1}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\ dV.

Integrating with respect to
t
yields the inequality in the case that
\nu=0
.

For a general
\nu\in(0,1/4)
, apply the above to
\widetilde{\varphi}=R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\varphi
and combine this with the equivalence of norms as in Lemma
6.7
.

\|\varphi R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{0}}\leq C_{\nu}\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{1,2}_{\nu}}.

∎

7.5
Integration by Parts

This subsection carries out the proof of Theorem
7.1
in the model case. The case of a general metric is treated in the subsequent section by a perturbation argument. The proof in the model case combines the holistic
integration by parts argument described in Equation (
7.24
) with estimates reminiscent of the proof of the uniform invertibility of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t}
in Section
6
(recall Item (2) of Lemma
6.18
).

First we show an estimate

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\ \|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}+\text{b.d. term}

(7.39)

for the weight
\nu=0
. Next, a weighted version of the Weitzenböck formula shows that for a weight
\nu<1/4

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\ \left(\|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

(7.40)

also holds where
K_{\varepsilon}
is as defined in
(\ref{Kedef})
. But in turn, we also have

\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\ \|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

(7.41)

where
\lesssim_{\varepsilon}
denotes a bound by a constant times an appropriate power of
\varepsilon
. Applying
7.39
again to the right hand side of
7.41
and showing the boundary term can be absorbed yields the result after the appropriate bookkeeping of powers of
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
7.1
) The index statement was proved in Proposition
7.15
, and it therefore suffices to show injectivity, for which it is enough to prove the second estimate in the statement of the theorem. The first estimate follows immediately from the second using
\tfrac{1}{R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{-2\nu/3}
. We therefore prove

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}.

(7.42)

By taking limits, it suffices to prove the estimate for smooth configurations. Thus let
\mathfrak{q}=(\varphi,a)
be a smooth configuration satisfying the mixed boundary and projection conditions. In particular, with such a configuration it makes sense to reference
\mathfrak{q}|_{\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}}
for any
t\in S^{1}
and integrate with respect to
t
at the end. The proof now consists of three steps corresponding to the bounds (
7.39
)- (
7.41
) as described above respectively.

Step 1:

The following estimate holds:

\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/6}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\|\mathfrak{\varphi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}.

(7.43)

Omitting the superscript
h_{\varepsilon}
from the proof, we may write

\mathcal{L}=\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{N}_{t}.

Expanding and integrating by parts (this was alluded to in
7.24
) yields

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}|^{2}+|\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}|^{2}+\langle\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q},\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}\rangle+\langle\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q},\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}|^{2}+|\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}|^{2}+\langle\mathfrak{q},\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}\rangle+\langle\mathfrak{q},\mathcal{N}_{t}\sigma_{t}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\rangle\ dV+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}|^{2}+|\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}|^{2}+\langle\mathfrak{q},\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\mathfrak{q}\rangle+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA.

Now,
\mathfrak{q}
may be decomposed into the component in the kernel subbundle and its slicewise
L^{2}
orthogonal complement as in Lemma
7.17
so that

\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}+\eta(t)\beta_{t}

where
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
\beta_{t}
on
\{t\}\times D_{\lambda}
for every
t
. Lemma
7.17
applies to show that

\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)\ \leq\ \|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon^{5/6}\Big\|\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

(7.44)

In addition, by definition of
\beta_{t}
as the span of
\ker(\mathcal{N}_{t})
we have

\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}=\mathcal{N}_{t}(\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}+\eta\beta_{t})=\mathcal{N}_{t}(\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}),

so by slicewise-orthogonality, which implies
\pi^{\text{ker}}_{t}(\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})=0
the main result of Section
6
from Corollary
6.26
shows

\|\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{slice}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/6}}\|\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/6}}\|\mathcal{N}_{t}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(7.45)

Substituting (
7.44
) and (
7.45
) into the integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\varepsilon^{5/6}\Big\|\frac{\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\|\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|_{H^{1}_{slice}}^{2}+\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle\mathfrak{q},\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\mathfrak{q}\rangle

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA.

and combining the slice norm with the
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}
and absorbing the
\varepsilon^{5/6}
term yields

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\|\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}+\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle\mathfrak{q},\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\mathfrak{q}\rangle

(7.47)

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA.

What remains is to show that the
L^{2}
norm of the
\eta\beta_{t}
components dominates the
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
norm of these. This is effectively a consequence of restricting to the high range of Fourier modes. Since
\beta_{t}
is normalized in the
H^{1}_{slice}
-norm, one has

\|\eta(t)\beta_{t}\|_{H^{1}_{slice}}=\|\eta(t)\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}^{2}

(7.48)

thus we will show that

\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\|\eta\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

(7.49)

Using the basic relation that
|a|^{2}=|(a+b)-b|^{2}\leq 2|a+b|^{2}+2|b|^{2}
shows

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\dot{\eta}\beta_{t}+\eta(t)\dot{\beta}_{t}|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{1}}|\dot{\eta}|^{2}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\beta_{t}|^{2}\ dV-\int_{S^{1}}|\eta|^{2}\int_{D_{\lambda}}|\dot{\beta}_{t}|^{2}\ dV.

Next, applying the bounds from Lemma
6.28
that

\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}^{2}\geq c\varepsilon^{7/6}\hskip 28.45274pt\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{7/6}

uniformly in
t
, and use the fact that
\eta(t)
has only Fourier modes for
|\ell|\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}
so that

\|\dot{\eta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{1})}\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon L_{0}^{2}}\|\eta\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}^{2}

shows

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{c\varepsilon^{7/6}}{2\varepsilon L_{0}^{2}}\|\eta\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}^{2}-C\varepsilon^{7/6}\|\eta\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\|\eta(t)\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}^{2}

which is (
7.49
). By (
7.48
) we conclude

\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}(\eta\beta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\|\eta\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{slice}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\|\eta\beta_{t}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}.

(7.50)

Substituting the above (
) into the integration by parts formula (
7.47
) then shows

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{C}\left(\|\eta\beta_{t}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\right)+\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle\mathfrak{q},\{\sigma_{t}\partial_{t},\mathcal{N}_{t}\}\mathfrak{q}\rangle+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA.

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{1/6}}{4C}\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}-2\varepsilon^{1/2}\|\mathfrak{q}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}+\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA.

where we have used Lemma
7.16
to bound the anti-commutator. Once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, we conclude the bound

\|\mathfrak{q}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/6}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\Big|\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA\Big|.

(7.51)

The following assertion therefore finishes Step 1:

Claim 7.20.1
.

\Big|\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA\Big|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}

(7.52)

Proof.

Recall the vector
w_{1}\in E_{-1,0}
such that
w_{1}\perp V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
and
w_{1}\in(\mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial})^{-1}(0)
from the definition of the mixed boundary and projection constraints (equation
7.25
). Let
u_{1}=\sigma_{1}Jw_{1}
and
u_{2}
its orthogonal complement in
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
.

Thus we have that
u_{1},u_{2},w_{1}
are pairwise orthogonal in the Hermitian innner product, with the first two spanning
V_{t}^{\Phi_{0}}
and the relation that
\sigma_{t}Jw_{1}=u_{1}
while
\langle\sigma_{t}Jw_{1},u_{2}\rangle=0
in the Hermitian inner product. Now write the
E_{-1,0}
-component of
\varphi|_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}
as

\pi^{(-1,0)}(\varphi|_{\partial N_{\varepsilon}})=a_{1}(t)u_{1}(t)+a_{2}(t)u_{2}(t)+b_{1}(t)w_{1}(t)

so that

|a_{1}(t)|^{2}+|a_{2}(t)|^{2}+|b_{1}(t)|^{2}\leq|\varphi(t)|^{2}

(7.53)

The twisted boundary conditions dictate that
b_{1}(t)
has only
t
-Fourier modes with
|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}
. Additionally, we have the time derivative

\pi^{(-1,0)}(\partial_{t}\varphi|_{\partial N_{\varepsilon}})=\dot{a}_{1}u_{1}+a_{1}\dot{u}_{1}\ +\ \dot{a}_{2}u_{2}+a_{2}\dot{u}_{2}\ +\ \dot{b}_{1}w_{1}+b_{1}\dot{w}_{1}.

where the dependence of each on
t
is implicit. Evaluating the inner product on the left hand side of the expression (
7.52
), we have

\displaystyle\langle-\sigma_{t}J\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\ dA

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{1}J(a_{1}u_{1}\ +\ a_{2}u_{2}\ +\ b_{1}w_{1})\ ,\ \dot{a}_{1}u_{1}\ +\ \dot{a}_{2}u_{2}\ +\ \dot{b}_{1}w_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle\ +\ \langle\sigma_{1}J(a_{1}u_{1}\ +\ a_{2}u_{2}\ +\ b_{1}w_{1})\ ,\ a_{1}\dot{u}_{1}\ +\ a_{2}\dot{u}_{2}\ +\ b_{1}\dot{w}_{1}\rangle.

Since the Lagriangian property implies
\sigma Jw_{1}\perp w_{1}
and likewise for
u_{i}
this reduces to

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{1}J(a_{1}u_{1}+a_{2}u_{2}),\dot{b}_{1}w_{1}\rangle+\langle\sigma_{1}J(b_{1}w_{1}),\dot{a}_{1}u_{1}+\dot{a}_{2}u_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle+C|\varphi(t)|^{2}\Big(|\dot{u}_{1}|+|\dot{u}_{2}|+|\dot{w}_{1}|\Big)

where we have used (
7.53
) on all the terms where the derivative hits the basis vectors. In fact, using the orthogonality conditions for the chosen basis, and the fact that their time derivatives are bounded by a constant depending only on
\Phi_{0}
, the above reduces to

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\langle a_{1},\dot{b}_{1}\rangle+\langle b_{1},\dot{a}_{1}\rangle+C|\varphi(t)|^{2}

Next, since
\dot{b}_{1}
has only Fourier modes with
|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}
, integrating the above yields we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\langle a_{1},\dot{b}_{1}\rangle+\langle b_{1},\dot{a}_{1}\rangle\ dA

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{S^{1}_{\theta}}\Big(\sum_{|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}}|\ell||(a_{1})_{\ell}||(b_{1})_{\ell}|\Big)rd\theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\int_{S_{\theta}^{1}}\Big(\sum_{|\ell|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}}|(a_{1})_{\ell}|^{2}+|(b_{1})_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)rd\theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}

and once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, the additional factor of
C|\varphi(t)|^{2}
can be absorbed.
∎

Step 2:
Let
K_{\varepsilon}
denote the compact operator defined in (
7.33
). The following estimate holds for
\nu\in(0,1/4)
and in particular for, say,
|\nu-\tfrac{1}{4}|<<1
, say
\nu=\tfrac{1}{4}-10^{-6}
.

\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\|K_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}.

(7.54)

This follows readily from the Weitzenböck formula and the weighted estimates of Lemma
7.18
and Proposition
7.19
in the previous subsection. Expanding as in
2.14
with the cross-term kept explicit,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{(}\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\mathbb{d}a\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle+\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\\
\mathbb{d}a\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}+\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\\
\mathbb{d}a\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV..

Since
\mathbb{d}=-\not{D}
up to viewing
a=(\zeta,\omega)
