under the isomorphisms of Section 7.1, Lemma
7.18
is applicable. Applying this lemma to
\mathbb{d}
and Proposition
7.19
to
\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
shows

\displaystyle C\left(\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{(}\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\nabla a\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\Big\|\frac{\varphi}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\\
\mathbb{d}a\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}+\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}a\\
\varphi\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{\mu(\not{D}\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\\
\gamma(\mathbb{d}a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}+\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\varphi\\
\mathbb{d}a\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}dV

\displaystyle+\ \int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}a\\
\varphi\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{\mu(\not{D}\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\\
\gamma(\mathbb{d}a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV.

Integrating by parts on the first cross term, and noting that the boundary conditions imply the boundary term vanishes (up to rewriting
a=(\zeta,\omega)
this is the same boundary term that vanishes in
6.22.1
and
6.50
in Section 6), and the expressions
2.29
from the proof of the Weitzenböck formula yield

\displaystyle\left(\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{(}\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle(\varphi,a),\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,a)\rangle_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle+\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \sigma(dr)\frac{2\nu}{R_{\varepsilon}}\frac{dR_{\varepsilon}}{dr}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV.

Recall from (
7.33
) that
K_{\varepsilon}
is supported in the region
r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}
. Restricting to this region, Young’s inequality shows

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix},\sigma(dr)\tfrac{2\nu}{R_{\varepsilon}}\tfrac{dR_{\varepsilon}}{dr}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4C\Big\|\frac{(\varphi,a)}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\frac{1}{4C\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}^{2}+\|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\frac{1}{4C}\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

and,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle(\varphi,a),\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,a)\rangle_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4C\varepsilon^{2}}\|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}^{2}+4C\int_{r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}}|\varphi|^{2}\frac{|\nabla_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4C}\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}+C\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}.

The last inequality follows from the inequality following inequality for the re-scaled quantities, which implies the quantity to the right of it.

\frac{|\nabla_{A^{H}}\Phi^{H}|^{2}}{|\Phi^{H}|^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{R^{2}}\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{|\nabla_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{R^{2}_{\varepsilon}}

Indeed,
\Phi^{H}
to
\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}
introduces the same factor on the top and the bottom, while rescaling the covariant derivative
\nabla_{A^{H}}
in
\rho
coordinates to
\nabla_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}
in
r
coordinates introduces a factor of
\left(\frac{K}{\varepsilon}\right)^{4/3}\leq C\frac{R^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}
. This shows the desired estimate for the
r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}
region.

Proceeding to the region where
r\geq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}
, we claim that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle(\varphi,a),\mathfrak{B}(\varphi,a)\rangle_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{3\gamma^{\prime}/2}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}^{2}+\Big\|\frac{\varphi}{R_{\varepsilon}}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)

(7.55)

here. To see this note the following things.

First, since up to exponentially small factors,
\mathfrak{B}
only sees the imaginary components of the spinor in this region, and

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}|\nabla_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|\sim\frac{1}{r^{1/2}\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon^{3\gamma^{\prime}/2}\frac{r}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{3\gamma^{\prime}/2}\frac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}

where
\sim
denotes a bound up to an exponentially small error (which are easily absorbed by the norm).

Likewise, since
\sigma(dr)
is a real form,
\sigma(dr)\gamma(a)
is a purely-imaginary form, and the term arising from the derivative of the weights similarly only sees the
\ker(\mu(\_,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}))^{\perp}
components. Thus

\displaystyle\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
a\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \sigma(dr)\frac{2\nu}{R_{\varepsilon}}\frac{dR_{\varepsilon}}{dr}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\\
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})}{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3\gamma^{\prime}/2}}\frac{|(\varphi^{\perp},a)|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\varepsilon^{3\gamma^{\prime}/2}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{3\gamma^{\prime}/2}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}|\gamma(a)\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}|\mu(\varphi,\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})|^{2}\right)

since
\frac{1}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2\gamma}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}\leq\varepsilon^{3\gamma}\frac{r}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{3\gamma}\frac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. Combining these yields (
7.55
). Since
3\gamma^{\prime}/2>0
, it follows from (
7.55
) that for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, the cross-terms can therefore be absorbed in the outside region as on the inside.

Putting the regions together again,

\left(\|K_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{(}\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{C}\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

up to increasing
C
by a constant factor. This completes Step
2
.

Step 3:
The following estimate holds, again for
|\tfrac{1}{4}-\nu|<<1
:

\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\varepsilon^{1/6-2\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

(7.56)

where
\gamma_{2}<<1
again.

This follows from the previous steps and the boundary absorption Lemma
7.20
. Combining the inequalities from the previous steps. Beginning with the inequality from Step 2:

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\|K_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\int_{r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}}\frac{|\mathfrak{q}|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\varepsilon^{(2/3-\gamma^{\prime})2\nu}\int_{r\leq\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma^{\prime}}}\frac{|\mathfrak{q}|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\varepsilon^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-\tfrac{2}{3}(\nu-\tfrac{1}{4})-2\gamma^{\prime}\nu}\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\varepsilon^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2\gamma_{2}}\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

where
\gamma_{2}={\tfrac{2}{3}(\nu-\tfrac{1}{4})+\gamma^{\prime}\nu}<<1
. In this, we have used the lower bound that
\frac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\geq\frac{C}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}
(which follows from the third bullet point of Lemma
4.6
) on the form component to absorb
K_{\varepsilon}
into the
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}
-norm.

Next applying the estimate from Step 1, the above shows

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+C\varepsilon^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-\gamma_{2}}\left(\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/6}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\varepsilon^{1/6-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/3-\gamma_{2}}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\mathfrak{q}|^{2}dA

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\varepsilon^{1/6-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/3-\gamma_{2}}(\varepsilon^{1/2})^{-2\nu}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\mathfrak{q}|^{2}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}dA

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\varepsilon^{1/6-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}L_{0}}\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\mathfrak{q}|^{2}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}dA

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\nu}}+\varepsilon^{1/6-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)+{C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}}\|\mathfrak{q}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}

where in the last line we have applied the boundary absorption Lemma
7.20
to show

\int_{\partial N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\varphi|^{2}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ rd\theta dt\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

and recalled that
L_{0}
is a universal constant independent of
\varepsilon
. Up to increasing
C
(by a factor of 2, say), the last term may be absorbed on the left hand side once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, yielding the desired estimate (
7.56
) and completing Step 3.

Since
R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\leq\varepsilon^{\nu}<\varepsilon^{1/6-2\gamma}
on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, the second term
\varepsilon^{1/6-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}
in the parentheses dominates the first. Taking the square root yields the desired estimate (
7.42
), completing the proof of Theorem
7.1
in the model case.
∎

7.6
General Metric

To complete the proof, we extend the above result from the model case to the general case. Thus we now assume, in full generality, that in geodesic normal coordinates and a trivialization on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}
), we have

\displaystyle g=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}+h\hskip 28.45274ptB_{0}=\text{ is a fixed smooth $SU(2)$-connection}

where
h
is described in Definition
3.4
, and that

\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}c(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
d(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{d}(t)r^{1/2}\ \ \ \ \ \\
\overline{c}(t)r^{1/2}e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j+\Phi^{h.o.}\hskip 56.9055ptA_{0}=\frac{i}{2}d\theta+\epsilon_{j}\tfrac{i}{2}dt

where
\Phi^{h.o.}
is the higher order terms given in Proposition
3.7
. Thus we have bounds

\displaystyle\|h\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cr

\displaystyle\|\nabla h\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\|B_{0}+\epsilon_{j}\tfrac{i}{2}dt\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{h.o.}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cr^{3/2}

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Phi^{h.o.}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cr^{1/2}

for constants
C
independent of
\varepsilon
. We also re-introduce the cut-off function as in Definition
4.5
to replace
h_{\varepsilon}(r)
with
\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)h_{\varepsilon}(r)
where
\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)
is equal to
1
on a neighborhood of radius
c\varepsilon^{1/2}
for
c<1
and supported in the neighborhood of radius
\varepsilon^{1/2}
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
7.1
in the case of the above). Let
\mathcal{L}^{\text{Euc}}
now denote the model operator. The result of Section
7.5
show that for
\nu<1/4
,

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}^{\text{Euc}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}.

Thus it suffices to show that

\|(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}-\mathcal{L}^{\text{Euc}})(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/16}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}

(7.57)

for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small and
\nu
sufficiently close to
1/4
. We may write

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}-\mathcal{L}^{\text{Euc}})(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|(\not{D}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}}-\not{D}^{\text{Euc}}_{A^{h_{\varepsilon}}})\varphi\|_{L^{2}}+\|(\mathbb{d}-\mathbb{d}^{\text{Euc}})a\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|(\gamma-\gamma^{\text{Euc}})(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\|(\mu-\mu^{\text{Euc}})(\varphi,\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}}+\|\mu(\varphi,\tfrac{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}}{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}}.

Bounding each term individually, one has

\displaystyle\|(\mathbb{d}-\mathbb{d}^{\text{Euc}})a\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}Cr^{2}|\nabla a|^{2}+C|a|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{2/3}\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\left(|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\varepsilon^{1/3}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\right)^{2}

and identically, for the Dirac operator with trivial connection

\displaystyle\|(\not{D}-\not{D}^{\text{Euc}})\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}Cr^{2}|\nabla a|^{2}+C|a|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\nabla a|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\varepsilon^{1/3}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\right)^{2}

while for the connection term

\displaystyle\|(\gamma-\gamma^{\text{Euc}})(A^{h_{\varepsilon}})\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}Cr^{2}|A^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}|\varphi|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}r^{3/2}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\varepsilon^{3/8}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\|B_{0}+\epsilon_{j}\tfrac{i}{2}dt\varphi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\varepsilon^{3/4}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}})^{2}.

since
r\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}
. This completes the two diagonal terms.

For the off-diagonal terms, we have

\displaystyle\|(\gamma-\gamma^{\text{Euc}})(a)\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}Cr^{2}\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+Cr^{2}\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}Cr^{3/2}\frac{|a|^{2}|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{3/8}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\right)^{2}

and since
|(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}|\leq Cr|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|\leq Cr|(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{l.o.}|
. And

\displaystyle\|(\mu-\mu^{\text{Euc}})(\varphi,\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\mu(\varphi,\tfrac{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{h.o.}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}}Cr^{2}|\varphi|^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\lambda(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}}C\frac{r^{2}\varepsilon^{1/2}}{{\varepsilon^{2}}}R_{\varepsilon}^{2-2\nu}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}^{2\nu}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{(\varepsilon^{1/2})^{4.5}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C(\varepsilon^{1/8}\|(\varphi,a)\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}})^{2}.

since
r\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}
.

Combining these estimates gives (
7.57
). This shows that

\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}:H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}\to L^{2}

is invertible, and the same bound

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}}

holds on the inverse as in the model case, where
H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}
still denotes the norm formed using the Euclidean structures and the model case. Switching the norm to the one formed using the non-model structures is essentially the same estimates, but we now only need them to be bounded by a uniform constant. Re-introducing the cut-off function
\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)
clearly introduces only an exponentially small change, which is of no consequence. This completes the proof of Theorem
7.1
in the general case.

∎

8
Implicit Function Theorem

In this final section we conclude the proofs of the main results Theorems
1.2
-
1.4
. The existence of the fiducial solutions advertised in Theorem
1.2
is concluded by applying the standard Inverse Function Theorem to solve the non-linear equation (
5.1
), which was

(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}+Q)(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})=E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}.

(8.1)

up to decreasing the size of tubular neighborhood by a factor of
1/2
.

The following quantitative version of the Inverse Function Theorem is taken from
[
16
]
(Theorem 18.3.6).

Theorem 8.1
.

(Inverse Function Theorem)

Let
H_{1},H_{2}
be Hilbert spaces, and
S:H_{1}\to H_{2}
a continuous map between them satisfying
S(0)=0
. Suppose that
S
has the form

S=\mathcal{L}+Q

where
\mathcal{L}
is linear and invertible, and
Q
is uniformly Lipschitz on the
\eta_{1}
radius ball
B_{\eta_{1}}(H_{1})\subset H_{1}
with Lipschitz constant
M
, i.e.

\|x_{1}\|_{1},\|x_{2}\|_{1}\leq\eta_{1}\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\hskip 28.45274pt\|Q(x_{1})-Q(x_{2})\|_{2}\leq M\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{1}.

If
M\leq 1/\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|
, then
S
is injective on
B_{\eta_{1}}(H_{1})
and the image contains the ball
B_{\eta_{2}}(H_{2})\subset H_{2}
where

\eta_{2}=\frac{\eta_{1}(1-M\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|)}{\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|}.

In particular, for every
y\in B_{\eta_{2}}(H_{2})
there is a unique
x\in B_{\eta_{1}}(H_{1})
satisfying

S(x)=y.

∎

Let
N_{\lambda/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
denote the tubular neighborhood of radius
\tfrac{3}{4}\lambda=\tfrac{3}{4}\varepsilon^{1/2}
, and let
\chi_{1}
denote a logarithmic cut-off function equal to
1
on
N_{\lambda/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and supported in
N_{\lambda}
such that

|d\chi_{1}|\leq\frac{C}{r}.

(8.2)

To solve Equation (
8.1
) on
N_{3\lambda/4}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
it suffices to solve

(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}+\chi_{1}^{2}Q)(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})=E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}.

(8.3)

on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, since
E_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}
is supported on the inner neighborhood as
\chi_{\varepsilon}(r)h_{\varepsilon}(r)=0
for
r\geq c\varepsilon^{1/2}
. The introduction of
\chi_{1}
allows us to apply Sobolev inequalities on the closed manifold with
\varepsilon
-independent constant, rather than scaling them to
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
.

We have the following interpolation bound for configurations
(\varphi,a)\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}
:

Lemma 8.2
.

For
0<\nu<1/4
,

\|\chi_{1}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{4}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\leq C\varepsilon^{-\nu/6}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}

Proof.

The Gagliardo-Nirenberg Interpolation inequality on
Y
(see Equation (1.4) of
[
1
]
and apply this using a partition of unity) states

\|u\|_{L^{4}(Y)}^{2}\leq C\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{3/2}+\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right).

Applying this to the configuration
\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}=\chi_{1}(\varphi,a)
yields

\displaystyle\|\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{4}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\|\nabla(\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q})\|^{3/2}_{L^{2}}+\|\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-4\nu/3}\left(\|R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\ \cdot\ \|R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\nabla(\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q})\|^{3/2}_{L^{2}}+\|R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)

since
R_{\varepsilon}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2/3}
. Then, as
1/R_{\varepsilon}\geq c\varepsilon^{-1/2}
on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
,

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-4\nu/3}\varepsilon^{1/4}\left(\|\tfrac{R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\ \cdot\ \|R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\nabla(\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q})\|^{3/2}_{L^{2}}+\|\tfrac{R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}}{R_{\varepsilon}}\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-\nu/3}\left(\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{1/2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}\ \cdot\ \left(\|R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}\chi_{1}\nabla\mathfrak{q})\|_{L^{2}}+\|R_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}d\chi_{1}\mathfrak{q}\|\right)^{3/2}+\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}\right)

and by, (
8.2
),
|d\chi_{1}|\leq\tfrac{C}{R_{\varepsilon}}
. Hence, also using that
\tfrac{1}{R_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\lesssim\tfrac{|\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for the connection component
a
,

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-\nu/3}\left(\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{1/2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}\ \cdot\ \left(\|\mathfrak{q}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}+\|\mathfrak{q}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}\right)^{3/2}+\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-\nu/3}\|\mathfrak{q}\|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}.

and taking the square root completes the Lemma.
∎

Using this to bound the quadratic term we now conclude the proofs of Theorem
1.2
-
1.4
and Corollary
1.3
. The statements given in the introduction follow from the statements here after replacing
\lambda
by
\lambda/2
. First, we apply the Inverse Function Theorem
8.1
to solve Equation (
8.1
) and thus conclude the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
1.2
). In the notation of the statement of the Inverse Function Theorem
8.1
, set

H_{1}=H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\hskip 56.9055ptH_{2}=L^{2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))

with
|\tfrac{1}{4}-\nu|<<1
as before. Theorem
7.1
shows that as a map
H_{1}\to H_{2}
we have

\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}\|\leq C_{\mathcal{L}}\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}

Set
\eta_{1}=C_{0}(\varepsilon)\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}\|
where
C_{0}(\varepsilon)=C_{0}\varepsilon^{-\gamma}
and
C_{0}
is a fixed constant so that the error of Lemma
4.7
obeys
\|E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\tfrac{1}{10}C_{0}(\varepsilon)
. For two configurations
\mathfrak{q}_{1}=(\varphi_{1},a_{1})
and
\mathfrak{q}_{2}=(\varphi_{2},a_{2})
we may write

\chi_{1}^{2}Q(\mathfrak{q}_{1})-\chi_{1}^{2}Q(\mathfrak{q}_{2})=\chi_{1}(\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2})\#\chi_{1}(\mathfrak{q}_{1}-\mathfrak{q}_{2})

where
\#
denotes a pointwise quadratic map. Then using Lemma
8.2
,
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in B_{\eta_{1}}(0)\subseteq H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}
implies

\displaystyle\|\chi_{1}^{2}Q(\mathfrak{q}_{1})-\chi_{1}^{2}Q(\mathfrak{q}_{2})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\chi_{1}(\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2})\|_{L^{4}(N_{\lambda})}\cdot\|\chi_{1}(\mathfrak{q}_{1}-\mathfrak{q}_{2})\|_{L^{4}(N_{\lambda})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\chi_{1}(\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2})\|_{L^{4}(N_{\lambda})}\cdot\|\chi_{1}(\mathfrak{q}_{1}-\mathfrak{q}_{2})\|_{L^{4}(N_{\lambda})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-\nu/3}\|\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}\cdot\|\mathfrak{q}_{1}-\mathfrak{q}_{2}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-\nu/3}2C_{0}(\varepsilon)C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}\|\mathfrak{q}_{1}-\mathfrak{q}_{2}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2C_{0}(\varepsilon)C\varepsilon^{-\gamma_{3}}\|\mathfrak{q}_{1}-\mathfrak{q}_{2}\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda})}

for some
\gamma_{3}<<1
. Thus the Lipschitz bound is satisfied with
M=2C_{0}(\varepsilon)C_{\mathcal{L}}\varepsilon^{-\gamma_{3}}\leq\tfrac{1}{C_{\mathcal{L}}\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}}=\frac{1}{\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|}
, and
M\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|<\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}}<\frac{1}{2}
once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. The Inverse Function Theorem applies with

\eta_{2}=\frac{\eta_{1}(1-M\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|)}{\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|}\geq C_{0}(\varepsilon)(1-\tfrac{1}{2})>\tfrac{C_{0}(\varepsilon)}{2},

and by our choice of
C_{0}(\varepsilon)
, the equation

(\mathcal{L}_{(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon)}+\chi_{1}^{2}Q)(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})=E^{(0)}_{\varepsilon}

therefore admits a unique solution
(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))
which then solves the Seiberg-Witten equation on
N_{3\lambda/4}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
,
such that

\|(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}.

(8.4)

The configurations

(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon}):=\Big(\frac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon},A^{h_{\varepsilon}}\Big)+(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})

(8.5)

are then the desired family of model solutions. This construction is local, so the proof applies independently on every component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
once
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. ∎

Corollary
1.3
is deduced directly from this using the main results of
[
24
]
. The details are given in Appendix
A
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
1.4
) Continuing to denote the cut-off function defined in
8.2
by
\chi_{1}
, denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\text{App}}
the linearization at the approximate solutions

(\Phi^{\text{App}}_{\varepsilon},A^{\text{app}}_{\varepsilon}):=\Big(\frac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon},A^{h_{\varepsilon}}\Big)+\chi_{1}^{2}(\varphi_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})

(8.6)

(so that in the definition (
1.7
) preceding the statement of Theorem
1.4
one takes
\chi=\chi_{1}^{2}
). Similarly denote the linearization at the de-singularized configurations by
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
. Lemma (
8.2
) and (
8.4
) imply

\|(\mathcal{L}^{\text{App}}-\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}})(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{2}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}\leq\varepsilon^{-\gamma_{4}}\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}(N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))}

for
\gamma_{4}<<1
, and as above,
\|(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{-1}\|_{L^{2}\to H^{1}_{\nu}}\leq C_{\mathcal{L}}\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}}
so

(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}})^{-1}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}+(\mathcal{L}^{\text{App}}-\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}})\Big)=Id+O(\varepsilon^{1/12-\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{4}}).

It follows that the same invertibility statement given in Theorem
7.1
for
\mathcal{L}^{h_{\varepsilon}}
holds for
\mathcal{L}^{\text{App}}
up to possibly increasing the constants by a factor of 2.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix I: Bootstrapping Convergence

In this section we use the results of
[
24
]
to deduce Corollary
1.3
by a straightforward (but slightly detailed) bootstrapping argument. Notice that the bounds on
\varphi^{\text{Re}}
stated in Corollary
1.3
follow directly from Theorem
7.1
and the definition of the
H^{1}_{\varepsilon,\nu}
-norm. For the
(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)
components, we apply the following result found in (Appendix I of)
[
24
]
. In it, we assume that

\left(\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon},A_{0}\right)+(\varphi,a)

(A.1)

is a solution of the two-spinor Seiberg-Witten equations near a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0},\Phi_{0})
satisfying Assumptions (
1
)-(
2
).

Proposition A.1
.

There exists a
c_{1}>0
such that if
K_{\varepsilon}\Subset N_{\lambda/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
are an
\varepsilon
-parameterized family of compact subsets satisfying
\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z}_{0})\geq c_{1}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma_{1}}
for any
\gamma_{1}>0
, and one has

\varepsilon\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{1,6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\to 0\hskip 56.9055pt\varepsilon^{2/3}\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{C^{0}(K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})}\to 0.

(A.2)

for some
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\supset K_{\varepsilon}
with
\text{dist}(Y-K_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},K_{\varepsilon})\geq\tfrac{c_{1}}{2}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma_{1}}
, then

\|(\varphi^{\text{Im}}_{\varepsilon},a_{\varepsilon})\|_{C^{0}(K_{\varepsilon})}\leq\frac{C}{|\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z})|^{3/2}\varepsilon}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{c}{\varepsilon}\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z})^{3/2}\right).

∎

Suppose that
K_{\varepsilon}
are a family of compact subsets satisfying
\text{dist}(K_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{Z}_{0})\geq c_{1}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma_{1}}
as in the statement of the proposition. The conclusion of the proof of Theorem
1.2
and the estimates of Theorem
7.1
is that the correction to
(\tfrac{\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})
that yields the model solutions satisfies
\|(\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\text{mod}},a_{\varepsilon}^{\text{mod}})\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/10}
. Since
(\Phi^{h_{\varepsilon}},A^{h_{\varepsilon}})-(\Phi_{0},A_{0})
is exponentially small on
K_{\varepsilon}
, then writing the model solutions in the form (
A.1
), one has

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/10}

(A.3)

as well. Thus it suffices to show this implies the bounds (
A.2
) hold. Once this is shown, applying Proposition (
A.1
) then yields Corollary
1.3
.

The bound (
A.3
) is shown by a standard bootstrapping argument, though a rather intricate one as if one is not careful, several applications of the elliptic estimates will pick up powers of
\varepsilon^{-1}
too large for the desired bounds to hold.

Let

K_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}=\{\tfrac{c_{1}}{2}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma_{1}}\leq r\leq\tfrac{3\lambda}{4}\}

denote the closed annulus of the indicated radii. By assumption, it contains
K_{\varepsilon}
. In addition, let

K_{\varepsilon}\Subset K_{\varepsilon}^{(m)}\Subset\ldots\Subset K_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}

be a nested sequence of finitely many (specifically 7) nested closed annuli. Choose them so that

K_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}:=K_{\varepsilon}^{(m)}=\{\tfrac{3c_{1}}{4}\varepsilon^{2/3-\gamma_{1}}\leq r\leq\tfrac{5\lambda}{8}\}.

Additionally, for each
n=1,...,m
let
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}
denote a logarithmic cut-off function supported on
K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}
and equal to 1 on
K_{\varepsilon}^{(n+1)}
. They may be chosen so that

|d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}|\leq\frac{C}{r}

uniformly. Finally, we fix a smooth background connection extending
d
in the chosen trivialization of
S_{E}
on
N_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
(given in Lemma
3.5
) with respect to which the Sobolev norms are taken.

Claim A.1.1
.

The bound (
A.3
) implies the following.

•

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/10}

•

\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}+\|\tfrac{\mu(\varphi^{\text{Im}},\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-(\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{3}{p})}.

Proof.

Both bullet points follow from applying the global (on
Y
) Sobolev and interpolation inequalities to
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(\varphi,a)
. Indeed, for the first bullet point one has

\|(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}\leq\|\chi^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})}=\|\chi^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{6}(Y)}\leq C\|\chi^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,a)\|_{L^{1,2}(Y)}\leq C\|(\varphi,a)\|_{H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})}

where we have used that
|\nabla(\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\varphi,a)|\leq\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}|\nabla(\varphi,a)|+\tfrac{\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}|(\varphi,a)|}{R_{\varepsilon}}.

For the second bullet point, we apply the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality on
Y
, which states that for
2<p<6
,

\|u\|_{L^{p}(Y)}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{1-\alpha}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\hskip 28.45274pt0<\alpha=\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{3}{p}<1.

(A.4)

To derive this from the standard version for scalar functions on bounded domains in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
(see, for instance, Equation (1.4) in
[
1
]
) use a partition of unity and apply the standard result to
|u|
, then invoke Kato’s inequality. Applying (
A.4
) to
\gamma(\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}
shows that

\displaystyle\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{1-\alpha}\|\nabla(\gamma(\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\varepsilon^{-1/10}\right)^{1-\alpha}\left[\|\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla a\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}+\|\tfrac{\nabla(\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}a\|^{\alpha}_{L^{2}}\right]+\varepsilon^{-1/10}

and
|\nabla(\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\Phi_{0}|=|(\nabla\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\Phi_{0}+\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\nabla\Phi_{0}|\lesssim|\tfrac{1}{r}\Phi_{0}|
on the support of
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
where
\tfrac{1}{r}\leq c\varepsilon^{-2/3}
, so the above is bounded by

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\varepsilon^{-1/10}\right)^{1-\alpha}\left(\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\varepsilon^{-\alpha/10}+\varepsilon^{-2\alpha/3}\varepsilon^{-\alpha/10}\right).

The same applies to
\tfrac{\mu(\varphi^{\text{Im}},\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-\alpha}=C\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-(\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{3}{p})}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\|\tfrac{\mu(\varphi^{\text{Im}},\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-\alpha}=C\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-(\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{3}{p})}.

(A.6)

∎

With this in hand, we claim:

Claim A.1.2
.

The following bounds are satisfied on
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=K_{\varepsilon}^{(m)}
:

\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{1,6}(K_{\varepsilon})}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\hskip 85.35826pt\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{C^{0}(K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}.

(A.7)

In particular, the hypotheses of Proposition
A.1
are satisfied.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{A}
denote a smooth background connection which extends over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. We have the elliptic estimates

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{L^{k,p}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k,p}\left(\|\not{D}_{\widetilde{A}}\varphi\|_{L^{k-1,p}(Y)}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\|a\|_{L^{k,p}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k,p}\left(\|\mathbb{d}a\|_{L^{k-1,p}(Y)}+\|a\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\right)

for for Sobolev norms on the closed manifold
Y
.

Next, for
n=1,...,6
, applying the above estimates to
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}(\varphi,a)
yields

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{L^{k,p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n+1)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k,p}\left(\|\not{D}_{\widetilde{A}}\varphi\|_{L^{k-1,p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}+\|\gamma(d\chi^{(n)}_{\varepsilon})\varphi\|_{L^{k-1,p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}\right)

\displaystyle\|a\|_{L^{k,p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n+1)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k,p}\left(\|\mathbb{d}a\|_{L^{k-1,p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}+\|\sigma(d\chi^{(n)}_{\varepsilon})a\|_{L^{k-1,p}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}+\|a\|_{L^{2}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}\right)

on the nested annuli
K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}
. We will apply these estimates using the fact that
(\varphi^{\text{Re}},\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)
solve the following non-linear equations on
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\text{Re}}_{A_{0}}\varphi^{\text{Re}}\ \ \hskip 28.45274pt\ \ +\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Im}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(A.8)

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\text{Re}}_{A_{0}}\varphi^{\text{Im}}+\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}+\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Re}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(A.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{d}a+\tfrac{\mu(\varphi^{\text{Im}},\Phi_{0})}{\varepsilon}+\mu(\varphi^{\text{Im}},\varphi^{\text{Re}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(A.10)

Now we bootstrap: apply the elliptic estimate of
\not{D}_{\widetilde{A}}
for
(k,p)=(1,\tfrac{12}{5})
to
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\varphi^{\text{Im}}
:

\displaystyle\|\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{1,12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(3)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1,12/5}\Big(\|\gamma(a)\tfrac{\Phi_{0}}{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}+\|(\widetilde{A}-A_{0})\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}+\|\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}

\displaystyle+\|\gamma(d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(5)})\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}+\|\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-(\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{3}{12/5})}+\varepsilon^{-1/3}\varepsilon^{-1/10}+\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-1/10}+\varepsilon^{-1/3}\varepsilon^{-1/10}+\varepsilon^{-1/10}\right)

where we have used the interpolation bound from the second item of
A.1.1
on the first term. For the second term, we apply Hölder’s inequality with exponents
p=\tfrac{5}{2}
and
q=\tfrac{5}{3}
to bound this by
\|\tfrac{1}{r}\|_{L^{4}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{6}}
. Since
\|\tfrac{1}{r}\|_{L^{4}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(n)})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1/3}
, the bound on the second term above follows again using the first item of
A.1.1
to bound
\|\varphi\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(2)})}
. For the third term,
\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Re}}
, we have simply applied Cauchy-Schwartz and the fact that
L^{24/5}\hookrightarrow L^{6}
then used the first bullet point of Claim
A.1.1
once again. The fourth term is identical to the second since
d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(5)}\sim\tfrac{1}{r}
as well, and the
L^{2}
-term is much smaller.

The exact same argument applies to
a
using the elliptic estimate for
\mathbb{d}a
, but without the
\widetilde{A}-A_{0}
term. Together, this gives

\|(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)\|_{L^{1,12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(3)})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/3-1/10}.

(A.11)

This implies, via the Sobolev embedding
L^{1,12/5}\hookrightarrow L^{12}
that

\|(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)\|_{L^{12}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1/3-1/10}.

(A.12)

as well. Indeed, applying the Sobolev inequality on
Y
to
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(3)}(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)
we find

\|(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)\|_{L^{12}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\leq\|(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)\|_{L^{1,12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(3)})}+\|d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(3)}(\varphi^{\text{Im}},a)\|_{L^{12/5}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(3)})}

and since
|d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(3)}|\lesssim\tfrac{1}{r}\lesssim\tfrac{|\Phi_{0}|}{\varepsilon}
on
K_{\varepsilon}^{(0)}
, the second bullet point of
A.1.1
shows the second term is strictly smaller than the first, giving (
A.12
).

With the above bound in hand, we now apply two final elliptic estimates to yield the two inequalities asserted. First, apply the
\not{D}_{\widetilde{A}}
estimate for
(k,p)=(1,6)
to
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(4)}\varphi^{\text{Re}}
. Similarly to before,

\displaystyle\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{1,6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(5)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1,6}\left(\|(\widetilde{A}-A_{0})\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|\gamma(d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1,3}\Big(\|(\widetilde{A}-A_{0})\|_{C^{0}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|a\|_{L^{12}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\|\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{12}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}

\displaystyle+\|d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{C^{0}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\varepsilon^{-2/3}\varepsilon^{-1/10}+(\varepsilon^{-1/10}\varepsilon^{-1/3})^{2}+\varepsilon^{-2/3}\varepsilon^{-1/10}\right)\leq C\varepsilon^{-2/3-2/10}

as both
(A-A_{0})
and
|d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}|
are bounded by constant multiples
\tfrac{1}{r}
and
r\geq c\varepsilon^{2/3}
, so
\|\tfrac{1}{r}\|_{C^{0}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(1)})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2/3}
. In addition, we have used (
A.12
) to bound the
L^{12}
norm. Since
\varepsilon^{-2/3-2/10}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}
, the first bound asserted in (
A.7
) follows.

For the second bound, we first apply the elliptic estimate to
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(5)}\varphi^{\text{Re}}
for
(k,p)=(1,3+\delta)
for
\delta<<1
on
K_{\varepsilon}^{(5)}
. This shows

\displaystyle\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{1,3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1,3+\delta}\Big(\|(\widetilde{A}-A_{0})\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})}+\|\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})}

(A.14)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\ \ \ \|\gamma(d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big(\|\tfrac{1}{r}\|_{L^{s}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}

(A.15)

\displaystyle+\|a\|_{L^{s}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\|\varphi^{\text{Im}}\|_{L^{6}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}+\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{2}}\Big).

(A.16)

Here, on the first and third terms we have applied Hölder’s inequality with exponents
p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
defiend by
6=(3+\delta)p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
the Hölder conjugate so that
\tfrac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1-\tfrac{3+\delta}{6}
. Because
\delta<<1
it follows that
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
are both very close to
2
. Thus we have
s=(3+\delta)q^{\prime}=6+\delta^{\prime}
for
\delta^{\prime}<<1
. We use that
\|\tfrac{1}{r}\|_{L^{s}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(4)})}\leq\varepsilon^{-4/9+\delta^{\prime\prime}}
and the
L^{6}
bound from Claim
A.1.1
to bound the first and third product. For the second term
\gamma(a)\varphi^{\text{Im}}
, the
L^{s}
-norm of
a
for
s=6+\delta^{\prime}
is easily bounded by the
L^{12}
norm, hence using (
A.12
) again for these terms leads to the following:

\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{1,3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})}\leq C(\varepsilon^{-4/9+\delta^{\prime\prime}}\varepsilon^{(-1/10)}+\varepsilon^{-1/3-1/10}\varepsilon^{(-1/10)}\leq C\varepsilon^{-2/3}.

Applying the Sobolev Embedding
C^{0,\beta}\hookrightarrow L^{1,3+\delta}
on
Y
for some
\beta<<1
shows that

\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{C^{0}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(7)})}\leq\|\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{1,3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})}+\|(d\chi_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})\varphi^{\text{Re}}\|_{L^{3+\delta}(K_{\varepsilon}^{(6)})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-2/3}

since final term was already bounded by
C\varepsilon^{-2/3}
in (
A.15
) above (the different cut-off function is immaterial for this estimate). This concludes the claim.
∎

Appendix B
Appendix II: Kernel Asymptotics

This appendix contains the proof of Lemma
6.28
that was deferred in Section 6.6. It is re-stated here for convenience. Recall that
\beta_{t}
denoted the
\widehat{H}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
-normalized element whose complex span was
\ker(\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{t})
.

Lemma B.1
.

The elements
\beta_{t}
have non-vanishing leading order term so that

\beta_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{-1/2}

for
\rho_{t}>\!>1
. As a consequence, we have the following bounds where the constants
C,c,\kappa_{1}
are independent of
\varepsilon,t

1.

c\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}\leq\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}

2.

If
\rho_{t}>\!>1
is sufficiently large,
|\dot{\beta}_{t}|\leq\kappa_{1}|\beta_{t}|
holds pointwise.

3.

{\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}}\leq\kappa_{1}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\lambda})}}
and
{\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{\lambda})}}\leq\kappa_{1}{\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\partial D_{\lambda})}}

Proof.

We omit the subscript
t
on
\rho_{t}
from the notation. Recall that up to an
\varepsilon
-independent normalization constant
\beta_{t}
is a linear combination of

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}(\rho)\alpha_{1}^{H}\\
-\overline{h}_{1}(\rho)\beta^{H}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}(\rho)\alpha_{2}^{H}\\
-\overline{h}_{1}(\rho)\beta^{H}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

\displaystyle\beta_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0\\
e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j+\begin{pmatrix}h_{j}(\rho)\alpha_{1}^{H}\\
-\overline{h}_{j}(\rho)\beta^{H}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\otimes 1+\begin{pmatrix}h_{j}(\rho)\alpha_{2}^{H}\\
-\overline{h}_{j}(\rho)\beta^{H}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\otimes j

where
h_{j}(\rho)=h_{j}^{t}(\rho)
is the
t
-parameterized family of solutions (subject to the boundary condition
6.30
) to

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h_{1}=\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\beta^{\circ}_{1},\Phi^{H})

where
\beta_{1}^{\circ}=(0,e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2})\otimes 1
is the first term above and likewise with
\otimes j
. The kernel elements are the ones satisfying
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=0
.

We claim now that for
\rho>>0
,
h^{t}_{1},h^{t}_{j}
and their
t
-derivatives take the form take the form

h^{t}_{1}=p_{1}(t)\frac{1}{\rho e^{i\theta}}+\frac{1}{\rho^{3/2}}g_{1}\hskip 42.67912pt\dot{h}^{t}_{1}=q_{1}(t)\frac{1}{\rho e^{i\theta}}+\frac{1}{\rho^{3/2}}g_{2}

(B.1)

for
\gamma>0
and and where
p_{1}(t),q_{1}(t)
are functions depending only on
\Phi_{0}
, and
\|g_{1}\|_{C^{0}}
,
\|g_{2}\|_{C^{0}}
are bounded independent of
\varepsilon
. The same holds for
j
.

Given this claim, the first statement of the lemma that
\beta_{t}
has leading order
\rho^{-1/2}
follows. To see this, write
\beta_{t}=w_{1}(t)\beta_{1}(t)+w_{j}(t)\beta_{j}(t)
for the element whose complex span is the subspace defined by the condition
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=0
. Since the components of
\Phi^{H}
are given by
\alpha_{1}^{H}=e^{H}c(t)\rho^{1/2}
and
\alpha_{2}^{H}=-e^{H}\overline{d}(t)\rho^{1/2}
we find the top two components of
\beta_{t}
are

(w_{1}p_{1}+w_{j}p_{j})c(t)\rho^{-1/2}+O(\rho^{-1})\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt-(w_{1}p_{1}+w_{j}p_{j}))\overline{d}(t)\rho^{-1/2}+O(\rho^{-1})

for the
\otimes 1
and
\otimes j
parts respectively. Since for each
t
the condition
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
holds, the
\rho^{-1/2}
order term vanishing would imply that
(w_{1}(t)p_{1}(t)+w_{j}(t)p_{j}(t))=0
for all
t
. But if this were the case, then the bottom two components are
w_{1}\beta_{1}^{\circ}
and
w_{2}\beta_{2}^{\circ}
respectively, which clearly has non-vanishing leading order, since we cannot have both
w_{1}=w_{2}=0
else
\beta_{t}=0
.

We now prove the claim that equation
B.1
holds. The same exact argument applies for
h_{1},h_{j}
, so we prove it for
h_{1}
. Recall that by definition
h_{1}
is the solution (subject to the boundary conditions
6.30
) of

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})h_{1}=\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\beta_{1}^{\circ},\Phi^{H})

(B.2)

where the right-hand side is given by

\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\beta^{\circ}_{1},\Phi^{H})=(e^{-H}\rho^{-1/2})\cdot\beta^{H}=e^{-2H}d(t)e^{-i\theta}

which is bounded by a constant independent of
r,\varepsilon
. We are going to construct the leading order term of
h_{1}
by hand.
To this end, take
\chi_{\varepsilon}
to be a cutoff equal to 0 outside a region of radius
\rho=\rho_{0}\varepsilon^{-1/6}
for some
\rho_{0}
independent of
\varepsilon
, and set

\widetilde{h}_{1}=-\frac{d(t)e^{-i\theta}}{2K(t)\rho}e^{-2H\chi_{\varepsilon}}.

(B.3)

It is
O(\rho^{-1})
. Ideally, we would want to take the definition
\widetilde{h}_{1}=-\mu(\beta_{1}^{\circ},\Phi^{H})/|\Phi^{H}|^{2}
, so that it would solve
(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})\widetilde{h}_{1}=-\mu(\beta_{1}^{\circ},\Phi^{H})+\Delta\widetilde{h}_{1}
, but this may not satisfy the required boundary conditions, which is the reason for the cut-off. Notice however, that
|\Phi^{H}|^{2}\sim 2K(t)\rho
up to an exponentially small error, and
H
is exponentially small, so our definition of
\widetilde{h}_{1}
is exponentially close to the desired one. Moreover, when
\widetilde{h}_{1}
is defined by
(\ref{h1tildedef})
, it satisfies that boundary conditions (
6.30
) because
\widetilde{h}_{1}
has only negative modes on the boundary, and

\overline{\partial}\widetilde{h}_{1}=0

once
\chi_{\varepsilon}=0
since
1/(\rho e^{i\theta})
is holomorphic.

Then one has

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})\widetilde{h}_{1}=\mu_{\mathbb{R}}(\beta_{1}^{\circ},\Phi^{H})+E

where
E=\Delta\widetilde{h}_{1}+O(e^{-\rho})=O(\rho^{-3})
. The exponentially small term arises from the difference
|\Phi^{H}|^{2}-2K(t)\rho
and the difference
e^{-2H}-e^{-2H\chi_{\varepsilon}}
. The true solution to (
B.2
) is therefore given by
h_{1}=\widetilde{h}_{1}+f_{1}
where
f_{1}
is the unique solution subject to the boundary condition (
6.30
) of

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})f_{1}=E.

(B.4)

and once
\rho>>0
then
\widetilde{h}_{1}
constitutes the leading order term asserted in the claim.

It remains to show that the remainder term coming from
f_{1}
has a
C^{0}
bound as desired. Consider the function
\chi\rho^{3/2}f_{1}
where
\chi
is a radially symmetric cutoff vanishing at the origin and equal to
1
outside the ball of radius
\rho=1
. Indeed, by Lemma
6.20
one has
\|f_{1}\|_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}\leq\|E\|_{L^{2}}\leq C
, where applying the above operator to this, we have

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})\chi\rho^{3/2}f_{1}=\chi\rho^{3/2}E-f_{1}\Delta(\chi\rho^{3/2})-\nabla(\chi\rho^{3/2-\gamma})\cdot\nabla f_{1}.

(B.5)

Next, since
E=O({\rho^{-3}})
then the first term lies in
L^{2}
. Since
\Delta(\chi\rho^{3/2})=O(\rho^{-1/2})
and
\nabla(\chi\rho^{3/2})=O(\rho^{1/2})
, the second and third terms have
L^{2}
-norm bounded by
\|f_{1}\|_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}\leq C
. Thus the right hand side has
L^{2}
-norm bounded by a constant independent of
\varepsilon
. It follows from the invertibility of the operator
-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2}
from Lemma
6.20
(notice the multiplication by
\rho
does not affect the boundary conditions) that

\|\chi\rho^{3/2}f_{1}\|_{T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}}\leq C

(B.6)

where
C
is still independent of
\varepsilon
. This implies in turn that

\|\chi\rho^{3/2}f_{1}\|_{C^{0}(D_{\varepsilon})}\leq C.

(B.7)

This follows from the Sobolev embedding. Indeed, notice that on the unit disk
D_{1}
, the Sobolev embedding
H^{1+\gamma}\hookrightarrow C^{0}
and the Gagliardo-Nirenberg Interpolation inequality (see equation (1.4) in
[
1
]
for the non-integer version of this inequality, and apply this to
|u|
). yield

\|u\|_{C^{0}(\overline{D_{1}})}\leq C\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}(D_{1})}\right)^{\tfrac{1-\gamma}{2}}\left(\|u\|_{L^{2,2}(D_{1})}\right)^{\tfrac{\gamma}{2}}

for any
\gamma>0
. Scaling functions on the disk of size
\varepsilon^{-1/6}
to the unit disk, the first norm
\|u\|_{L^{2}}
scales like
\varepsilon^{1/6}
, while the
L^{2,2}
norm scales like
\varepsilon^{-1/6}
, while the left-hand-side is independent of scaling. Thus for
\gamma<1/2
one has

\|u\|_{C^{0}(\overline{D_{\varepsilon}})}\leq C\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}\right)^{\tfrac{1-\gamma}{2}}\left(\|u\|_{L^{2,2}(D_{\varepsilon})}\right)^{\tfrac{\gamma}{2}}

and both these norms are bounded by the
T\mathcal{G}^{\mathbb{C}}
-norm on
D_{\varepsilon}
. Once
B.7
is established, we simply take
g=\rho^{3/2}f_{1}
on the region where
\rho
is large enough that
\chi=1
.

Now the same argument may be repeated for
\dot{f}_{t}
. The difference is that we must add the term
\partial_{t}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}f_{t}
to the right hand side of
B.4
and observe that
\|\rho^{3/2}\partial_{t}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}f_{t}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C
by
B.6
. Using this, and noting the asymptotics of
E
are unaffected by differentiating with respect to
t
, it is not hard to show in analogy with
B.5
that

(-\Delta-|\Phi^{H}|^{2})\chi\rho^{3/2}\dot{f}_{1}=\chi\rho^{3/2}\dot{E}+\chi\rho^{3/2}\partial_{t}|\Phi^{H}|^{2}f_{1}-\dot{f}_{1}\Delta(\chi\rho^{3/2})-\nabla(\chi\rho^{3/2-\gamma})\cdot\nabla\dot{f}_{1}

has a right-hand side in
L^{2}
. It follows that
\dot{f}_{t}=\rho^{-3/2}g_{2}
where
\|g_{2}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C
as well. This concludes the bound on the smaller term asserted in (
B.1
). For the first term of (
B.1
) we note

\frac{d}{dt}p_{1}(t)\frac{1}{\rho e^{-i\theta}}=\dot{p}_{1}(t)\frac{1}{\rho e^{i\theta}}+p_{1}(t)\frac{1}{\rho^{2}e^{i\theta}}\dot{\rho}=q_{1}(t)\frac{1}{\rho e^{i\theta}}

since
\dot{\rho}=\tfrac{2\dot{K}}{3K}\rho
. This completes the claim and thus the statements on the asymptotics of
\beta_{t}
.

The bounds asserted in the lemma now follow readily. Let
\Lambda
be a radius after which the
\rho^{-3/2}
term is negligible, then

\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}\geq c\left(\int_{\rho\geq\Lambda}|\rho^{-1/2}|^{2}rdr\right)^{1/2}\geq c\varepsilon^{2/3}\left(\int_{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon^{-1/3}}d\rho\right)^{1/2}\geq c\varepsilon^{1/2}

while the fact that
\beta_{t}
is bounded over the origin yields
|\beta_{t}|\leq C\rho^{-1/2}
and reversing the above inequalities show the upper bound.

The leading order term in each component consists of a product
p_{i}(t)\rho_{t}^{-1/2}
where
p_{i}(t)
depends only on
\Phi_{0}
and the derivatives of its leading coefficients. Since
\frac{d\rho_{t}}{dt}=\rho\tfrac{K^{\prime}(t)}{K(t)}
one has

\frac{d}{dt}p_{i}(t)\rho_{t}^{-1/2}=p_{i}^{\prime}(t)\rho_{t}^{-1/2}-p_{i}(t)\tfrac{K^{\prime}(t)}{2K}\rho_{t}^{-1/2}

so the leading term of the derivatives is bounded above by a constant times
\rho_{t}^{-1/2}
as well. And for
\rho>>0
the bound
\|g_{2}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C
in
B.1
shows that the sub-leading order are negligible for the derivative as well. Thus there is a pointwise bound

|\dot{\beta}_{t}|\leq C\rho_{t}^{-1/2}.

since we know
\dot{\beta}_{t}
is smooth across the origin. This implies

\|\dot{\beta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{1/2+1/12}\lesssim\|\beta_{t}\|_{L^{2}},

and a pointwise bound
|\dot{\beta}_{t}|\leq C|\beta_{t}|
once
\rho_{t}>>0
. The bound on the ratio of the integrals over
\partial D_{\varepsilon}
follows.

∎
